<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We prove the endpoint gradient bound $`\|\nabla Mf\|_1\le C\|\nabla f\|_1`$ for every real-valued $`f\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)`$, where $`Mf(x)`$ is the supremum of the averages of $`|f|`$ over disks centered at <i>x</i>, and <i>C</i> is an absolute constant. The maximal function belongs to $`W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)`$ and has a globally integrable weak gradient. This gives a positive resolution of the planar centered-disk case of the endpoint question of Hajłasz and Onninen.

- **C1** (theorem, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There is an absolute constant $C<\infty$ such that, for every real-valued $f\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, the function $Mf$ is locally integrable, belongs to $W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$, and satisfies $$\label{eq:main} \int_{\mathbb R^2}\|\nabla Mf\|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x \le C\int_{\mathbb R^2}\|\nabla f\|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$ In particular, its distributional derivatives are represented by globally integrable functions.
  - Explanation: Bounds the globally integrable weak gradient of the centered disk maximal function for real Sobolev input in the plane.
- **C2** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-finite-band)): There is an absolute constant $C$ such that, for every real $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ and $0<a\le b$ with $b/a$ a power of two, including one, $$\label{eq:intro-band} \int_{\mathbb R^2}\|\nabla S_{a,b}g\|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x \le C\int_{\mathbb R^2}\|\nabla g\|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$ The constant is independent of $a$, $b$, and the number of dyadic radius scales between them.
  - Explanation: Bounds signed finite-band gradients uniformly over the dyadic radius scales.
- **C3** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-local)): Let $Q$ be a square of side length one and let $\Omega=\{y:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(y,Q)<10\}$. Suppose that, on $\Omega$, $$\label{eq:local-decomposition} h=\sum_i v_i\sigma_i+\beta, \qquad \beta=\sum_d\beta_d.$$ The sums are finite. The measures in the sums are finite, defined on $\mathbb R^2$, and absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. Each $\sigma_i$ is nonnegative and each $v_i$ is a constant unit vector. For each $d$, write $m_d=\|\beta_d\|(\mathbb R^2)$ and assign a number $s_d\in(0,1]$. If $s_d<1$, assume that $\beta_d(\mathbb R^2)=0$ and that $\beta_d$ is supported in a ball of radius at most $C_s s_d$, where $C_s$ is fixed. Set $$S=\sum_i\sigma_i, \qquad A=\sum_d s_d^\alpha m_d.$$ For $x\in Q$, define $$\begin{aligned} \mathcal I(x) &=\{r\in[1,2]:u_r(x)\ge u_t(x) \text{ for every }t>0\text{ with }\|t-r\|\le1/4\},\\ \mathcal J(x) &=\{r\in[1,2]:u_r(x)=\max_{1\le t\le2}u_t(x)\}\cup\{1,2\}. \end{aligned}$$ Then the following estimates hold.* 1. **Unfavorable directional gradients.* If $e$ is a unit vector and $e\cdot v_i\ge\kappa>0$ for all $i$, then $$\label{eq:local-direction} \int_Q\sup_{r\in\mathcal I(x)} (-e\cdot\nabla u_r(x))_+\,dx\le C A.$$* 2. **Band maxima and forced endpoints.* Without a condition on the directions $v_i$, $$\label{eq:local-size} \int_Q\sup_{r\in\mathcal J(x)}\|\nabla u_r(x)\|\,dx \le C\bigl(A+S(\mathbb R^2)\bigr).$$* 3. **Differences of nearby averages.* We also have $$\label{eq:local-value} \int_Q\sup_{0<t,t'\le4}\|u_t(x)-u_{t'}(x)\|\,dx \le C\bigl(A+S(\mathbb R^2)\bigr).$$* *Here $C$ depends only on $C_s$, and in the first estimate also on $\kappa$. A supremum of nonnegative quantities over an empty set is zero.
  - Explanation: Controls unfavorable directional gradients, band endpoints and value oscillations through positive directions and discounted cancelling errors.
- **C4** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-local-balanced)): Suppose that $r\in[1,2]$ and $0<\delta\le1/4$, and that $$u_r(x)\ge u_{r-\delta}(x),\qquad u_r(x)\ge u_{r+\delta}(x).$$ There is a probability density $k$ supported on $[r-\delta,r+\delta]$, bounded by $C/\delta$, such that $$K(y)=\int k(t)\mathbf1_{B(x,t)}(y)\,dt$$ satisfies $$\label{eq:local-zero-moment} \int K(y)\overline{y-x}\,dh(y)=0.$$ Moreover, $0\le K\le1$, $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(K)\le C/\delta$, $K=1$ on $B(x,r-\delta)$, and $K=0$ outside $B(x,r+\delta)$. The density may be chosen measurably in $(x,r)$.
  - Explanation: Constructs probability mixtures of nearby disks preserving the zero complex moment.
- **C5** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-local-affine)): Let $x\in\mathbb R^2$, $r\in[1,2]$, $0<\delta\le1/4$, and $$D=\{y:\bigl\|\|y-x\|-r\bigr\|\le\delta\}.$$ Let $h$ be a complex measure whose variation is finite on $B(x,3)$. Suppose that $K_0,K_1:\mathbb R^2\to[0,1]$ are Borel functions supported in $B(x,3)$, that $K_1-K_0$ is supported in $D$, and that $$\int K_i(y)\overline{y-x}\,dh(y)=0\qquad(i=0,1).$$ Set $P_i=\int K_i\,dh$. For every $z\in D$, $$\label{eq:local-affine-identity} P_1-P_0=\int \left(1-\frac{\overline{y-x}}{\overline{z-x}}\right) (K_1-K_0)(y)\,dh(y).$$ For every $0<\rho\le1$, this implies $$\begin{aligned} \label{eq:local-affine-bound} \|P_1-P_0\|\le{}&2\rho\int_{D\cap B(z,\rho)} \|K_1-K_0\|\,d\|h\|\notag\\ &+10\int_{D\setminus B(z,\rho)}\|K_1-K_0\|\,d\|h\|. \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Uses zero moments to insert a factor vanishing at a shell point and suppress the nearby mass.
- **C6** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-shell-concentration)): Let $S$ and $\nu$ be finite positive Borel measures on $\mathbb R^2$, let $U>0$ and $T\ge0$, and suppose that $$0<\rho\le1, \qquad 0<\delta\le\frac{\rho^2}{1600}.$$ Write $$D(x,r,\delta)=\{y:\bigl\|\|y-x\|-r\bigr\|\le\delta\}.$$ There is a Borel set $E\subset\mathbb R^2$ such that $$\label{eq:shell-exception} \|E\|\le C\left[ \left(\frac{\nu(\mathbb R^2)}{U}\right)^2\frac{\delta}{\rho} +\frac{\nu(\mathbb R^2)}{U}\frac{\delta}{\rho^2} \left(1+\frac{T}{U}\right)\right],$$ and the following implication holds for every $x\notin E$ and every $r\in[1,2]$: if $$\label{eq:shell-hypotheses} \nu(D(x,r,\delta))\ge U, \qquad S(B(x,r-\delta))\le T,$$ then there is $z\in D(x,r,\delta)$ for which $$\label{eq:shell-cap} (S+\nu)\bigl(D(x,r,\delta)\setminus B(z,\rho)\bigr)\le2U.$$ The constant in <a href="#eq:shell-exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-exception">[eq:shell-exception]</a> is absolute. In particular, neither the exceptional set nor its bound involves a choice of $r$.
  - Explanation: Controls shell concentration outside a common exceptional set of centers under an inner positive-mass bound.
- **C7** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-prices-remove)): Let $U,V$ be finite nonnegative measures, of masses $P,N$. There are nonnegative submeasures $\mu_+\le U$, $\mu_-\le V$, both of mass $d=\min(P,N)$, such that $$U-V=\rho+\mu_+-\mu_-,\qquad \rho(\mathbb R^2)=P-N,\qquad \|\rho\|(\mathbb R^2)=\|P-N\|.$$ The residual $\rho$ has constant sign. If $U,V$ are absolutely continuous and supported in a common closed set, so are all the constructed measures.
  - Explanation: Removes equal positive and negative masses proportionally, leaving a constant-sign residual.
- **C8** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-prices-scalar-pairing)): For every $c\in\mathcal C$ there are measures $\rho_s^c,\mu_{s,+}^c,\mu_{s,-}^c$ at all vertices $s\in V_l$, $1\le l\le m$, supported in $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi_s$ and absolutely continuous, with $\rho_s^c$ of constant sign and $\mu_{s,\pm}^c$ nonnegative, such that $$\label{eq:prices-scalar-masses} \rho_s^c(\mathbb R^2)=c_s,\qquad \|\rho_s^c\|(\mathbb R^2)=\|c_s\|, \qquad \mu_{s,+}^c(\mathbb R^2)=\mu_{s,-}^c(\mathbb R^2)=d_s^c.$$ Put $\beta_s^c=\mu_{s,+}^c-\mu_{s,-}^c$. For every $q\in V_k$, $1\le k\le m$, the following is an identity of measures on the whole plane: $$\label{eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction} \phi_q\nu^c=\rho_q^c+ \sum_{l=k}^m\sum_{s\in V_l}\lambda_{qs}\beta_s^c.$$ Only finitely many terms in this identity are nonzero, and $$\label{eq:prices-pair-properties} \beta_s^c(\mathbb R^2)=0,\qquad \|\beta_s^c\|(\mathbb R^2)\le2d_s^c,\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\beta_s^c\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi_s \subseteq\overline B(s,\sqrt2 r_l).$$
  - Explanation: Reconstructs scalar gradient measures through residuals and cancelling pairs at every refinement level.
- **C9** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-prices)): For $p\in V_k$, $1\le k\le m$, define $$D_{Q_p}=\sum_{c\in\mathcal C} \sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q_p)} \sum_{l=k}^m2^{-\alpha(l-k)} \sum_{s\in V_l}\lambda_{qs}d_s^c, \qquad \alpha=\frac1{100}. \label{eq:prices-definition}$$ These nonnegative prices satisfy $$\sum_{k=1}^m\sum_{p\in V_k}D_{Q_p} \le\frac{41^2}{2(1-2^{-\alpha})} \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}\\|c(\nabla g)\\|_{L^1} \le C V. \label{eq:price-budget}$$ For every such cell $Q$ and every $n\in\mathcal N$, $$\begin{aligned} &\int_Q\sup_{t\in\mathcal I_k(x)} \sum_{\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}} (-e_\varepsilon(n)\cdot\nabla u_t(x))_+\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x \le C\left(D_Q+ \sum_{\substack{q\in\mathcal R(Q)\\(q,n)\text{ bad}}}a_q\right), \\ &\int_Q\left[\sup_{t\in\mathcal J_k(x)}\|\nabla u_t(x)\| +r_k^{-1}v_k(x)\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x \le C\left(D_Q+\sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q)}a_q\right). \end{aligned} \label{eq:prices}$$ All constants are absolute: the direction palette, support radius factor and cone aperture are fixed, and there is no dependence on $m$.
  - Explanation: Assigns common discounted prices with a total variation budget and local directional and value bounds.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-labels-neighbors)): There is an absolute constant $C$ such that $$\sum_{k=1}^m\sum_{q\in V_k}a_q E_k(q)\le C V. \label{eq:neighbor-budget}$$
  - Explanation: Bounds mass-weighted disagreement of neighboring directions across the refinement levels.
- **C11** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-good-label)): Set $c_*=(\tau/10)^2$. Suppose $q\in V_k$, $E_k(q)\le c_*$, and $\|x-q\|_{\infty}\le50r_k$. Then $(q,n_k)$ is good with $\mathbb P_x$-probability one.
  - Explanation: Ensures the label law assigns good directions near a vertex with controlled disagreement.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-label-packing)): For every real $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ and every $m\ge1$, the quantities constructed above satisfy $$\sum_{l=1}^m\sum_{q\in V_l}a_q \left[ \sup_{x\in D(q)}\bigl(R_l(x)+R_{l+1}(x)\bigr) +\sum_{k=1}^l2^{k-l} \operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{x\in D(q)}T_k(x) \right] \le C V. \label{eq:label-packing}$$ The constant is absolute and independent of $m$.
  - Explanation: Bounds switch and spatial row-derivative costs weighted by vertex masses.
- **C13** (construction, [source](#stmt-lem-labels-particles)): There is a finite positive measure $\mu$ on pairs consisting of a terminal path $(q_1,\ldots,q_m)$ and a birth level $B\in\{1,\ldots,m\}$ such that, for every node $\xi$ at level $k$, $$\mu\{B\le k,\ (q_1,\ldots,q_k)=\xi\}=M_\xi. \label{eq:labels-node-occupancy}$$ In particular, $$\mu\{B\le k,\ q_k=q\}=a_q, \qquad \mu(\text{all particles})=A_m\le V. \label{eq:labels-vertex-occupancy}$$ Writing $y=q_m$ for the terminal position of a particle, its complete path obeys $$\|y-q_s\|_{\infty}<r_s\qquad(1\le s\le m). \label{eq:labels-path-locality}$$ The path is defined also at levels preceding its birth.
  - Explanation: Realizes vector masses by a finite positive measure on complete refinement paths with birth levels.
- **C14** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-labels-switch-packing)): The particle measure satisfies $$\begin{aligned} \sum_{\xi\text{ intrinsically marked}}M_\xi&\le C V, \label{eq:labels-mark-budget}\\ \int\sum_{s=B}^m E_s(q_s)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu&\le C V, \label{eq:labels-particle-energy}\\ \int\sum_{s=B}^m R_s(y)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu&\le C V. \label{eq:labels-particle-switches} \end{aligned}$$
  - Explanation: Bounds intrinsic marks, direction disagreement and expected switches after particle birth.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-labels-spatial-comparison)): Fix a particle, a level $1\le l\le m$, and $x\in D(q_l)$. Set $$c_s=R_s(y)+E_s(q_s),\qquad 1\le s\le m.$$ For $1\le k\le\min(l+1,m)$, $$R_k(x)+T_k(x) \le C\sum_{s=1}^k2^{s-k}c_s. \label{eq:labels-spatial-comparison}$$ The assertion for $R_k$ holds at every $x$ in the specified set, and the assertion for $T_k$ holds almost everywhere there. The constant is uniform over particles, $l$, and $x$.
  - Explanation: Compares switch and row-derivative costs at nearby points with geometric convolutions along the complete particle path.
- **C16** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-sobolev-band)): For every real $g\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ and $0<a\le b<\infty$, $S_{a,b}g$ is globally Lipschitz and $$\label{eq:band-lipschitz} \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(S_{a,b}g)\le\frac{\\|\nabla g\\|_1}{\pi a^2}.$$ If $b/a$ is a nonnegative integer power of two, then $$\label{eq:sobolev-band} \int_{\mathbb R^2}\|\nabla S_{a,b}g\|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\le C\\|\nabla g\\|_1,$$ where $C$ is absolute and independent of the band. This includes $a=b$, when the envelope is one average.
  - Explanation: Gives global Lipschitz regularity for positive lower-radius bands and uniform gradient bounds for dyadic bands.
- **C17** (bound, [source](#stmt-cor-bv-input)): Let $BV(\mathbb R^2)$ denote the globally integrable functions whose distributional derivatives have finite total variation. For every real $f\in BV(\mathbb R^2)$, $$Mf\in L^2(\mathbb R^2)\cap BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2), \qquad \|D(Mf)\|(\mathbb R^2)\le C\|Df\|(\mathbb R^2),$$ with the constant from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Writing $P(F)=\|D\mathbf 1_F\|(\mathbb R^2)$ for perimeter, every measurable set $E$ with $\|E\|<\infty$ and $P(E)<\infty$ satisfies $$\int_0^1 P(\{M\mathbf 1_E>t\})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\le CP(E).$$ These superlevel sets have finite measure for every $t>0$ and finite perimeter for almost every $t>0$.
  - Explanation: Extends the variation bound to bounded-variation inputs and bounds integrated perimeters of maximal superlevel sets.
- **C18** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-appendix-sobolev-calculus)): For real $u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ there are real $u_j\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ converging to $u$ in $W^{1,1}$, and $$\label{eq:appendix-translation} \\|u(\cdot+z)-u\\|_1\le \|z\|\,\\|\nabla u\\|_1 \qquad(z\in\mathbb R^2).$$ Moreover $\|u\|\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ with $\|\nabla\|u\|\|\le\|\nabla u\|$ almost everywhere. If $\chi$ is smooth and compactly supported, then $\chi u\in W^{1,1}$ and $\nabla(\chi u)=\chi\nabla u+u\nabla\chi$. For locally Lipschitz real functions $v,w$, their maximum is locally Lipschitz and satisfies, almost everywhere, $$\label{eq:appendix-maximum} \nabla\max(v,w)= \begin{cases} \nabla v,&v>w,\\ \nabla w,&w>v,\\ \nabla v=\nabla w,&v=w. \end{cases}$$ Products of locally Lipschitz functions satisfy the weak product rule. Their weak derivatives agree almost everywhere with their classical derivatives.
  - Explanation: Records smooth approximation, translation bounds, absolute-value calculus and maximum and product rules.
- **C19** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-appendix-planar-embedding)): For every real $u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\\|u\\|_2^2\le\\|\partial_1u\\|_1\\|\partial_2u\\|_1, \qquad\text{in particular }\\|u\\|_2\le\\|\nabla u\\|_1.$$
  - Explanation: Embeds planar inhomogeneous Sobolev input into the square-integrable class.
- **C20** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-appendix-square)): For a square $Q$ of side length $t>0$ and $u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, write $u_Q=\|Q\|^{-1}\int_Qu$. Then $$\label{eq:appendix-poincare} \int_Q\|u-u_Q\|\le Ct\int_Q\|\nabla u\|.$$ For every grid square $Q$ of side $t$, there is a smooth cutoff $\chi_Q$ equal to one on the closed concentric dilation $3Q$, supported in the interior of $5Q$, with $0\le\chi_Q\le1$ and $\|\nabla\chi_Q\|\le C/t$. With $c_Q=u_{5Q}$, it satisfies $$\label{eq:appendix-localization} \\|\nabla[\chi_Q(u-c_Q)]\\|_1 \le C\int_{5Q}\|\nabla u\|.$$ Every disk of radius at most $t$ centered in $Q$ lies in $3Q$. The dilations $5Q$ of grid squares have bounded overlap. If the squares under consideration meet $O\subset\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<t\}$, all their dilations $5Q$ lie in $\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<6t\}$. Constants are independent of $Q,t,u,O,K$.
  - Explanation: Provides square Poincaré localization, cutoffs, disk containment and bounded overlap.
- **C21** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-appendix-vector-measure)): Suppose $u_j\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$ converge locally in $L^1$ to $u$, and $\sup_j\int_{\mathbb R^2}\|\nabla u_j\|\le M<\infty$. Then the distributional gradient of $u$ is a finite vector Radon measure $H$, with $$\label{eq:appendix-local-lsc} \|H\|(\mathbb R^2)\le M,\qquad \|H\|(O)=\mathcal V(u;O) \le\liminf_j\int_O\|\nabla u_j\| \quad\text{for every open }O.$$ If $\|H\|(K)=0$ for every compact Lebesgue-null set $K$, then $H=G\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x$ for some $G\in L^1(\mathbb R^2;\mathbb R^2)$ and $\\|G\\|_1=\|H\|(\mathbb R^2)$. In that case $u\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, with weak gradient $G$.
  - Explanation: Extracts a finite derivative measure by local lower semicontinuity and identifies an integrable weak gradient once null-set mass vanishes.

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-local-balanced",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose that $r\\in[1,2]$ and $0<\\delta\\le1/4$, and that $$u_r(x)\\ge u_{r-\\delta}(x),\\qquad u_r(x)\\ge u_{r+\\delta}(x).$$ There is a probability density $k$ supported on $[r-\\delta,r+\\delta]$, bounded by $C/\\delta$, such that $$K(y)=\\int k(t)\\mathbf1_{B(x,t)}(y)\\,dt$$ satisfies $$\\label{eq:local-zero-moment}\n\\int K(y)\\overline{y-x}\\,dh(y)=0.$$ Moreover, $0\\le K\\le1$, $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Lip}}(K)\\le C/\\delta$, $K=1$ on $B(x,r-\\delta)$, and $K=0$ outside $B(x,r+\\delta)$.         "exact_quote": "Let $x\\in\\mathbb R^2$, $r\\in[1,2]$, $0<\\delta\\le1/4$, and $$D=\\{y:\\bigl||y-x|-r\\bigr|\\le\\delta\\}.$$ Let $h$ be a complex measure whose variation is finite on $B(x,3)$. Suppose that $K_0,K_1:\\mathbb R^2\\to[0,1]$ are Borel functions supported in $B(x,3)$, that $K_1-K_0$ is supported in $D$, and that $$\\int K_i(y)\\overline{y-x}\\,dh(y)=0\\qquad(i=0,1).$$ Set $P_i=\\int K_i\\,dh$. For every $z\\in D$, $$\\label{eq:local-affine-identity}\n P_1-P_0=\\int\n \\left(1-\\frac{\\overline{y-x}}{\\overline{z-x}}\\right)\n (K_1-K_0)(y)\\,dh(y).$$ For every $0<\\rho\\le1$, this implies $$\\begin{aligned}\n\\label{eq:local-affine-bound}\n |P_1-P_0|\\le{}&2\\rho\\int_{D\\cap B(z,\\rho)}\n                    |K_1-K_0|\\,d|h|\\notag\\\\\n &+10\\int_{D\\setminus B(z,\\rho)}|K_1-K_0|\\,d|h|.\n\\end{aligned}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-085.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

        "exact_quote": "Let $S$ and $\\nu$ be finite positive Borel measures on $\\mathbb R^2$, let $U>0$ and $T\\ge0$, and suppose that $$0<\\rho\\le1,\n\\qquad 0<\\delta\\le\\frac{\\rho^2}{1600}.$$ Write $$D(x,r,\\delta)=\\{y:\\bigl||y-x|-r\\bigr|\\le\\delta\\}.$$ There is a Borel set $E\\subset\\mathbb R^2$ such that $$\\label{eq:shell-exception}\n|E|\\le C\\left[\n  \\left(\\frac{\\nu(\\mathbb R^2)}{U}\\right)^2\\frac{\\delta}{\\rho}\n  +\\frac{\\nu(\\mathbb R^2)}{U}\\frac{\\delta}{\\rho^2}\n           \\left(1+\\frac{T}{U}\\right)\\right],$$ and the following implication holds for every $x\\notin E$ and every $r\\in[1,2]$: if $$\\label{eq:shell-hypotheses}\n\\nu(D(x,r,\\delta))\\ge U,\n\\qquad S(B(x,r-\\delta))\\le T,$$ then there is $z\\in D(x,r,\\delta)$ for which $$\\label{eq:shell-cap}\n(S+\\nu)\\bigl(D(x,r,\\delta)\\setminus B(z,\\rho)\\bigr)\\le2U.$$ The constant in <a href=\"#eq:shell-exception\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:shell-exception\">[eq:shell-exception]</a> is absolute.       },

        "anchor": "stmt-lem-prices-scalar-pairing",
        "exact_quote": "For every $c\\in\\mathcal C$ there are measures $\\rho_s^c,\\mu_{s,+}^c,\\mu_{s,-}^c$ at all vertices $s\\in V_l$, $1\\le l\\le m$, supported in $\\mathop{\\mathrm{supp}}\\phi_s$ and absolutely continuous, with $\\rho_s^c$ of constant sign and $\\mu_{s,\\pm}^c$ nonnegative, such that $$\\label{eq:prices-scalar-masses}\n \\rho_s^c(\\mathbb R^2)=c_s,\\qquad |\\rho_s^c|(\\mathbb R^2)=|c_s|,\n \\qquad\n \\mu_{s,+}^c(\\mathbb R^2)=\\mu_{s,-}^c(\\mathbb R^2)=d_s^c.$$ Put $\\beta_s^c=\\mu_{s,+}^c-\\mu_{s,-}^c$. For every $q\\in V_k$, $1\\le k\\le m$, the following is an identity of measures on the whole plane: $$\\label{eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction}\n \\phi_q\\nu^c=\\rho_q^c+\n       \\sum_{l=k}^m\\sum_{s\\in V_l}\\lambda_{qs}\\beta_s^c.$$ Only finitely many terms in this identity are nonzero, and $$\\label{eq:prices-pair-properties}\n \\beta_s^c(\\mathbb R^2)=0,\\qquad\n |\\beta_s^c|(\\mathbb R^2)\\le2d_s^c,\\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{supp}}\\beta_s^c\\subseteq\\mathop{\\mathrm{supp}}\\phi_s\n       \\subseteq\\overline B(s,\\sqrt2 r_l).$$",

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-prices",
        "exact_quote": "For $p\\in V_k$, $1\\le k\\le m$, define $$D_{Q_p}=\\sum_{c\\in\\mathcal C}\n       \\sum_{q\\in\\mathcal R(Q_p)}\n       \\sum_{l=k}^m2^{-\\alpha(l-k)}\n       \\sum_{s\\in V_l}\\lambda_{qs}d_s^c,\n \\qquad \\alpha=\\frac1{100}.\n \\label{eq:prices-definition}$$ These nonnegative prices satisfy $$\\sum_{k=1}^m\\sum_{p\\in V_k}D_{Q_p}\n \\le\\frac{41^2}{2(1-2^{-\\alpha})}\n           \\sum_{c\\in\\mathcal C}\\|c(\\nabla g)\\|_{L^1}\n \\le C V.\n \\label{eq:price-budget}$$ For every such cell $Q$ and every $n\\in\\mathcal N$, $$\\begin{aligned}\n &\\int_Q\\sup_{t\\in\\mathcal I_k(x)}\n       \\sum_{\\varepsilon\\in\\{+,-\\}}\n       (-e_\\varepsilon(n)\\cdot\\nabla u_t(x))_+\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm{d}x\n   \\le C\\left(D_Q+\n       \\sum_{\\substack{q\\in\\mathcal R(Q)\\\\(q,n)\\text{ bad}}}a_q\\right),\n \\\\\n &\\int_Q\\left[\\sup_{t\\in\\mathcal J_k(x)}|\\nabla u_t(x)|\n                  +r_k^{-1}v_k(x)\\right]\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm{d}x\n   \\le C\\left(D_Q+\\sum_{q\\in\\mathcal R(Q)}a_q\\right).\n\\end{aligned}\n\\label{eq:prices}$$ All constants are absolute: the direction palette, support radius factor and cone aperture are fixed, and there is no dependence on $m$.",

      },

        "exact_quote": "For real $u\\in W^{1,1}(\\mathbb R^2)$ there are real $u_j\\in C_c^\\infty(\\mathbb R^2)$ converging to $u$ in $W^{1,1}$, and $$\\label{eq:appendix-translation}\n \\|u(\\cdot+z)-u\\|_1\\le |z|\\,\\|\\nabla u\\|_1\n \\qquad(z\\in\\mathbb R^2).$$ Moreover $|u|\\in W^{1,1}(\\mathbb R^2)$ with $|\\nabla|u||\\le|\\nabla u|$ almost everywhere. If $\\chi$ is smooth and compactly supported, then $\\chi u\\in W^{1,1}$ and $\\nabla(\\chi u)=\\chi\\nabla u+u\\nabla\\chi$. For locally Lipschitz real functions $v,w$, their maximum is locally Lipschitz and satisfies, almost everywhere, $$\\label{eq:appendix-maximum}\n \\nabla\\max(v,w)=\n \\begin{cases}\n  \\nabla v,&v>w,\\\\\n  \\nabla w,&w>v,\\\\\n  \\nabla v=\\nabla w,&v=w.\n \\end{cases}$$ Products of locally Lipschitz functions satisfy the weak product rule.         "anchor": "stmt-lem-appendix-square",
        "exact_quote": "For a square $Q$ of side length $t>0$ and $u\\in W^{1,1}(\\mathbb R^2)$, write $u_Q=|Q|^{-1}\\int_Qu$. Then $$\\label{eq:appendix-poincare}\n \\int_Q|u-u_Q|\\le Ct\\int_Q|\\nabla u|.$$ For every grid square $Q$ of side $t$, there is a smooth cutoff $\\chi_Q$ equal to one on the closed concentric dilation $3Q$, supported in the interior of $5Q$, with $0\\le\\chi_Q\\le1$ and $|\\nabla\\chi_Q|\\le C/t$. With $c_Q=u_{5Q}$, it satisfies $$\\label{eq:appendix-localization}\n \\|\\nabla[\\chi_Q(u-c_Q)]\\|_1\n       \\le C\\int_{5Q}|\\nabla u|.$$ Every disk of radius at most $t$ centered in $Q$ lies in $3Q$. The dilations $5Q$ of grid squares have bounded overlap. If the squares under consideration meet $O\\subset\\{x:\\mathop{\\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<t\\}$, all their dilations $5Q$ lie in $\\{x:\\mathop{\\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<6t\\}$. Constants are independent of $Q,t,u,O,K$.",

      },

          "source_id": "PM-085.1"
        }

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Centered disk maximal function** ([source](#sec:introduction)): For $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$, the centered disk maximal function is $$Mf(x)=\sup_{r>0}\frac1{\pi r^2}\int_{B(x,r)}\|f(y)\|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y, \qquad B(x,r)=\{y\in\mathbb R^2:\|y-x\|<r\}.$$
- **Signed disk averages and finite-band envelope** ([source](#pm-h-3)): For real $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$, put $$A_tg(x)=\frac1{\pi t^2}\int_{B(x,t)}g(y)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y, \qquad S_{a,b}g(x)=\sup_{a\le t\le b}A_tg(x).$$
- **Dyadic tents and cells** ([source](#pm-h-20)): For $p\in V_k$, define $$Q_p=p+[0,r_k)^2, \qquad \phi_p(x)=\prod_{j=1}^2 \left(1-\frac{\|x_j-p_j\|}{r_k}\right)_+.$$
- **Switch probability** ([source](#pm-h-26)): At a step $k\ge2$, condition on the old label $n=n_{k-1}$, sample $z\in V_k$ with probabilities $\phi_z(x)$, and switch to $n_z$ with probability $$p_k(n,z)=P(\|n-\Theta_z\|), \qquad P(d)=\min\{1,\tau^{-2}(d-\tau)_+^2\}. \label{eq:labels-switch-function}$$
- **Run envelope** ([source](#sec:assembly)): For $1\le i\le j\le m$, put $$\label{eq:run-envelope} F_{ij}(x)=\max_{r_j\le t\le2r_i}u_t(x).$$
- **Distributional variation** ([source](#pm-h-42)): For $u\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$ and open $O\subset\mathbb R^2$, define its distributional variation on $O$ by $$\label{eq:appendix-variation} \mathcal V(u;O)= \sup\left\{-\int u\,\operatorname{div}\varphi: \varphi\in C_c^1(O;\mathbb R^2),\ \|\varphi(x)\|\le1\right\}.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-lem-local) / [proof](#sec:local): Balance disk moments and apply shell concentration with discounted cancelling errors. -> Local directional, endpoint and value estimates
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-prop-prices) / [proof](#sec:prices): Remove equal scalar masses from finest tents upward and telescope the discounted charges. -> Common discounted prices
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-prop-label-packing) / [proof](#sec:labels): Realize vector masses by particles and compare finite-state label laws in scale and space. -> Switch and spatial derivative packing
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-prop-finite-band) / [proof](#sec:assembly): Assemble runs, subtract baselines and cancel differentiated probability rows before taking absolute values. -> Signed finite-band gradient bound
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#sec:extension): Approximate Sobolev inputs, extract the derivative measure and remove its singular part by signed square localization. -> Globally integrable weak gradient
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-cor-bv-input) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-bv-input): Mollify bounded-variation inputs and pass to the limit, then apply coarea to superlevel sets. -> Bounded-variation and integrated perimeter bounds

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I1**: Equal positive and negative masses can be removed while preserving the signed difference and leaving a constant-sign residual.
- **I2**: The centered disk maximal function has a globally integrable weak gradient controlled by the input gradient.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A1**: Infer global integrability of the maximal function from its gradient bound. -> The maximal function may fail to be globally integrable.
- **A2**: Treat finite cancellation tests as certification of the endpoint gradient theorem. -> The tests do not establish the analytic estimates or the limiting argument.
- **A3**: Transfer the gradient estimate to arbitrary averaging geometries. -> A different geometry is outside the stated result.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The proportional-removal construction has a finite-support rational restriction. It implements the cancellation invariant only; it does not compute the continuous maximal function or certify its endpoint gradient estimate.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON batch of pairs of equal-length lists of rational strings on stdin.
# PRECONDITIONS: nonnegative weights; common finite support; at most 256 cases and 16 sites per case.
# OUTPUTS: JSON batch of mu_plus, mu_minus and rho rational-weight lists.
# TIME: O(b*n) exact rational operations for b cases with n sites.
# SPACE: O(b*n) rational values, excluding integer bit lengths.
# ARITHMETIC: python-flint fmpq exact arbitrary-precision rationals.
# FAILURE: invalid or oversized input raises an exception and exits nonzero.
import sys, json
from flint import fmpq
def remove(left, right):
    if len(left) != len(right) or len(left) > 16:
        raise ValueError('support')
    u = [fmpq(t) for t in left]
    v = [fmpq(t) for t in right]
    if any(t < 0 for t in u + v):
        raise ValueError('negative weight')
    P = sum(u, fmpq(0))
    N = sum(v, fmpq(0))
    d = min(P, N)
    plus = [(d / P) * t if P else fmpq(0) for t in u]
    minus = [(d / N) * t if N else fmpq(0) for t in v]
    residual = [(a - b) - (c - e) for a, b, c, e in zip(u, plus, v, minus)]
    return {"mu_plus": list(map(str, plus)), "mu_minus": list(map(str, minus)), "rho": list(map(str, residual))}
batch = json.loads(sys.stdin.read())
if not isinstance(batch, list) or len(batch) > 256:
    raise ValueError('batch')
print(json.dumps([remove(*case) for case in batch]))
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: a software implementation contract. The theorem bounds the weak gradient of a continuous centered-disk maximal function; it supplies no runtime or memory guarantee. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: a simulation model, temporal evolution law, or discretization error bound. Centered disk averages alone do not certify a simulation. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: a deployable tool contract. The finite-band envelope is an analytic operator with a positive lower radius, rather than a specified computational interface. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | Conditional transfer: equal-mass removal gives a finite cancellation procedure when nonnegative measures have an exact finite representation and their masses can be computed exactly. The residual keeps constant sign and the original signed difference is reconstructed. |
| data_structures | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | Conditional transfer: a finite refinement forest can retain complete terminal paths together with birth levels. Occupancy includes a particle only after birth, allowing exact node-mass bookkeeping when capacities and refinement weights are represented exactly. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: a hardware execution or acceleration contract. Independence from the number of radius scales is an analytic bound, with no processor model or throughput statement. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: a security model or cryptographic construction. The finite probability law selects analytic directions and establishes no security property. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: a learning model, statistical guarantee, or training objective. A bound for a particular continuum maximal operator does not establish generalization or training stability. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: an optimization algorithm with an objective, feasible representation, and convergence guarantee. Maximization over disk radii defines an analytic envelope. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: a distributed computation contract. Refinement weights reconstruct vector measures and do not specify communication, consistency, or failure handling. |
| other (Harmonic analysis and continuum regularity) | True | strong | adaptable | inferred | Direct analytic application: real-valued inhomogeneous Sobolev input on the plane and the centered disk maximal operator yield local Sobolev regularity with a globally integrable weak gradient. Use as a continuum regularity contract only when the input class and averaging geometry agree. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L-geometry** (hypothesis): The estimate concerns centered disks in the plane. Transfer to other dimensions, uncentered neighborhoods or other shapes requires a separate result. Blocked use: ['Apply the endpoint theorem to a different averaging geometry.']
- **L-input** (hypothesis): The main theorem requires real-valued input in the inhomogeneous Sobolev space, with globally integrable input and weak first derivatives. Blocked use: ['Replace the input hypothesis by local integrability alone.']
- **L-output-integrability** (edge_case): The maximal function has local Sobolev regularity and globally integrable weak gradient; global integrability of the maximal function is not guaranteed. Blocked use: ['Claim global integrability of the maximal function from its gradient bound.']
- **L-optimal-constant** (unknown): The optimal absolute constant is not determined. Blocked use: ['Report a sharp numerical constant for the endpoint estimate.']
- **L-gradient-continuity** (unknown): Boundedness of the gradient map does not establish its continuity. Blocked use: ['Claim continuity of the planar gradient map from the norm estimate.']
- **L-signed-localization** (hypothesis): The localization argument must permit signed inputs after subtracting a local mean. Blocked use: ['Replace the signed finite-band estimate by a result that only permits nonnegative inputs.']
- **L-band-endpoints** (hypothesis): The stated uniform finite-band estimate assumes a positive lower radius and a dyadic ratio of the band endpoints, including equal endpoints. Blocked use: ['Assert the stated proposition for arbitrary endpoint ratios without a further argument.']
- **L-variation-singularity** (approximation): A total-variation bound alone does not exclude a singular derivative part; the absolute-continuity argument is a separate requirement. Blocked use: ['Treat a finite derivative measure as an integrable weak gradient without excluding its singular part.']
- **L-bv-output** (formal_scope): For arbitrary bounded-variation input, the corollary gives local bounded variation and a global derivative-measure bound, without asserting absolute continuity of that measure. Blocked use: ['Extend the Sobolev weak-gradient conclusion to arbitrary bounded-variation input.']
- **L-local-components** (hypothesis): The local estimates require absolutely continuous component measures; the shell-concentration lemma itself permits atoms. Blocked use: ['Use the sharp-kernel limit argument for atomic component measures without additional justification.']
- **L-radius-selection** (formal_scope): The touching argument holds an attaining radius fixed and does not differentiate a selected optimizing radius. Blocked use: ['Differentiate an optimizing radius as if its smooth dependence had been proved.']
- **L-finite-code** (formal_scope): Proportional removal becomes an executable finite procedure only after specifying an exact finite representation of the input measures. This restricted procedure does not certify the continuous endpoint theorem. Blocked use: ['Treat a finite measure implementation as a certificate for all Sobolev inputs.']
- **L-runtime** (unknown): ABSENT: runtime, memory and discretization-error guarantees for implementing the continuous maximal operator. Blocked use: ['Translate the analytic gradient bound into a computational complexity bound.']

<a id="pm-formal"></a>
### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 085 (`lean/docs/085.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [An Endpoint Gradient Bound for the Centered Disk Maximal Operator](../../preprints/An-Endpoint-Gradient-Bound-for-the-Centered-Disk-Maximal-Operator-September-26-2026/article.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The centered disk maximal operator takes the supremum of averages of $|f|$ over disks centered at each point. The formalization proves that every real $f\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ has a maximal function that is finite almost everywhere, belongs to $W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, and has a globally integrable weak gradient satisfying $\|\nabla Mf\|_1\le C\|\nabla f\|_1$ for one absolute constant $C$.
>
> The earlier signed finite-band estimate for smooth compactly supported functions is also retained. The general endpoint statement covers the Sobolev setting of the paper; a BV extension is outside these statements.

| declaration | kind |

| `Plane` | abbrev |
| `absoluteDiskAverage` | def |
| `maximal` | def |
| `maximalReal` | def |
| `testPartial` | def |
| `HasWeakGradient` | def |
| `InW11Loc` | def |
| `gradientNorm` | def |
| `EndpointStatement` | def |
| `main_endpoint` | theorem |

Comparator source `DiskMaximal.lean` (2431 bytes, sha256 `b86a9fd647b4b8dcff4e4fb95e7d4bba78d0b758c8b0beeb385d8e9f895c8017`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

open MeasureTheory Set
open scoped ENNReal NNReal Topology ContDiff

noncomputable section
namespace CenteredDiskEndpoint

/-- The Euclidean plane, not the product space with its max norm. -/
abbrev Plane := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)

/-- Absolute disk average, retaining possible infinite values. -/
def absoluteDiskAverage (f : Plane → ℝ) (x : Plane) (r : ℝ) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  (∫⁻ y in Metric.ball x r, ENNReal.ofReal |f y|) /
    ENNReal.ofReal (Real.pi * r ^ 2)

/-- The actual centered disk maximal function, over every positive real radius. -/
def maximal (f : Plane → ℝ) (x : Plane) : ℝ≥0∞ :=
  ⨆ (r : ℝ) (_ : 0 < r), absoluteDiskAverage f x r

/-- Real representative; the endpoint also asserts that `maximal f` is finite a.e. -/
def maximalReal (f : Plane → ℝ) (x : Plane) : ℝ := (maximal f x).toReal

/-- The i-th coordinate derivative of a smooth test function. -/
def testPartial (φ : Plane → ℝ) (i : Fin 2) (x : Plane) : ℝ :=
  fderiv ℝ φ x (EuclideanSpace.single i 1)

/-- Distributional first derivatives represented by a vector-valued function. -/
def HasWeakGradient (f : Plane → ℝ) (g : Plane → Plane) : Prop :=
  ∀ (φ : Plane → ℝ), ContDiff ℝ ∞ φ → HasCompactSupport φ →
    ∀ i : Fin 2, (∫ x, f x * testPartial φ i x) = -∫ x, g x i * φ x

/-- Local W^(1,1), expressed using the same distributional gradient convention. -/
def InW11Loc (f : Plane → ℝ) : Prop :=
  LocallyIntegrable f ∧
    ∃ g : Plane → Plane, LocallyIntegrable g ∧ HasWeakGradient f g

/-- The gradient L1 norm in Euclidean norm. -/
def gradientNorm (g : Plane → Plane) : ℝ := ∫ x, ‖g x‖

/-- Exact endpoint, including finiteness before passing to the real representative.
Quantifying the input gradient makes the norm bound independent of a choice of
representative of its weak derivatives. -/
def EndpointStatement : Prop :=
  ∃ C : ℝ, 0 ≤ C ∧
    ∀ (f : Plane → ℝ) (g : Plane → Plane),
      Integrable f → Integrable g → HasWeakGradient f g →
      (∀ᵐ x, maximal f x ≠ ⊤) ∧
      LocallyIntegrable (maximalReal f) ∧
      InW11Loc (maximalReal f) ∧
      ∃ G : Plane → Plane, Integrable G ∧
        HasWeakGradient (maximalReal f) G ∧ gradientNorm G ≤ C * gradientNorm g

theorem main_endpoint : EndpointStatement := by
  sorry

end CenteredDiskEndpoint

end

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We prove the endpoint gradient bound $`\|\nabla Mf\|_1\le C\|\nabla f\|_1`$ for every real-valued $`f\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)`$, where $`Mf(x)`$ is the supremum of the averages of $`|f|`$ over disks centered at <i>x</i>, and <i>C</i> is an absolute constant. The maximal function belongs to $`W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)`$ and has a globally integrable weak gradient. This gives a positive resolution of the planar centered-disk case of the endpoint question of Hajłasz and Onninen.

| [stmt-lem-appendix-planar-embedding](#stmt-lem-appendix-planar-embedding) | lemma | `lem:appendix-planar-embedding` | 20 | For every real $u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, |
| [stmt-lem-appendix-square](#stmt-lem-appendix-square) | lemma | `lem:appendix-square` | 21 | For a square $Q$ of side length $t>0$ and $u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, write $u_Q=&#124;Q&#124;^{-1}\int_Qu$. |
| [stmt-lem-appendix-vector-measure](#stmt-lem-appendix-vector-measure) | lemma | `lem:appendix-vector-measure` | 22 | Suppose $u_j\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$ converge locally in $L^1$ to $u$, and $\sup_j\int_{\mathbb R^2}&#124;\nabla u_j&#124;\le M<\infty$. |

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

For $f\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$, the centered disk maximal function is $$Mf(x)=\sup_{r>0}\frac1{\pi r^2}\int_{B(x,r)}|f(y)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,
 \qquad B(x,r)=\{y\in\mathbb R^2:|y-x|<r\}.$$ Every averaging disk is centered at the point of evaluation, and all positive radii are allowed. Kinnunen proved that this operator is bounded on $W^{1,p}$ for $1<p<\infty$ (Kinnunen 1997, Theorem 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Kinnunen1997](#ref-Kinnunen1997)]. The pointwise control of its gradient by $M(|\nabla f|)$ gives the corresponding norm bound through the strong $L^p$ maximal inequality. At $p=1$ that last step fails. Hajłasz and Onninen asked whether the gradient estimate itself nevertheless survives (Hajłasz and Onninen 2004, Question 1) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[HajlaszOnninen2004](#ref-HajlaszOnninen2004)]. We give a positive resolution of the planar centered-disk case of their endpoint question.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:main"></a>
**Theorem 1**. *There is an absolute constant $C<\infty$ such that, for every real-valued $f\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, the function $Mf$ is locally integrable, belongs to $W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$, and satisfies $$\label{eq:main}
 \int_{\mathbb R^2}|\nabla Mf|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
 \le C\int_{\mathbb R^2}|\nabla f|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$ In particular, its distributional derivatives are represented by globally integrable functions.*

</div>

Here $W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ is the inhomogeneous Sobolev space: the input and its weak first derivatives are globally integrable. Vector norms are Euclidean. No radiality or compact-support condition is imposed on the input. The maximal function itself need not be globally integrable, even for a nonzero nonnegative smooth function of compact support. The constant in <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a> is finite and absolute; its optimal value is not determined. The argument below concerns disks in the plane.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### The signed estimate behind the theorem

The intermediate estimate retains the sign of the input. For real $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$, put $$A_tg(x)=\frac1{\pi t^2}\int_{B(x,t)}g(y)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y,
 \qquad S_{a,b}g(x)=\sup_{a\le t\le b}A_tg(x).$$ For fixed $0<a\le b<\infty$, this envelope is compactly supported and Lipschitz. The issue is to bound its gradient uniformly as the range of radii grows.

<a id="stmt-prop-finite-band"></a>
<div id="prop:finite-band" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:intro-band"></a>
**Proposition 2** (Signed finite-band estimate). *There is an absolute constant $C$ such that, for every real $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ and $0<a\le b$ with $b/a$ a power of two, including one, $$\label{eq:intro-band}
 \int_{\mathbb R^2}|\nabla S_{a,b}g|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
 \le C\int_{\mathbb R^2}|\nabla g|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$ The constant is independent of $a$, $b$, and the number of dyadic radius scales between them.*

</div>

The proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-band">2</a> is given in Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">5</a>, after the local and multiscale estimates described below. A single radius, $a=b$, is covered directly by convolution. The signed form of Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-band">2</a> has a specific purpose in the final regularity argument. After extension to Sobolev inputs, one applies it to $g_Q=\chi_Q(g-c_Q)$, where $c_Q$ is a local mean and $\chi_Q$ equals one on the small disks being tested. On those disks, $A_tg_Q=A_tg-c_Q$, so the corresponding envelopes have the same gradient. Poincaré’s inequality then bounds $\|\nabla g_Q\|_1$ by the gradient of $g$ on a slightly larger square. The function $g_Q$ can take both signs. Signed averages also occur in Weigt’s dyadic and uncentered cube estimates (Weigt 2023, 2025) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Weigt2023Dyadic](#ref-Weigt2023Dyadic), [Weigt2025Cubes](#ref-Weigt2025Cubes)]; their role here is to permit this local subtraction.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Earlier endpoint results

In one dimension, Tanaka proved an $L^1$ derivative bound for the uncentered maximal operator (Tanaka 2002, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Tanaka2002](#ref-Tanaka2002)]. Aldaz and Pérez Lázaro extended the result to inputs of bounded variation, established absolute continuity of the maximal function, and obtained the sharp variation constant $1$ (Aldaz and Pérez Lázaro 2007, Theorem 2.5) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[AldazPerezLazaro2007](#ref-AldazPerezLazaro2007)]. Kurka proved a variation bound with an absolute constant for the centered operator on functions of bounded variation, together with local Sobolev regularity and an integrable derivative for Sobolev inputs (Kurka 2015, Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Kurka2015](#ref-Kurka2015)]. The ordering of intervals used in these arguments does not directly organize a family of centered disks.

In higher dimensions, Aldaz and Pérez Lázaro proved regularity for block-decreasing inputs, invariant under coordinate sign changes and nonincreasing in each positive coordinate, and uncentered maximal operators over balls of unconditional norms; their weak-gradient conclusion applies, in particular, to block-decreasing $W^{1,1}$ input and Euclidean balls (Aldaz and Pérez Lázaro 2009, Theorem 11) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[AldazPerezLazaro2009Block](#ref-AldazPerezLazaro2009Block)]. Luiro proved the $L^1$ gradient estimate for radial Sobolev input and the uncentered ball operator (Luiro 2018, Theorem 3.12) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Luiro2018Radial](#ref-Luiro2018Radial)]. Weigt established variation bounds for the dyadic maximal operator (Weigt 2023) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[Weigt2023Dyadic](#ref-Weigt2023Dyadic)] and for the uncentered maximal operator over axis-parallel cubes (Weigt 2025, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Weigt2025Cubes](#ref-Weigt2025Cubes)]. Their geometry and indexing differ from centered disks. Moreover, a variation estimate controls a derivative measure, which may still have a singular part. For fractional orders $0<\beta<1$, Beltran and Madrid established centered endpoint gradient bounds for radial $W^{1,1}$ input (Beltran and Madrid 2020, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BeltranMadrid2020CenteredFractional](#ref-BeltranMadrid2020CenteredFractional)]. Weigt subsequently proved endpoint gradient estimates for both the centered and uncentered operators (Weigt 2022, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Weigt2022Fractional](#ref-Weigt2022Fractional)]. The positive order restricts overlap among optimizing balls in that proof; the mechanism degenerates at order zero.

Lahti and Weigt’s conditional regularity theorem upgrades local bounded variation of the centered maximal function to local Sobolev regularity when the input is Sobolev (Lahti and Weigt 2025, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[LahtiWeigt2025](#ref-LahtiWeigt2025)]. It assumes the requisite variation bound. We prove that bound and then remove the singular part directly using the signed localization above. Finally, boundedness and continuity of the gradient map are distinct questions. The one-dimensional continuity results of Carneiro, Madrid and Pierce for the uncentered operator (Carneiro, Madrid, and Pierce 2017) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[CarneiroMadridPierce2017](#ref-CarneiroMadridPierce2017)] <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[GonzalezRiquelme2023Continuity](#ref-GonzalezRiquelme2023Continuity)], and of González-Riquelme for the centered operator (González-Riquelme 2023), concern this further issue. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> asserts the planar norm estimate.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### How the finite-band estimate is proved

Write <a id="eq:signed-average"></a>$$\label{eq:signed-average}
 u_t=A_tg,\qquad h=(\nabla g)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,\qquad
 V=|h|(\mathbb R^2)=\int|\nabla g|.$$ Scaling reduces Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-band">2</a> to radii in $[2^{-m},1]$. The proof has one geometric input and two separate multiscale constructions. The first construction organizes scalar cancellation; the second chooses directions and pays for their changes in both scale and space.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Disk moments and concentration in a shell.

At an interior maximizing radius, the radial derivative of $u_t$ vanishes. A rotational moment vanishes on every disk. In complex notation these identities give a zero first moment, which is preserved by suitable positive mixtures of nearby disks. Variations of optimizing balls also produce weighted-gradient identities in Luiro’s work (Luiro 2018, Lemmas 2.2 and 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Luiro2018Radial](#ref-Luiro2018Radial)]; we derive the identities needed for centered signed averages directly.

Identify $\mathbb R^2$ with $\mathbb C$, let $x$ be the disk center, and let $z$ be a point of its thin boundary shell. The purpose of the moment identity is visible in the affine factor $$W_z(y)=1-\frac{\overline{y-x}}{\overline{z-x}}.$$ This factor vanishes at $z$. Inserting it in a difference of balanced disk kernels therefore makes mass near $z$ inexpensive. This leads to the geometric question: when is almost all relevant shell mass concentrated near one point? A sweep by nested disks tangent at that point answers it outside one exceptional set of centers, uniformly over every radius in the band. Crucially, it requires a bound on positive mass only in an inner disk; the error mass is controlled separately.

These observations yield a local directional estimate. On a square of side $r$, decompose $h$ into positive measures with directions in one cone and localized errors. Errors smaller than the observation scale have zero total vector mass; errors at scale $r$ are charged their full variation. An error of variation $b$ supported at scale $s\le r$ is charged $b(s/r)^\alpha$, with a fixed $\alpha=1/100$. The resulting sum controls the integral over the square of the supremum, over locally maximizing radii, of the gradient component pointing against the cone. Companion estimates handle band endpoints and the value oscillation $$v_k(x)=\sup_{0<t,t'\le4r_k}|u_t(x)-u_{t'}(x)|,
 \qquad r_k=2^{-k}.$$ Those estimates include the endpoint arguments and the comparison of successively narrower kernels; shell concentration alone does not supply them.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Scalar cancellation and a common price for each cell.

Let $\phi_q$ be the product of the two one-dimensional tents at a vertex $q$ of the level-$k$ dyadic grid: each factor equals one at its vertex coordinate and decreases linearly to zero one mesh width away. Set $h_q=\int\phi_q\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}h$, $a_q=|h_q|$. The tents form positive partitions of unity and refine positively onto finer grids. We express vectors in each of finitely many bases formed by the two boundary directions of a cone. For each scalar coordinate, we start with the actual signed density at the finest level. Pair its positive and negative parts in equal masses, retain the constant-sign remainder, and repeat this operation on the weighted remainders at each parent. This bottom-up construction retains the support and birth level of every pair, including cancellation inside the finest tents. Its identity is an identity of measures, so there is no unrepresented terminal density.

The masses paired at successive levels form a telescoping sum. Charging each pair at coarser observation scales with the factor $(s/r)^\alpha$ gives a nonnegative price $D_Q$ for every dyadic cell, with <a id="eq:overview-price-budget"></a>$$\label{eq:overview-price-budget}
 \sum_Q D_Q\le CV.$$ One price works for every direction in the fixed finite palette. The local estimates then cost $D_Q$ together with appropriate nearby vector masses $a_q$ at the observation scale. Multiscale oscillation budgets already play a role in Kurka’s one-dimensional proof (Kurka 2015, Lemma 1.5) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Kurka2015](#ref-Kurka2015)]; the scalar measure decomposition and its common prices are proved here.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### Vector particles and the cost of choosing directions.

The remaining vector masses require a different construction. Vector refinement makes their total increase with the level, while keeping it at most $V$. We realize these masses by a finite positive measure on refinement paths, adding mass when needed and recording its birth level. This construction preserves the complete path, including levels before birth, so that it can compare choices of direction made from the first scale onward. These vector particles differ from the scalar cancelling pairs: their role is to turn estimates along paths into sums of vector-mass charges over cells.

Two costs must be controlled. A change of direction starts a new run of radius scales and creates endpoint terms. Spatial variation of the choice of direction creates derivatives of its weights when we integrate by parts. Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">4</a> constructs a finite-state law at each point $x$ and bounds sums of both costs, weighted by these vector masses, by $CV$, with the spatial suprema needed for the cell estimates. Vector-additivity defects control changes along particles, and comparison at nearby points transfers that control to the spatially varying law.

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
###### Assembly and cancellation of differentiated rows.

After the common prices and the direction costs have been bounded, we assemble the radius bands. A run is a maximal consecutive interval of scales carrying the same direction. For each history, apply the local directional estimate to the supremum over the radii of every run, then average over histories. The common prices and the bounds for direction changes pay for the unfavorable components and the endpoints of runs.

For each run, subtract the average at its starting scale from its envelope; the endpoint estimates pay for the gradients of these baselines. The vector quantity to be integrated by parts is the sum of the resulting envelopes multiplied by their run directions. Its history weight depends on $x$. If $\pi_k(x;n,n')$ is the probability of passing from direction $n$ to direction $n'$ at step $k$, normalization of each row gives $$\sum_{n'}\nabla_x\pi_k(x;n,n')=0.$$ Before taking absolute values, we can therefore subtract the analogous vector sum for the preceding history, ending its last run at level $k-1$. That sum is independent of the next direction. The difference contains only extensions of the last run and new runs, and is bounded by $C\sum_{l\ge k}v_l$. The local value estimate contributes the scale $r_l$, while a differentiated transition row has scale $1/r_k$. Their ratio $r_l/r_k$ is exactly the geometric weight controlled by the direction-cost estimate. Together with the common-price budget, these bounds give <a href="#eq:intro-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-band">[eq:intro-band]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### From the finite-band estimate to a weak gradient

Approximation extends Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-band">2</a> to signed Sobolev inputs. Applying it to $g=|f|$ and increasing the radius bands first gives a finite vector Radon measure representing the derivative of $Mf$. To prove that this measure has no singular part, fix a compact null set and a positive radius threshold. The envelope over larger radii is Lipschitz, so its contribution vanishes as the neighborhood of the null set shrinks. For small radii, the signed cutoff and local mean subtraction described above reduce the cost to the integral of $|\nabla g|$ within a fixed multiple of the threshold distance. After shrinking the neighborhood, let the radius threshold tend to zero. This gradient integral then vanishes as well. Inner regularity gives absolute continuity of the derivative measure and proves Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

Lower-radius Lipschitz truncations appear in Hajłasz and Malý (Hajłasz and Malý 2010, Lemma 8) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[HajlaszMaly2010](#ref-HajlaszMaly2010)], and local/nonlocal splitting with subtraction of an input mean appears in Saari (Saari 2019, sec. 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Saari2019](#ref-Saari2019)]. The signed estimate above supplies the gradient-only localization used here.

Section <a href="#sec:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:local">2</a> proves disk cancellation and the local estimates. Section <a href="#sec:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prices">3</a> gives scalar refinement and common prices; Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">4</a> constructs the vector particles and spatial label law. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">5</a> assembles the finite bands, and Section <a href="#sec:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:extension">6</a> passes to Sobolev input and removes the singular part. An analytic appendix records the Sobolev calculus, square localization, and vector-measure facts in the forms used in the final step.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Conventions

A constant denoted by $C$ may increase between occurrences. All parameters, including cone apertures, finite direction nets and geometric neighborhood sizes, are fixed absolute choices. In a local lemma the explicitly listed cone and support constants are allowed dependencies. No constant depends on the number of radius scales.

For a vector measure $\nu$, $|\nu|$ denotes its total variation. For a real number $a$, $a_+=\max(a,0)$. A supremum of nonnegative quantities over an empty set is zero. When $\nabla u_t$ is written, the radius is held fixed. We use the classical strong $L^p$ maximal inequality for $p>1$ (Kinnunen 1997, (1.2)) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Kinnunen1997](#ref-Kinnunen1997)]; a general account is given in (Stein 1970, vol. 30, chap. I) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Stein1970](#ref-Stein1970)]. The analytic appendix records the remaining standard inputs in the forms used here.

<a id="sec:local"></a>
### Cancellation at disks and three local bounds

Throughout this section, $g\in C^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ is real valued, $h=\nabla g\,dx$, and $u_t=A_tg$. The averages are signed. No compact-support assumption on $g$ is needed for the local estimates. Our goal is to control three quantities on a unit square: unfavorable directional gradients at locally maximizing radii, gradients at band maxima and endpoints, and differences of averages. The common input is a decomposition of the gradient into positive directions and error pieces, with small support and zero total mass below size one. The discount on the latter errors will make the scale sum in Section <a href="#sec:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prices">3</a> finite. We fix <a id="eq:local-alpha"></a>$$\label{eq:local-alpha}
\alpha=\frac1{100}.$$ An equality of measures on an open set means equality of their restrictions there. The component measures themselves will always remain defined on the whole plane.

<a id="stmt-lem-local"></a>
<div id="lem:local" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-decomposition"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Local estimates). *Let $Q$ be a square of side length one and let $\Omega=\{y:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(y,Q)<10\}$. Suppose that, on $\Omega$, $$\label{eq:local-decomposition}
h=\sum_i v_i\sigma_i+\beta,
\qquad \beta=\sum_d\beta_d.$$ The sums are finite. The measures in the sums are finite, defined on $\mathbb R^2$, and absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. Each $\sigma_i$ is nonnegative and each $v_i$ is a constant unit vector. For each $d$, write $m_d=|\beta_d|(\mathbb R^2)$ and assign a number $s_d\in(0,1]$. If $s_d<1$, assume that $\beta_d(\mathbb R^2)=0$ and that $\beta_d$ is supported in a ball of radius at most $C_s s_d$, where $C_s$ is fixed. Set $$S=\sum_i\sigma_i,
\qquad A=\sum_d s_d^\alpha m_d.$$ For $x\in Q$, define $$\begin{aligned}
\mathcal I(x)
&=\{r\in[1,2]:u_r(x)\ge u_t(x)
                 \text{ for every }t>0\text{ with }|t-r|\le1/4\},\\
\mathcal J(x)
&=\{r\in[1,2]:u_r(x)=\max_{1\le t\le2}u_t(x)\}\cup\{1,2\}.
\end{aligned}$$ Then the following estimates hold.*

<a id="eq:local-direction"></a>
1.  **Unfavorable directional gradients.* If $e$ is a unit vector and $e\cdot v_i\ge\kappa>0$ for all $i$, then $$\label{eq:local-direction}
    \int_Q\sup_{r\in\mathcal I(x)}
                      (-e\cdot\nabla u_r(x))_+\,dx\le C A.$$*

<a id="eq:local-size"></a>
2.  **Band maxima and forced endpoints.* Without a condition on the directions $v_i$, $$\label{eq:local-size}
    \int_Q\sup_{r\in\mathcal J(x)}|\nabla u_r(x)|\,dx
        \le C\bigl(A+S(\mathbb R^2)\bigr).$$*

<a id="eq:local-value"></a>
3.  **Differences of nearby averages.* We also have $$\label{eq:local-value}
    \int_Q\sup_{0<t,t'\le4}|u_t(x)-u_{t'}(x)|\,dx
        \le C\bigl(A+S(\mathbb R^2)\bigr).$$*

*Here $C$ depends only on $C_s$, and in the first estimate also on $\kappa$. A supremum of nonnegative quantities over an empty set is zero.*

</div>

All the quantities in this statement are measurable. To see this, extend the displayed definitions of $\mathcal I(x)$ and $\mathcal J(x)$ to all $x\in\mathbb R^2$. Their graphs are closed in $\mathbb R^2\times[1,2]$, by joint continuity of $u_t(x)$ and continuity of a maximum over a compact interval. For either of the first two suprema, its superlevel set at a positive closed threshold is the projection of a closed set with the radius variable in a compact interval, and is therefore closed. Restricting these Borel functions to $Q$ proves the claim also for half-open squares. The last supremum can be taken over positive rational $t,t'\le4$, by continuity in the radius variables.

The first estimate discounts cancellation without charging the positive mass $S$: its cone contribution is favorable at every tested radius. Comparisons of kernels will still require a bound on its weighted mass. The second estimate pays for $S$ and also covers the two forced radii, so it can be used when a radius interval ends. The third controls values rather than gradients; its factor under rescaling will pay for derivatives of spatial weights. Section <a href="#sec:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prices">3</a> gives those scaled applications.

We prove the first two bounds by comparing successively narrower disk kernels. Before estimating their changes, we explain why a maximizing radius supplies a useful moment identity and why concentration in a small cap makes that identity effective.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Disk moments and an affine cancellation

We seek cancellation in a thin shell where two nearby disks differ. Estimating by the entire gradient mass on that shell would ignore the maximizing property of the radius. At an interior maximizing radius, a radial derivative vanishes; a rotational moment vanishes for every disk. We combine these identities using complex notation, then preserve them while smoothing the disk boundary.

Variations of optimizing balls also give weighted-gradient cancellation identities in Luiro’s work on the uncentered maximal operator (Luiro 2018, Lemmas 2.2 and 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Luiro2018Radial](#ref-Luiro2018Radial)]. There the balls optimize averages of $|f|$. We derive the identities needed here directly for centered signed averages.

<a id="eq:local-moment-identities"></a>
<a id="eq:local-spatial-gradient"></a>
We identify the plane with the complex numbers, using the usual positive orientation. A vector measure is then a complex measure, and $\overline{y-x}\,dh(y)$ denotes complex multiplication. For every disk, $$\label{eq:local-moment-identities}
\operatorname{Im}\int_{B(x,t)}\overline{y-x}\,dh(y)=0,
\qquad
\partial_tu_t(x)=\frac1{\pi t^3}
 \operatorname{Re}\int_{B(x,t)}\overline{y-x}\,dh(y).$$ The first identity follows by integrating the divergence of $g(y)(y-x)^\perp$: the vector field $(y-x)^\perp$ is divergence free and tangent to the boundary circle. For the second, differentiate $u_t(x)=\pi^{-1}\int_{B(0,1)}g(x+tw)\,dw$. The same change of variables gives $$\label{eq:local-spatial-gradient}
\nabla u_t(x)=\frac{h(B(x,t))}{\pi t^2}.$$

<a id="stmt-lem-local-balanced"></a>
<div id="lem:local-balanced" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-zero-moment"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Balanced kernels). *Suppose that $r\in[1,2]$ and $0<\delta\le1/4$, and that $$u_r(x)\ge u_{r-\delta}(x),\qquad u_r(x)\ge u_{r+\delta}(x).$$ There is a probability density $k$ supported on $[r-\delta,r+\delta]$, bounded by $C/\delta$, such that $$K(y)=\int k(t)\mathbf1_{B(x,t)}(y)\,dt$$ satisfies $$\label{eq:local-zero-moment}
\int K(y)\overline{y-x}\,dh(y)=0.$$ Moreover, $0\le K\le1$, $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(K)\le C/\delta$, $K=1$ on $B(x,r-\delta)$, and $K=0$ outside $B(x,r+\delta)$. The density may be chosen measurably in $(x,r)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-balanced"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-balanced</summary>

*Proof.* Normalize $t^{-3}$ separately on $[r-\delta,r]$ and $[r,r+\delta]$ to obtain probability densities $k_-$ and $k_+$. Let $M_-$ and $M_+$ be the real moments in <a href="#eq:local-moment-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-moment-identities">[eq:local-moment-identities]</a> averaged with these densities. Integration of that identity shows $M_-\ge0$ and $M_+\le0$. If $M_--M_+>0$, take $$k=a k_-+(1-a)k_+,
\qquad a=\frac{-M_+}{M_--M_+};$$ if both moments are zero, take $k=k_-$. The imaginary moments are zero separately, so <a href="#eq:local-zero-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-zero-moment">[eq:local-zero-moment]</a> follows. The normalizing factors are comparable to $\delta$, because $3/4\le t\le9/4$. This proves the density bound and all the claims about $K$. The formula for $a$ is Borel, with the stated convention at zero, which gives the last assertion. ◻

</details>

The balanced kernels in Lemma <a href="#lem:local-balanced" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-balanced">4</a> smooth the disk boundary without losing its zero moment. Consider two such kernels, possibly with different widths. Their difference is supported in the larger shell. The following elementary observation explains what we want to know about the mass on that shell.

<a id="stmt-lem-local-affine"></a>
<div id="lem:local-affine" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:local-affine-identity"></a>
<a id="eq:local-affine-bound"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Affine cancellation). *Let $x\in\mathbb R^2$, $r\in[1,2]$, $0<\delta\le1/4$, and $$D=\{y:\bigl||y-x|-r\bigr|\le\delta\}.$$ Let $h$ be a complex measure whose variation is finite on $B(x,3)$. Suppose that $K_0,K_1:\mathbb R^2\to[0,1]$ are Borel functions supported in $B(x,3)$, that $K_1-K_0$ is supported in $D$, and that $$\int K_i(y)\overline{y-x}\,dh(y)=0\qquad(i=0,1).$$ Set $P_i=\int K_i\,dh$. For every $z\in D$, $$\label{eq:local-affine-identity}
 P_1-P_0=\int
 \left(1-\frac{\overline{y-x}}{\overline{z-x}}\right)
 (K_1-K_0)(y)\,dh(y).$$ For every $0<\rho\le1$, this implies $$\begin{aligned}
\label{eq:local-affine-bound}
 |P_1-P_0|\le{}&2\rho\int_{D\cap B(z,\rho)}
                    |K_1-K_0|\,d|h|\notag\\
 &+10\int_{D\setminus B(z,\rho)}|K_1-K_0|\,d|h|.
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-local-affine"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-local-affine</summary>

*Proof.* Since $|z-x|\ge r-\delta\ge3/4$, the denominator is nonzero. Subtract the difference of the two zero-moment identities, divided by $\overline{z-x}$, from $P_1-P_0$. This gives <a href="#eq:local-affine-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-affine-identity">[eq:local-affine-identity]</a>. Its multiplier is $$W_z(y)=\frac{\overline{z-y}}{\overline{z-x}}.$$ On $B(z,\rho)$ its absolute value is at most $2\rho$. On $D$, $|y-z|\le2(r+\delta)\le9/2$ and $|z-x|\ge3/4$, so $|W_z|\le6\le10$. Splitting the integral into the two regions proves <a href="#eq:local-affine-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-affine-bound">[eq:local-affine-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

Thus concentration near $z$ turns the factor $1$ in a variation estimate into the small factor $2\rho$. We will call $D\cap B(z,\rho)$ a cap of the shell. The geometric question is now concrete: when can all but a controlled amount of shell mass be placed in one cap?

For the intended use, two positive measures play different roles. One, denoted by $\nu$, detects whether the shell carries enough error mass to require this argument. The other, denoted by $S$, describes positive contributions to the gradient. The conclusion must control both measures on the shell, but the available hypothesis bounds only $S$ in the inner disk. A bound involving all of $S(\mathbb R^2)$ would lose that information. The tangent-disk sweep below is what allows the inner mass bound to suffice.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### One exceptional set for all radii

We use Lebesgue measure for the size $|E|$ of a set of centers. The measures $S$ and $\nu$ in the next lemma are arbitrary finite positive Borel measures; they may have atoms. No maximizing radius is selected, and no regularity of these measures is assumed.

<a id="stmt-lem-shell-concentration"></a>
<div id="lem:shell-concentration" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:shell-exception"></a>
<a id="eq:shell-hypotheses"></a>
<a id="eq:shell-cap"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Concentration in a thin shell). *Let $S$ and $\nu$ be finite positive Borel measures on $\mathbb R^2$, let $U>0$ and $T\ge0$, and suppose that $$0<\rho\le1,
\qquad 0<\delta\le\frac{\rho^2}{1600}.$$ Write $$D(x,r,\delta)=\{y:\bigl||y-x|-r\bigr|\le\delta\}.$$ There is a Borel set $E\subset\mathbb R^2$ such that $$\label{eq:shell-exception}
|E|\le C\left[
  \left(\frac{\nu(\mathbb R^2)}{U}\right)^2\frac{\delta}{\rho}
  +\frac{\nu(\mathbb R^2)}{U}\frac{\delta}{\rho^2}
           \left(1+\frac{T}{U}\right)\right],$$ and the following implication holds for every $x\notin E$ and every $r\in[1,2]$: if $$\label{eq:shell-hypotheses}
\nu(D(x,r,\delta))\ge U,
\qquad S(B(x,r-\delta))\le T,$$ then there is $z\in D(x,r,\delta)$ for which $$\label{eq:shell-cap}
(S+\nu)\bigl(D(x,r,\delta)\setminus B(z,\rho)\bigr)\le2U.$$ The constant in <a href="#eq:shell-exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-exception">[eq:shell-exception]</a> is absolute. In particular, neither the exceptional set nor its bound involves a choice of $r$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-shell-concentration"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-shell-concentration</summary>

*Proof.* If the cap conclusion fails, every shell point $z$ sees more than $U$ of either $\nu$ or $S$ outside its cap. The first alternative will restrict the centers to thin strips. For the second, nested tangent disks capture that remote $S$-mass during a short parameter interval, starting with mass at most $T$. We first prove the needed geometry, then combine the two alternatives in one Borel majorant that contains no radius parameter.

<a id="eq:shell-entry"></a>
Put $v=200\delta/\rho^2$, so $v\le1/8$. We first record the geometry of disks tangent at a fixed point $z$. For a unit vector $\theta$, the disks $$B(z+L\theta,L),\qquad L>0,$$ increase with $L$. In fact, for $w=y-z$, membership is the inequality $$\label{eq:shell-entry}
|w|^2<2L\,w\cdot\theta.$$ When $w\cdot\theta>0$, the entry parameter is $L_*(y)=|w|^2/(2w\cdot\theta)$.

<a id="eq:shell-entry-window"></a>
<a id="eq:shell-earlier-interior"></a>
Fix $x,z$ with $l=|x-z|\in[1/2,3]$ and put $\theta=(x-z)/l$. If $|y-z|\ge\rho$ and $\bigl||y-x|-l\bigr|\le2\delta$, then, with $a=(y-z)\cdot\theta$, $$\bigl||y-z|^2-2la\bigr|
 =\bigl||y-x|^2-l^2\bigr|\le13\delta.$$ The restriction on $\delta$ gives $a\ge\rho^2/12>0$, and hence $$\label{eq:shell-entry-window}
|L_*(y)-l|\le78\frac{\delta}{\rho^2}<\frac v2.$$ Thus all such points enter strictly between $l-v$ and $l+v$. Conversely, if $|y-z|\ge\rho$ and $y\in B(z+(l-v)\theta,l-v)$, then $a>\rho^2/(2(l-v))\ge\rho^2/6$. It follows that $$|y-x|^2<l^2-2va
 \le l^2-\frac{200}{3}\delta<(l-2\delta)^2.$$ We have proved $$\label{eq:shell-earlier-interior}
\{y:|y-z|\ge\rho\}\cap B(z+(l-v)\theta,l-v)
       \subset B(x,l-2\delta).$$ Figure <a href="#fig:tangent-disks" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:tangent-disks">1</a> illustrates the nested disks and these two comparisons.

<figure id="fig:tangent-disks">

<figcaption>The tangent-disk sweep. All boundary circles pass through $z$. A shell point $y$ with $|y−z| ≥ ρ$ enters between the parameters $l − v$ and $l + v$, with $v$ proportional to $δ/ρ^{2}$. Mass outside $B(z,ρ)$ already present at $l − v$ lies in the inner disk $B(x,l−2δ)$. When $z ∈ D(x,r,δ)$, this disk is contained in the controlled disk $B(x,r−δ)$. The drawing is schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-tangent-disks"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:tangent-disks`)

TikZ/PGF source, copied verbatim from the source:

```latex
\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.05,>=Stealth]
 \draw[gray!60,thin] (1.55,0) circle[radius=1.55];
 \draw[black,thick] (2,0) circle[radius=2];
 \draw[gray!75,dashed] (2.45,0) circle[radius=2.45];
 \draw[->,gray] (-.35,0)--(5.35,0) node[right] {\(\theta\)};
 \fill (0,0) circle (1.8pt) node[below left] {\(z\)};
 \fill (1.55,0) circle (1.3pt) node[below=5pt] {\(z+(l-v)\theta\)};
 \fill (2,0) circle (1.6pt) node[above=4pt] {\(x=z+l\theta\)};
 \fill (2.45,0) circle (1.3pt);
 \draw[gray] (2.45,-.07)--(5.3,-.85)
   node[right,black] {\(z+(l+v)\theta\)};
 \fill (2,2) circle (2pt) node[above right] {\(y\)};
 \draw[dotted] (0,0)--(2,2);
 \node[align=left,anchor=west] at (5.3,1.65)
   {\(\displaystyle B(z+L\theta,L)\)\\[2pt]increases with \(L\)};
\end{tikzpicture}
```

Visual witness description of PDF page 10 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A schematic geometric diagram illustrating a tangent-disk sweep. Two circular disks passing through a common boundary point $z$ on the left are shown, with a horizontal ray extending to the right with an arrow labeled $\theta$. Along this ray, the points $z$, $z + (l - v)\theta$, $x = z + l\theta$ (marked with a solid point), and $z + (l + v)\theta$ are indicated. In the shell region between the boundaries of the inner disk and the outer disk, a point $y$ is plotted. An arrow pointing outward on the right is accompanied by the text "$B(z + L\theta, L)$ increases with $L$".
```


<a id="eq:shell-ray-condition"></a>
<a id="eq:shell-majorant"></a>
For $z\in\mathbb R^2$ and $\theta$ on the unit circle define $$H_{z,\theta}(L)=
 \begin{cases}
 S\bigl(\{y:|y-z|\ge\rho\}\cap B(z+L\theta,L)\bigr),&L>0,\\
 0,&L\le0.
 \end{cases}$$ This is bounded and nondecreasing in $L$. It is jointly Borel in all its arguments, since it is the integral of the Borel indicator given by <a href="#eq:shell-entry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-entry">[eq:shell-entry]</a>, together with the other displayed tests. For $l=|x-z|\in[1/2,3]$ and $\theta=(x-z)/l$, let $\mathcal C(x,z)$ be the condition $$\label{eq:shell-ray-condition}
H_{z,\theta}(l-v)\le T,
\qquad H_{z,\theta}(l+v)-H_{z,\theta}(l-v)\ge U.$$ Define the nonnegative Borel function $$\begin{aligned}
\mathcal M(x)=\frac1U\int_{1/2\le|x-z|\le3}
 \Bigg[&\frac1U\int
 \mathbf1_{\{\,\left||y-x|-|x-z|\right|\le2\delta,
                         \ |y-z|\ge\rho\,\}}\,d\nu(y)
 \notag\\[-2mm]
 &+\mathbf1_{\{\mathcal C(x,z)\}}\Bigg]\,d\nu(z),
 \qquad E=\{x:\mathcal M(x)\ge1\}.
 \label{eq:shell-majorant}
\end{aligned}$$

Suppose that <a href="#eq:shell-hypotheses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-hypotheses">[eq:shell-hypotheses]</a> holds at $(x,r)$ and that <a href="#eq:shell-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-cap">[eq:shell-cap]</a> fails for every $z\in D=D(x,r,\delta)$. For any such $z$, either $\nu(D\setminus B(z,\rho))>U$ or $S(D\setminus B(z,\rho))>U$. Also $l=|x-z|\in[1/2,3]$ and every $y\in D$ satisfies $\bigl||y-x|-l\bigr|\le2\delta$. In the first alternative, the first term in brackets in <a href="#eq:shell-majorant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-majorant">[eq:shell-majorant]</a> exceeds one. In the second alternative, <a href="#eq:shell-entry-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-entry-window">[eq:shell-entry-window]</a> gives the second inequality in <a href="#eq:shell-ray-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-ray-condition">[eq:shell-ray-condition]</a>. The first inequality follows from <a href="#eq:shell-earlier-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-earlier-interior">[eq:shell-earlier-interior]</a>, because $l-2\delta\le r-\delta$. The bracket is therefore at least one for every $z\in D$. Since $\nu(D)\ge U$, we obtain $\mathcal M(x)\ge1$. This proves the required implication outside the Borel set $E$, simultaneously for all $r$.

It remains to estimate $\int\mathcal M$. For fixed $y,z$ with $|y-z|\ge\rho$, the centers in the first term satisfy $|x-z|\le3$ and $$\bigl||x-y|^2-|x-z|^2\bigr|\le13\delta.$$ The difference of squares is an affine function of $x$ with gradient of length $2|y-z|$. These centers lie in a strip of width at most $13\delta/|y-z|$ intersected with $\overline B(z,3)$, and thus have area at most $C\delta/\rho$. Tonelli’s theorem bounds the first contribution to $\int\mathcal M$ by $C\nu(\mathbb R^2)^2\delta/(U^2\rho)$.

For the second contribution, fix $z,\theta$ and abbreviate $H=H_{z,\theta}$ and $G=\min(H,T+U)$. On the set of $l$ satisfying <a href="#eq:shell-ray-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-ray-condition">[eq:shell-ray-condition]</a>, $$G(l+v)-G(l-v)\ge U.$$ Because $G$ is bounded and nondecreasing, $$\int_{\mathbb R}\bigl(G(l+v)-G(l-v)\bigr)\,dl\le2v(T+U).$$ For completeness, integration over $[-M,M]$ cancels the overlapping intervals and leaves two intervals of length $2v$; letting $M$ increase proves the inequality. Thus the permitted $l$ have length at most $2v(1+T/U)$. Polar coordinates about $z$, with $l\le3$, and another application of Tonelli’s theorem bound the second contribution by $C\nu(\mathbb R^2)v(1+T/U)/U$. Since $|E|\le\int\mathcal M$, these two estimates prove <a href="#eq:shell-exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-exception">[eq:shell-exception]</a>. ◻

</details>

Under its shell and inner-disk mass hypotheses, the shell lemma supplies a cap for every radius at a center outside one Borel set. The affine identity then suppresses the mass on that cap. To obtain the discounted estimates, we still have to compare kernel widths, control the positive interior mass in the directional argument, and treat radii forced at band endpoints. We do this next; the value estimate will require a separate kernel.
