<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Cancellation of small error pieces

We now compare kernels at dyadic widths. Put <a id="eq:local-exponents"></a>$$\label{eq:local-exponents}
b=2\alpha=\frac1{50},\qquad
\eta=\frac1{10},\qquad \gamma=\frac15,
\qquad \delta_j=2^{-j}.$$ Choose a fixed $\delta_*>0$ so small that $\delta_*\le1/4$ and $\delta\le\delta^{2\gamma}/1600$ whenever $0<\delta\le\delta_*$.

<a id="eq:local-basic-cancellation"></a>
We record estimates valid for any sequence of probability-mixture kernels $K_j$, centered at the same $x$ and $r\in[1,2]$, with the support and Lipschitz properties of Lemma <a href="#lem:local-balanced" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-balanced">4</a> at width $\delta_j$. The zero-moment condition is not needed for these estimates. Write <a id="eq:local-coarse-measure"></a>$$\label{eq:local-coarse-measure}
\nu_j=\sum_{s_d\ge\delta_j^2}|\beta_d|,
\qquad p_j=\int K_j\,d\beta,
\qquad D_j=D(x,r,\delta_j).$$ Then $$\label{eq:local-basic-cancellation}
\nu_j(\mathbb R^2)\le A\delta_j^{-b},
\qquad |p_j|\le C A\delta_j^{-\alpha}.$$ Indeed, for $s_d<1$, subtracting the value of $K_j$ at the center of a supporting ball gives $$\left|\int K_j\,d\beta_d\right|
 \le C m_d\min(1,s_d/\delta_j).$$ For $s_d=1$, the same upper bound holds directly from $|K_j|\le1$. Now use $\min(1,a)\le a^\alpha$ for $a>0$ and the definition of $A$. The bound for $\nu_j$ follows directly from $s_d\ge\delta_j^2$.

<a id="eq:local-fine-cancellation"></a>
<a id="eq:local-increment-tests"></a>
If $F$ is a scalar or complex test function with $\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(F)\le C/\delta_j$, the pieces with $s_d<\delta_j^2$ satisfy $$\label{eq:local-fine-cancellation}
\left|\sum_{s_d<\delta_j^2}\int F\,d\beta_d\right|
 \le \frac{C}{\delta_j}\sum_{s_d<\delta_j^2}s_dm_d
 \le C A\delta_j^{1-b}.$$ Only the Lipschitz bound is used here; constants cancel because each of these pieces has zero total vector mass. In particular, <a href="#eq:local-fine-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-fine-cancellation">[eq:local-fine-cancellation]</a> applies to $K_{j+1}-K_j$ and to $\overline{y-x}(K_{j+1}-K_j)$. These tests have the required global Lipschitz bounds, since their supports are in $B(x,3)$. Their supports are also in $D_j$. Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
|p_{j+1}-p_j|
&\le C\bigl(\nu_j(D_j)+A\delta_j^{1-b}\bigr),
\notag\\
\left|\int\overline{y-x}(K_{j+1}-K_j)\,d\beta(y)\right|
&\le C\bigl(\nu_j(D_j)+A\delta_j^{1-b}\bigr).
\label{eq:local-increment-tests}
\end{aligned}$$

All these kernels are supported in $\Omega$ when $x\in Q$. Thus <a href="#eq:local-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-decomposition">[eq:local-decomposition]</a> can be used in every pairing below, even if some of its component measures have support outside $\Omega$. For fixed $x,r$, as $j\to\infty$ the kernels converge off the circle to $K_\infty=\mathbf1_{B(x,r)}$. Absolute continuity of all the component measures gives convergence of their integrals by dominated convergence. The same holds for the moment integrals. In particular, if every finite-width kernel is balanced, then $K_\infty$ has zero moment as well. This is a statement at each fixed $(x,r)$: every circle has zero mass for every absolutely continuous component. There is no exceptional set depending on the radius to remove. Absolute continuity is used here, but was not needed in the Borel shell lemma.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The directional estimate

We now prove <a href="#eq:local-direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-direction">[eq:local-direction]</a>. If $A=0$, all the error measures vanish, and $e\cdot h(B(x,r))\ge0$ for every relevant disk; the conclusion follows. Assume $A>0$.

We prove a superlevel-set estimate by separating widths at which the coarse error mass in the shell is small from those at which it is large. Small-mass widths have directly summable error increments. At the remaining widths we compare only increases of the negative projection: the later negative projection controls the interior positive mass, allowing Lemma <a href="#lem:shell-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shell-concentration">6</a> to be applied.

<a id="eq:local-start-scale"></a>
<a id="eq:local-inactive"></a>
Let $\lambda\ge L_0A$, where the fixed constant $L_0$ will be chosen large enough in the course of the proof. Put $q=A/\lambda$ and choose $j_0$ so that $$\label{eq:local-start-scale}
q/2<\delta_{j_0}\le q.$$ We require $L_0\ge1/\delta_*$, so all widths under consideration are at most $\delta_*$. At a fixed $x\in Q$ and $r\in\mathcal I(x)$, use Lemma <a href="#lem:local-balanced" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-balanced">4</a> to choose $K_j$ for every $j\ge j_0$, and write $$P_j=\int K_j\,dh,
\qquad N_j=(-e\cdot P_j)_+,
\qquad U_j=\lambda\delta_j^\eta.$$ Call $j$ active for $(x,r)$ if $\nu_j(D_j)\ge U_j$. If it is inactive, <a href="#eq:local-increment-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-increment-tests">[eq:local-increment-tests]</a> and $1-b>\eta$ give $$\label{eq:local-inactive}
|p_{j+1}-p_j|
+\left|\int\overline{y-x}(K_{j+1}-K_j)\,d\beta(y)\right|
 \le C\lambda\delta_j^\eta.$$ Here and below we use $A\le\lambda$.

<a id="eq:local-shell-tail"></a>
Choose $C_0$ sufficiently large depending only on $C_s$ and $\kappa$, and put $T_j=C_0\lambda\delta_j^{-b}$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:shell-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shell-concentration">6</a> with $$\nu=\nu_j,\quad \delta=\delta_j,\quad
\rho=\delta_j^\gamma,\quad U=U_j,\quad T=T_j.$$ It gives Borel sets $E_j$, independent of $r$, such that $$\label{eq:local-shell-tail}
|E_j|\le C\left[
q^2\delta_j^{14/25}+q\delta_j^{9/25}\right].$$ To check the exponents, the first term of <a href="#eq:shell-exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:shell-exception">[eq:shell-exception]</a> gives $1-\gamma-2b-2\eta=14/25$. The second term is a sum of powers $1-2\gamma-b-\eta$ and $1-2\gamma-2b-2\eta=9/25$; because $\delta_j\le1$, both are bounded by a constant times the latter. Constants in <a href="#eq:local-shell-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-shell-tail">[eq:local-shell-tail]</a> may depend on the already fixed $C_0$.

<a id="eq:local-cone-sign"></a>
<a id="eq:local-first-active"></a>
Fix a center outside $\bigcup_{j\ge j_0}E_j$, and keep any $r\in\mathcal I(x)$ fixed. Positivity in the direction $e$ implies $$\label{eq:local-cone-sign}
e\cdot P_j\ge\kappa\int K_j\,dS+e\cdot p_j,
\qquad N_j\le|p_j|.$$ Before the first active index, all the increments of $p_j$ obey <a href="#eq:local-inactive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-inactive">[eq:local-inactive]</a>. Hence, if $j$ is the first active index, or if $j=\infty$ and there are no active indices, $$\label{eq:local-first-active}
N_j\le C\left(A\delta_{j_0}^{-\alpha}
                   +\lambda\delta_{j_0}^\eta\right).$$ The formula at infinity follows from the dominated-convergence observation above.

The first active width has therefore been controlled. To continue, we compare consecutive active widths, allowing arbitrarily many inactive widths between them. Only an increase of the negative projection needs a new estimate; that sign will supply the positive interior-mass bound required by the shell lemma.

<a id="eq:local-gap-errors"></a>
<a id="eq:local-chosen-cap"></a>
Suppose now that $j$ is active and $l>j$ is the next active index; set $l=\infty$ if there is no next one. The individual bound in <a href="#eq:local-basic-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-basic-cancellation">[eq:local-basic-cancellation]</a> at $j$ and $j+1$, followed by the inactive increments between $j+1$ and $l$, gives $$\label{eq:local-gap-errors}
|p_j|+|p_l|
 \le C\left(A\delta_j^{-\alpha}
                  +\lambda\delta_j^\eta\right)
 \le C\lambda\delta_j^{-b}.$$ We need to estimate a change only when $N_l>N_j$. In that case $e\cdot P_l<0$, so <a href="#eq:local-cone-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-cone-sign">[eq:local-cone-sign]</a> gives $$\int K_l\,dS\le\kappa^{-1}|p_l|.$$ Since $K_l=1$ on $B(x,r-\delta_j)$, this proves $S(B(x,r-\delta_j))\le T_j$ after fixing $C_0$ large enough to dominate the constant just obtained. The shell lemma therefore supplies a point $z\in D_j$ such that $$\label{eq:local-chosen-cap}
(S+\nu_j)\bigl(D_j\setminus B(z,\delta_j^\gamma)\bigr)
 \le2U_j.$$ This point is used only at the current center and radius; no measurable selection of $z$ is required.

<a id="eq:local-weighted-good"></a>
To estimate the positive mass on the cap, we need a weighted bound for both kernels being compared. The sign at $l$ supplies one, but $e\cdot P_j>0$ would give no corresponding bound for $K_j$. We therefore replace $K_j$, when necessary, by a kernel with zero projection and the same negative part. If $e\cdot P_j\le0$, set $K_*=K_j$. Otherwise, interpolate between $K_j$ and $K_l$ to obtain a convex combination $K_*$ with $e\cdot P_*=0$, where $P_* =\int K_*\,dh$. This is possible because $e\cdot P_l<0$. In either case, $$N_*:=(-e\cdot P_*)_+=N_j,
\qquad e\cdot P_*\le0.$$ Writing $p_* =\int K_*\,d\beta$ and using <a href="#eq:local-gap-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-gap-errors">[eq:local-gap-errors]</a> and <a href="#eq:local-cone-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-cone-sign">[eq:local-cone-sign]</a>, we get $$\label{eq:local-weighted-good}
\int(K_l+K_*)\,dS\le C\lambda\delta_j^{-b}.$$

<a id="eq:local-corrected-difference"></a>
<a id="eq:local-good-cap-cost"></a>
All of $K_j,K_l,K_*$ have zero moment, including when $l=\infty$ by the preceding sharp-kernel limit. Since $|z-x|\ge1/2$, define $$W(y)=1-\frac{\overline{y-x}}{\overline{z-x}}.$$ The affine identity in Lemma <a href="#lem:local-affine" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-affine">5</a> gives $$\label{eq:local-corrected-difference}
P_l-P_* =\int W(y)(K_l-K_*)(y)\,dh(y).$$ The difference of kernels is supported on $D_j$, and $$|W|\le C\delta_j^\gamma\quad\hbox{on }B(z,\delta_j^\gamma),
\qquad |W|\le C\quad\hbox{on }D_j.$$ The contribution of $\sum_i v_i\sigma_i$ to <a href="#eq:local-corrected-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-corrected-difference">[eq:local-corrected-difference]</a> is therefore at most $$\label{eq:local-good-cap-cost}
C\lambda\delta_j^{\gamma-b}+C U_j,$$ by <a href="#eq:local-weighted-good" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-weighted-good">[eq:local-weighted-good]</a>, $|K_l-K_*|\le K_l+K_*$, and <a href="#eq:local-chosen-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-chosen-cap">[eq:local-chosen-cap]</a>.

<a id="eq:local-active-increment"></a>
For the error measures, $K_l-K_*$ is a scalar multiple in $[0,1]$ of $K_l-K_j$. Keep $W$ fixed and telescope <a id="eq:local-error-telescoping"></a>$$\label{eq:local-error-telescoping}
\int W(K_l-K_j)\,d\beta
 =\sum_{i=j}^{l-1}\int W(K_{i+1}-K_i)\,d\beta.$$ For $l=\infty$ this equality means the limit of the finite sums. We never apply a Lipschitz cancellation bound directly to the sharp kernel $K_\infty$ or to its interpolant: only the finite consecutive differences in this sum need such a bound. On the first step, the pieces contributing to $\nu_j$ cost at most $C\delta_j^\gamma\nu_j(\mathbb R^2)+CU_j$ by <a href="#eq:local-chosen-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-chosen-cap">[eq:local-chosen-cap]</a>. The remaining pieces cost $CA\delta_j^{1-b}$ by <a href="#eq:local-fine-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-fine-cancellation">[eq:local-fine-cancellation]</a>, since $W(K_{j+1}-K_j)$ has Lipschitz constant at most $C/\delta_j$. For $j<i<l$, use $$\int W(K_{i+1}-K_i)\,d\beta
 =p_{i+1}-p_i-\frac1{\overline{z-x}}
       \int\overline{y-x}(K_{i+1}-K_i)\,d\beta(y).$$ These are inactive steps, so their absolute values sum to at most $C\lambda\delta_j^\eta$ by <a href="#eq:local-inactive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-inactive">[eq:local-inactive]</a>. Combining the estimates gives $$\left|\int W(K_l-K_j)\,d\beta\right|
 \le C\left(A\delta_j^{\gamma-b}
       +A\delta_j^{1-b}+\lambda\delta_j^\eta\right).$$ Together with <a href="#eq:local-good-cap-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-good-cap-cost">[eq:local-good-cap-cost]</a>, and using $\gamma-b=9/50>\eta$, this yields $$\label{eq:local-active-increment}
N_l\le N_j+|P_l-P_*|\le N_j+C\lambda\delta_j^\eta.$$ The same inequality is automatic when $N_l\le N_j$.

Sum <a href="#eq:local-active-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-active-increment">[eq:local-active-increment]</a> along the active indices. If there are finitely many, include the last comparison with $l=\infty$; if there are infinitely many, pass to the limit along them. Dominated convergence and <a href="#eq:local-first-active" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-first-active">[eq:local-first-active]</a> give <a id="eq:local-final-projection"></a>$$\label{eq:local-final-projection}
(-e\cdot h(B(x,r)))_+
 \le C\left(A\delta_{j_0}^{-\alpha}
                   +\lambda\delta_{j_0}^\eta\right)
 \le C\lambda\left(q^{1-\alpha}+q^\eta\right).$$ This holds for every $r\in\mathcal I(x)$ at the fixed center outside the exceptional sets. Increase $L_0$, depending only on $C_s$ and $\kappa$, so that the last bound is less than $\lambda$ whenever $q\le L_0^{-1}$.

Let $Y(x)=\sup_{r\in\mathcal I(x)}(-e\cdot h(B(x,r)))_+$. Summing <a href="#eq:local-shell-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-shell-tail">[eq:local-shell-tail]</a> and using <a href="#eq:local-start-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-start-scale">[eq:local-start-scale]</a>, we have proved <a id="eq:local-direction-tail"></a>$$\label{eq:local-direction-tail}
|\{x\in Q:Y(x)>\lambda\}|
 \le C\left[
       (A/\lambda)^{64/25}+(A/\lambda)^{34/25}\right]
 \qquad(\lambda\ge L_0A).$$ Both powers exceed one. The layer-cake formula, the trivial bound $|Q|=1$ for smaller thresholds, and the substitution $\lambda=As$ give $$\int_QY\,dx
 \le L_0A+C A\int_{L_0}^{\infty}
                  (s^{-64/25}+s^{-34/25})\,ds\le C A.$$ Finally, <a href="#eq:local-spatial-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-spatial-gradient">[eq:local-spatial-gradient]</a> and $r\ge1$ prove <a href="#eq:local-direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-direction">[eq:local-direction]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The size estimate, including band endpoints

To prove <a href="#eq:local-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-size">[eq:local-size]</a>, place the measures $v_i\sigma_i$ among the error measures with assigned size one. The augmented price is $A_{\mathrm{tot}}=A+S(\mathbb R^2)$, where $A$ and $S$ refer to the original decomposition. The augmented positive part is zero; in this proof, $\beta$ and $\nu_j$ refer to the augmented error family. If $A_{\mathrm{tot}}=0$, the result is immediate.

Take $\lambda\ge L_0A_{\mathrm{tot}}$, put $q=A_{\mathrm{tot}}/\lambda$, and choose $j_0$ as in <a href="#eq:local-start-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-start-scale">[eq:local-start-scale]</a>. Here $L_0$ will depend only on $C_s$. For $x\in Q$ and $r\in\mathcal J(x)$, choose the balanced kernel $K_j$ when $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(r,\{1,2\})\ge\delta_j$. Such an $r$ is an actual maximizer on $[1,2]$, so the two neighboring radii needed in Lemma <a href="#lem:local-balanced" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local-balanced">4</a> are admissible. Otherwise choose the uniform probability mixture over $[r-\delta_j,r+\delta_j]$. The latter kernel need not have zero moment, but has all the same support, positivity and Lipschitz bounds. Thus <a href="#eq:local-basic-cancellation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-basic-cancellation">[eq:local-basic-cancellation]</a>–<a href="#eq:local-increment-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-increment-tests">[eq:local-increment-tests]</a> remain valid with $A_{\mathrm{tot}}$ in place of $A$. Write $P_j=\int K_j\,dh=p_j$, and again call $j$ active when $\nu_j(D_j)\ge U_j=\lambda\delta_j^\eta$.

<a id="eq:local-endpoint-exception"></a>
There is an additional Borel exceptional set for each width: $$\label{eq:local-endpoint-exception}
F_j=\bigcup_{a\in\{1,2\}}
 \left\{x:\nu_j\{y:\bigl||y-x|-a\bigr|\le2\delta_j\}
                         \ge U_j\right\}.$$ For each fixed $y$, the areas of these two annuli of centers sum to $24\pi\delta_j$. Tonelli’s theorem and Markov’s inequality give <a id="eq:local-endpoint-area"></a>$$\label{eq:local-endpoint-area}
|F_j|\le24\pi\delta_j\frac{\nu_j(\mathbb R^2)}{U_j}
 \le C(A_{\mathrm{tot}}/\lambda)\delta_j^{22/25}.$$ If $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(r,\{1,2\})<\delta_j$, the shell $D_j$ lies in one of the annuli in <a href="#eq:local-endpoint-exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-endpoint-exception">[eq:local-endpoint-exception]</a>. Thus, outside $F_j$, an active index must satisfy $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(r,\{1,2\})\ge\delta_j$, and both $K_j$ and $K_{j+1}$ have zero moment. In particular, the forced radii $r=1,2$ are covered even if they are not maximizers.

Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:shell-concentration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:shell-concentration">6</a> with $S=0$, $T=0$, $\nu=\nu_j$, $\delta=\delta_j$, $\rho=\delta_j^\gamma$, and $U=U_j$. It supplies Borel exceptional sets $E_j$, independent of $r$, with bound $C[q^2\delta_j^{14/25}+q\delta_j^{12/25}]$, which is at most the right side of <a href="#eq:local-shell-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-shell-tail">[eq:local-shell-tail]</a>. Its constants have no cone parameter: the positive measure and its interior-mass threshold are both zero. At an active step outside $E_j\cup F_j$, its interior-mass hypothesis is automatic, and it gives a point $z\in D_j$ with $\nu_j(D_j\setminus B(z,\delta_j^\gamma))\le2U_j$. The zero moments allow the correction $$P_{j+1}-P_j=\int
 \left(1-\frac{\overline{y-x}}{\overline{z-x}}\right)
 (K_{j+1}-K_j)(y)\,d\beta(y).$$ The coarse pieces cost at most $C A_{\mathrm{tot}}\delta_j^{\gamma-b}+CU_j$, and the fine pieces cost at most $C A_{\mathrm{tot}}\delta_j^{1-b}$, exactly as above. Hence $$|P_{j+1}-P_j|\le C\lambda\delta_j^\eta.$$ At an inactive step the same inequality follows directly from <a href="#eq:local-increment-tests" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-increment-tests">[eq:local-increment-tests]</a>, without using a moment identity. Starting with $|P_{j_0}|\le CA_{\mathrm{tot}}\delta_{j_0}^{-\alpha}$ and summing all increments, we obtain $$|h(B(x,r))|\le C\lambda(q^{1-\alpha}+q^\eta)<\lambda$$ after increasing $L_0$. This holds simultaneously for all $r\in\mathcal J(x)$ outside $\bigcup_{j\ge j_0}(E_j\cup F_j)$. The measure bound is therefore <a id="eq:local-size-tail"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\left|\left\{x\in Q:
       \sup_{r\in\mathcal J(x)}|h(B(x,r))|>\lambda\right\}\right|
 \le C\bigl[&(A_{\mathrm{tot}}/\lambda)^{64/25}
              +(A_{\mathrm{tot}}/\lambda)^{34/25}
 \notag\\[-1mm]
              &+(A_{\mathrm{tot}}/\lambda)^{47/25}\bigr].
\label{eq:local-size-tail}
\end{aligned}$$ Every exponent is larger than one. The same layer-cake computation as before gives the unnormalized bound by $CA_{\mathrm{tot}}$. Since $A_{\mathrm{tot}}=A+S(\mathbb R^2)$, <a href="#eq:local-spatial-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-spatial-gradient">[eq:local-spatial-gradient]</a> proves <a href="#eq:local-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-size">[eq:local-size]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The value-oscillation estimate

This estimate involves all small radii and does not use a maximizing property. We express $g$ minus a fixed mean through its gradient, prove an $L^{3/2}$ cancellation bound for the resulting kernel, and then apply the strong maximal inequality.

<a id="eq:local-value-kernel"></a>
We finish the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local">3</a> by establishing <a href="#eq:local-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-value">[eq:local-value]</a>. Choose a nonnegative smooth probability density $\psi$ supported in the interior of $Q$, with uniformly bounded derivatives after translating and rotating a fixed choice, and put $$c=\int g(z)\psi(z)\,dz,
\qquad E_Q=\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,Q)<4\}.$$ For $x\in E_Q$, integrate $\nabla g$ along the segments from $x$ to points of $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi$ and change variables to obtain $$\label{eq:local-value-kernel}
g(x)-c=\int L(x,y)\cdot dh(y),
\qquad
L(x,y)=-(y-x)\int_0^1
              \psi\bigl(x+(y-x)/t\bigr)t^{-3}\,dt.$$ Indeed, this is the fundamental theorem of calculus in $g(x)-g(z)=-\int_0^1(z-x)\cdot\nabla g(x+t(z-x))\,dt$, followed by $y=x+t(z-x)$.

<a id="eq:local-value-kernel-bounds"></a>
The displayed formula defines $L(x,y)$ for every $y\ne x$ in the plane, not only for $y\in\Omega$. This global definition will allow cancellation against a support center outside $\Omega$. For each fixed $x\in E_Q$, the kernel is supported on segments joining $x$ to $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\psi$, and hence is supported in $\Omega$. It is smooth in $y\ne x$, with the uniform bounds $$\label{eq:local-value-kernel-bounds}
|L(x,y)|\le\frac{C}{|x-y|},
\qquad |\nabla_yL(x,y)|\le\frac{C}{|x-y|^2}.$$ To verify them, if the integrand or one of its derivatives is nonzero, then $|y-x|\le Ct$, since $x\in E_Q$ and $x+(y-x)/t$ lies in a fixed bounded neighborhood of $Q$. The first bound follows by integrating $t^{-3}$ from $|y-x|/C$ to one and multiplying by $|y-x|$. Differentiation in $y$ produces either the same integral or $|y-x|\int t^{-4}\,dt$, which gives the second bound.

<a id="eq:local-kernel-lp"></a>
Fix $p=3/2$, so $2/p-1=1/3>\alpha$. The first bound in <a href="#eq:local-value-kernel-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-value-kernel-bounds">[eq:local-value-kernel-bounds]</a> implies $$\label{eq:local-kernel-lp}
\sup_{y\in\mathbb R^2}\|L(\cdot,y)\|_{L^p(E_Q)}\le C.$$ This follows by integrating $|x-y|^{-p}$ on a bounded set; $p<2$ makes the singularity integrable, uniformly in $y$.

<a id="eq:local-value-lp"></a>
Consider a piece $\beta_d$ with $s_d<1$, supported in $B(z,a)$, where we may take $a=C_s s_d$ after increasing $C_s$ to at least one. On $E_Q\cap B(z,2a)$, the first kernel bound and Minkowski’s inequality give $$\left\|\int L(\cdot,y)\cdot d\beta_d(y)\right\|_
                       {L^p(E_Q\cap B(z,2a))}
 \le C a^{2/p-1}m_d.$$ In fact, for $y\in B(z,a)$ the relevant $x$ lie within $3a$ of $y$, and the $L^p$ norm of $|x-y|^{-1}$ there is $C a^{2/p-1}$. For $|x-z|>2a$, use the zero total vector mass to subtract $L(x,z)$. The derivative bound in <a href="#eq:local-value-kernel-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-value-kernel-bounds">[eq:local-value-kernel-bounds]</a>, applied on the segment from $z$ to $y$, gives $$\left|\int L(x,y)\cdot d\beta_d(y)\right|
 \le C a m_d|x-z|^{-2}.$$ Its $L^p$ norm on $\{|x-z|>2a\}$ is at most $C a^{2/p-1}m_d$, since $p>1$. Combining the two regions yields <a id="eq:local-value-piece"></a>$$\label{eq:local-value-piece}
\left\|\int L(\cdot,y)\cdot d\beta_d(y)\right\|_{L^p(E_Q)}
 \le C s_d^{1/3}m_d\le C s_d^\alpha m_d.$$ The constant may depend on $C_s$. For a piece of assigned size one, and for the positive measures, use <a href="#eq:local-kernel-lp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-kernel-lp">[eq:local-kernel-lp]</a> directly. Minkowski’s inequality and the finite decomposition therefore give $$\label{eq:local-value-lp}
\|g-c\|_{L^{3/2}(E_Q)}\le C\bigl(A+S(\mathbb R^2)\bigr).$$ These same estimates show that the integrals obtained by inserting <a href="#eq:local-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-decomposition">[eq:local-decomposition]</a> into <a href="#eq:local-value-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-value-kernel">[eq:local-value-kernel]</a> exist for almost every $x$; thus the use of the potentially singular kernel is justified.

Let $f_Q=(g-c)\mathbf1_{E_Q}$, extended by zero to the plane. Every disk centered at $x\in Q$ of radius at most four is contained in $E_Q$. Consequently $$\sup_{0<t,t'\le4}|u_t(x)-u_{t'}(x)|\le2Mf_Q(x),$$ where the maximal function on the right uses absolute values. The classical strong $L^{3/2}$ maximal inequality (Kinnunen 1997, (1.2)) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Kinnunen1997](#ref-Kinnunen1997)], followed by Hölder’s inequality on the unit square and <a href="#eq:local-value-lp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-value-lp">[eq:local-value-lp]</a>, proves <a href="#eq:local-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-value">[eq:local-value]</a>. This completes the proof of Lemma <a href="#lem:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local">3</a>.

<a id="sec:prices"></a>
### Dyadic vector masses and common prices

Throughout this section, let $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ be real valued, and write $$h=\nabla g\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,
 \qquad V=|h|(\mathbb R^2),
 \qquad u_t=A_tg.$$ The averages $u_t$ are signed. Fix an integer $m\ge1$; all estimates below are uniform in $m$. Lemma <a href="#lem:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local">3</a> discounts a cancelling error according to the size of its support. We now decompose the gradient into errors to which that discount applies, and assign one summable price to each dyadic cell. The same price will work for every direction in a fixed finite palette. The construction starts with the actual density of each scalar coordinate of the gradient at the finest level and removes equal positive and negative masses as it moves toward coarser levels.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Positive refinement and cone coordinates

<a id="eq:hat-refinement"></a>
For every integer $k$, set $r_k=2^{-k}$ and $V_k=r_k\mathbb Z^2$. These definitions are not restricted to $1,\ldots,m$: the neighboring direction estimate in Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">4</a> will also use level $k-12$. A vertex includes its level, so vertices at different levels are distinct even when their spatial positions agree. For $p\in V_k$, define $$Q_p=p+[0,r_k)^2,
 \qquad
 \phi_p(x)=\prod_{j=1}^2
       \left(1-\frac{|x_j-p_j|}{r_k}\right)_+.$$ For $p\in V_i$ and $q\in V_k$ with $i<k$, put $\lambda_{pq}=\phi_p(q)$, and at equal levels put $\lambda_{pq}=\mathbf 1_{\{p=q\}}$. These nonnegative coefficients satisfy $$\begin{gathered}
 \sum_{p\in V_i}\lambda_{pq}=1,
 \qquad
 \phi_p=\sum_{q\in V_k}\lambda_{pq}\phi_q
 \quad (i\le k),
 \\
 \lambda_{ps}=\sum_{q\in V_j}\lambda_{pq}\lambda_{qs}
 \quad (i\le j\le k,\ p\in V_i,\ s\in V_k).
\end{gathered}
\label{eq:hat-refinement}$$ To verify these identities, first consider one coordinate. The hats on a fixed grid sum to one: between two consecutive vertices, the only nonzero hats have complementary affine values. A coarse hat is affine on every interval of any finer dyadic grid, since all its breakpoints belong to that grid. It therefore equals the piecewise affine interpolant of its values at the finer vertices. Taking products proves the first two identities in <a href="#eq:hat-refinement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hat-refinement">[eq:hat-refinement]</a>; evaluating the second identity at $s$ proves the third. The sums are locally finite. Notice that the normalization is in the coarse index $p$ for each fixed fine vertex $q$. These coefficients are refinement weights, not stochastic transition rows.

<a id="eq:prices-support-nesting"></a>
If $q\in V_{k+1}$ and $\lambda_{pq}>0$, then in each coordinate $q_j-p_j\in\{-r_k/2,0,r_k/2\}$. Consequently $$\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi_q\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi_p.
 \label{eq:prices-support-nesting}$$ Thus each vertex has nine positive-weight children, and the supports remain nested along every path of positive-weight edges.

<a id="eq:prices-vector-mass-budget"></a>
Define the vector masses and their polar decompositions by $$h_p=\int\phi_p\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}h,
 \qquad a_p=|h_p|,
 \qquad h_p=a_p\theta_p,$$ where $\theta_p$ is an arbitrary fixed unit vector when $a_p=0$. Refinement gives $$h_p=\sum_{q\in V_k}\lambda_{pq}h_q
 \quad(p\in V_i,\ i\le k).$$ The triangle inequality and the column sums in <a href="#eq:hat-refinement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hat-refinement">[eq:hat-refinement]</a> imply $$\sum_{p\in V_i}a_p
 \le\sum_{q\in V_k}a_q
 \le V
 \quad(i\le k).
 \label{eq:prices-vector-mass-budget}$$ The last inequality follows directly from $\sum_q\phi_q=1$. At each fixed level only finitely many of these masses can be nonzero, because $h$ has compact support.

<a id="eq:prices-scalar-coordinates"></a>
Fix $\tau=1/100$ and a finite $\tau/20$-net $\mathcal N$ of the unit circle, measured in chord distance. For $n\in\mathcal N$, let $n^\perp$ be its counterclockwise quarter turn and put $$e_+(n)=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}n+\frac12n^\perp,
 \qquad
 e_-(n)=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}n-\frac12n^\perp.$$ These are unit vectors and $e_+(n)\cdot e_-(n)=1/2$. Call $(q,n)$ *good* when $\theta_q$ belongs to the closed sector between $e_-(n)$ and $e_+(n)$, and *bad* otherwise. The two scalar coefficient functionals $$c_{n,+}(z)=\frac{n\cdot z}{\sqrt3}+n^\perp\cdot z,
 \qquad
 c_{n,-}(z)=\frac{n\cdot z}{\sqrt3}-n^\perp\cdot z
 \label{eq:prices-scalar-coordinates}$$ satisfy $$z=e_+(n)c_{n,+}(z)+e_-(n)c_{n,-}(z),
 \qquad
 |c_{n,+}(z)|+|c_{n,-}(z)|
 =2\max\left\{\frac{|n\cdot z|}{\sqrt3},
                     |n^\perp\cdot z|\right\}
 \le2|z|.$$ In particular, for a good pair $(q,n)$, both coefficients of $h_q$ are nonnegative, including when $h_q=0$. This sign information will supply the positive cone measures in Lemma <a href="#lem:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local">3</a>.

<a id="eq:cone-algebra"></a>
For the finite-band assembly, we also need to bound a vector’s norm by its signed projection along $n$ and the unfavorable projections controlled by the local estimate. The following inequality gives that bound, with an absolute constant: $$|z|\le C\left(n\cdot z+
                \sum_{\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}}
                (-e_\varepsilon(n)\cdot z)_+\right)
 \quad(z\in\mathbb R^2).
 \label{eq:cone-algebra}$$ Here the expression in parentheses is nonnegative. Indeed, writing $a=n\cdot z$, its value is positive for every nonzero $z$: if $a>0$ this is immediate; if $a=0$, one of the two negative parts equals $|z|/2$; and if $a<0$, their sum is at least $-(e_+(n)+e_-(n))\cdot z=\sqrt3|a|$, so the expression is at least $(\sqrt3-1)|a|>0$. Its minimum on the unit circle is therefore positive by continuity. Rotation invariance makes this minimum independent of $n$, and homogeneity proves <a href="#eq:cone-algebra" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cone-algebra">[eq:cone-algebra]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Cancellation from the finest level upward

<a id="eq:prices-scalar-defects"></a>
Let $\mathcal C$ be the indexed finite family consisting of $c_{n,+},c_{n,-}$ for every $n\in\mathcal N$; repeated functionals, if any, retain their indices. For $c\in\mathcal C$ write $$\nu^c=c(h)=c(\nabla g(x))\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,
 \qquad c_s=c(h_s)=\int\phi_s\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\nu^c.$$ For $s\in V_l$, $1\le l\le m$, define $$d_s^c=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\frac12\left(
       \sum_{t\in V_{l+1}}\lambda_{st}|c_t|-|c_s|\right),
       & l<m,\\[6pt]
 \displaystyle\frac12\left(
       \int\phi_s(x)|c(\nabla g(x))|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-|c_s|\right),
       & l=m.
 \end{cases}
 \label{eq:prices-scalar-defects}$$ These numbers are nonnegative by refinement and the triangle inequality. They measure the equal positive and negative masses that can be removed at $s$. At level $m$ the definition uses the positive and negative parts of the density itself. In particular, $c_s=0$ need not imply $d_s^c=0$.

We will realize this numerical cancellation by measures supported in the corresponding tent. The elementary operation is proportional removal; it does not require the two measures being paired to be mutually singular.

<a id="stmt-lem-prices-remove"></a>
<div id="lem:prices-remove" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Removing equal masses). *Let $U,V$ be finite nonnegative measures, of masses $P,N$. There are nonnegative submeasures $\mu_+\le U$, $\mu_-\le V$, both of mass $d=\min(P,N)$, such that $$U-V=\rho+\mu_+-\mu_-,\qquad
 \rho(\mathbb R^2)=P-N,\qquad |\rho|(\mathbb R^2)=|P-N|.$$ The residual $\rho$ has constant sign. If $U,V$ are absolutely continuous and supported in a common closed set, so are all the constructed measures.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prices-remove"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prices-remove</summary>

*Proof.* When $P>0$ set $\mu_+=(d/P)U$; when $P=0$ set $\mu_+=0$. Define $\mu_-$ in the same way from $N,V$, and set $\rho=(U-\mu_+)-(V-\mu_-)$. If $P\ge N$ and $P>0$, then $\mu_-=V$ and $\rho=(1-N/P)U\ge0$. The case $N\ge P$ is symmetric. If $P=N=0$, every measure is zero. These formulas prove the mass, sign and support assertions, including all zero cases. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-prices-scalar-pairing"></a>
<div id="lem:prices-scalar-pairing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:prices-scalar-masses"></a>
<a id="eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction"></a>
<a id="eq:prices-pair-properties"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Exact scalar pairing). *For every $c\in\mathcal C$ there are measures $\rho_s^c,\mu_{s,+}^c,\mu_{s,-}^c$ at all vertices $s\in V_l$, $1\le l\le m$, supported in $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi_s$ and absolutely continuous, with $\rho_s^c$ of constant sign and $\mu_{s,\pm}^c$ nonnegative, such that $$\label{eq:prices-scalar-masses}
 \rho_s^c(\mathbb R^2)=c_s,\qquad |\rho_s^c|(\mathbb R^2)=|c_s|,
 \qquad
 \mu_{s,+}^c(\mathbb R^2)=\mu_{s,-}^c(\mathbb R^2)=d_s^c.$$ Put $\beta_s^c=\mu_{s,+}^c-\mu_{s,-}^c$. For every $q\in V_k$, $1\le k\le m$, the following is an identity of measures on the whole plane: $$\label{eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction}
 \phi_q\nu^c=\rho_q^c+
       \sum_{l=k}^m\sum_{s\in V_l}\lambda_{qs}\beta_s^c.$$ Only finitely many terms in this identity are nonzero, and $$\label{eq:prices-pair-properties}
 \beta_s^c(\mathbb R^2)=0,\qquad
 |\beta_s^c|(\mathbb R^2)\le2d_s^c,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\beta_s^c\subseteq\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi_s
       \subseteq\overline B(s,\sqrt2 r_l).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-prices-scalar-pairing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-prices-scalar-pairing</summary>

<a id="eq:prices-terminal-identity"></a>
*Proof.* Fix $c$ and first take $s\in V_m$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:prices-remove" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prices-remove">7</a> to the actual terminal measures $$U_s=\phi_s(\nu^c)_+,
 \qquad V_s=\phi_s(\nu^c)_-.$$ Their masses have difference $c_s$ and sum $\int\phi_s\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}|\nu^c|$. Hence their smaller mass is exactly $d_s^c$, and removal gives <a href="#eq:prices-scalar-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-scalar-masses">[eq:prices-scalar-masses]</a> and $$\label{eq:prices-terminal-identity}
 \phi_s\nu^c=\rho_s^c+\beta_s^c.$$ If the positive and negative terminal masses agree, the residual is zero while both paired measures keep that common mass.

<a id="eq:prices-residual-recursion"></a>
Suppose that all residuals at level $l+1$ have been constructed. At $s\in V_l$ combine their positive and negative parts separately: $$U_s=\sum_{t\in V_{l+1}}\lambda_{st}(\rho_t^c)_+,
 \qquad
 V_s=\sum_{t\in V_{l+1}}\lambda_{st}(\rho_t^c)_-.$$ The residuals have constant sign, so the masses $P_s,N_s$ of these two measures obey $$P_s-N_s=\sum_t\lambda_{st}c_t=c_s,
 \qquad
 P_s+N_s=\sum_t\lambda_{st}|c_t|.$$ Thus $\min(P_s,N_s)=d_s^c$. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:prices-remove" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:prices-remove">7</a> again constructs the measures in <a href="#eq:prices-scalar-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-scalar-masses">[eq:prices-scalar-masses]</a> and proves $$\label{eq:prices-residual-recursion}
 \sum_{t\in V_{l+1}}\lambda_{st}\rho_t^c
      =\rho_s^c+\beta_s^c.$$ Absolute continuity is preserved by finite sums and scalar multiplication. Support nesting in <a href="#eq:prices-support-nesting" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-support-nesting">[eq:prices-support-nesting]</a> keeps all these measures in $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi_s$. The construction proceeds through finitely many levels. At each level only finitely many measures can be nonzero, since the terminal density has compact support and each vertex has finitely many parents.

It remains to verify that no density has been lost during removal. At level $m$, <a href="#eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction">[eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction]</a> is <a href="#eq:prices-terminal-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-terminal-identity">[eq:prices-terminal-identity]</a>. If the identity holds for all children of $q\in V_k$, refinement yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \phi_q\nu^c
 &=\sum_{t\in V_{k+1}}\lambda_{qt}\phi_t\nu^c\\
 &=\sum_t\lambda_{qt}\rho_t^c
   +\sum_{l=k+1}^m\sum_{s\in V_l}
        \left(\sum_t\lambda_{qt}\lambda_{ts}\right)\beta_s^c\\
 &=\rho_q^c+\beta_q^c
   +\sum_{l=k+1}^m\sum_{s\in V_l}\lambda_{qs}\beta_s^c.
\end{aligned}$$ The last line uses <a href="#eq:prices-residual-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-residual-recursion">[eq:prices-residual-recursion]</a> and coefficient composition in <a href="#eq:hat-refinement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hat-refinement">[eq:hat-refinement]</a>. It is the required identity at $q$. All rearrangements are finite for a fixed root because branching and depth are finite. Finally, equal masses in <a href="#eq:prices-scalar-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-scalar-masses">[eq:prices-scalar-masses]</a> give zero mass and variation at most $2d_s^c$ for each difference; the tent support gives the last assertion in <a href="#eq:prices-pair-properties" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-pair-properties">[eq:prices-pair-properties]</a>. ◻

</details>

The measures are constructed once at each vertex. A descendant’s pair may appear in several coarse identities, with its specified coefficient; these identities do not assert a disjoint allocation.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### The equivalent path form.

The price estimate below uses the vertex identity. The following equivalent form expresses shared-descendant coefficients as sums of path weights, using the same cancelling pieces. Section <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">4</a> will construct a separate positive measure on vector paths. Fix $q\in V_k$ with $1\le k\le m$, and let $\xi$ range over all positive-weight paths $\xi=(q_k,\ldots,q_l)$ with $q_k=q$ and $k\le l\le m$, including the trivial path $(q)$. Set $$w_\xi=\prod_{j=k}^{l-1}\lambda_{q_jq_{j+1}},
 \qquad \mu_{\xi,\pm}^c=w_\xi\mu_{q_l,\pm}^c,
 \qquad w_{(q)}=1.$$ Composition gives $\sum_{\xi:q_l=s}w_\xi=\lambda_{qs}$, and therefore <a id="eq:prices-scalar-pairing"></a>$$\label{eq:prices-scalar-pairing}
 \phi_q c(h)=\rho_q^c+
       \sum_\xi(\mu_{\xi,+}^c-\mu_{\xi,-}^c).$$ Each path pair has mass $w_\xi d_s^c$ in each sign and support in $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi_s$, where $s=q_l$. This recovers the path identity without a separate allocation along every path.

<a id="eq:prices-scalar-defect-budget"></a>
The cancellation amounts have an exact total budget. Summing <a href="#eq:prices-scalar-defects" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-scalar-defects">[eq:prices-scalar-defects]</a> over a level and using the column normalization in <a href="#eq:hat-refinement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hat-refinement">[eq:hat-refinement]</a> cancels intermediate absolute masses. The terminal partition of unity gives $$\sum_{l=1}^m\sum_{s\in V_l}d_s^c
 =\frac12\left(
       \int|c(\nabla g)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-
       \sum_{s\in V_1}|c_s|\right)
 \le\frac12\|c\|\,V.
 \label{eq:prices-scalar-defect-budget}$$ Here $\|c\|$ denotes the Euclidean operator norm, equal to $2/\sqrt3$ for every $c\in\mathcal C$ by <a href="#eq:prices-scalar-coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-scalar-coordinates">[eq:prices-scalar-coordinates]</a>. When $m=1$, the same formula is just the sum of the terminal defects. Zero masses introduce no exception to either the reconstruction or this telescoping identity.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### One price at every observation scale

For $Q=Q_p$ at level $k$, put $$\mathcal R(Q)=\{q\in V_k:|q-p|_\infty\le20r_k\},
 \qquad |z|_\infty=\max\{|z_1|,|z_2|\}.$$ A pair created at level $l\ge k$ has support of radius at most $\sqrt2r_l$. At observation scale $r_k$, the local estimate therefore charges it the relative factor $(r_l/r_k)^\alpha=2^{-\alpha(l-k)}$. We sum these charges over all the scalar coordinates so that the resulting price is independent of the label chosen later.

The scaled radius sets from Lemma <a href="#lem:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local">3</a> are $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal I_k(x)
   &=\{r\in[r_k,2r_k]:u_r(x)\ge u_t(x)
             \text{ for every }t>0\text{ with }|t-r|\le r_k/4\},\\
 \mathcal J_k(x)
   &=\{r\in[r_k,2r_k]:u_r(x)=\max_{r_k\le t\le2r_k}u_t(x)\}
       \cup\{r_k,2r_k\}.
\end{aligned}$$ Also define $$v_k(x)=\sup_{0<t,t'\le4r_k}|u_t(x)-u_{t'}(x)|.$$ An empty supremum of nonnegative quantities is zero, as before.

<a id="stmt-prop-prices"></a>
<div id="prop:prices" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:price-budget"></a>
<a id="eq:prices"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Common discounted prices). *For $p\in V_k$, $1\le k\le m$, define <a id="eq:prices-definition"></a>$$D_{Q_p}=\sum_{c\in\mathcal C}
       \sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q_p)}
       \sum_{l=k}^m2^{-\alpha(l-k)}
       \sum_{s\in V_l}\lambda_{qs}d_s^c,
 \qquad \alpha=\frac1{100}.
 \label{eq:prices-definition}$$ These nonnegative prices satisfy $$\sum_{k=1}^m\sum_{p\in V_k}D_{Q_p}
 \le\frac{41^2}{2(1-2^{-\alpha})}
           \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}\|c(\nabla g)\|_{L^1}
 \le C V.
 \label{eq:price-budget}$$ For every such cell $Q$ and every $n\in\mathcal N$, $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int_Q\sup_{t\in\mathcal I_k(x)}
       \sum_{\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}}
       (-e_\varepsilon(n)\cdot\nabla u_t(x))_+\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
   \le C\left(D_Q+
       \sum_{\substack{q\in\mathcal R(Q)\\(q,n)\text{ bad}}}a_q\right),
 \\
 &\int_Q\left[\sup_{t\in\mathcal J_k(x)}|\nabla u_t(x)|
                  +r_k^{-1}v_k(x)\right]\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
   \le C\left(D_Q+\sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q)}a_q\right).
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:prices}$$ All constants are absolute: the direction palette, support radius factor and cone aperture are fixed, and there is no dependence on $m$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-prices"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-prices</summary>

*Proof.* We first sum the prices. At a fixed level each vertex $q$ belongs to exactly $41^2$ neighborhoods $\mathcal R(Q_p)$, since each of the two integer coordinate differences ranges from $-20$ to $20$. Nonnegativity permits reordering, and the column sums give $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{k=1}^m\sum_{p\in V_k}D_{Q_p}
 &=41^2\sum_{c\in\mathcal C}\sum_{l=1}^m\sum_{s\in V_l}d_s^c
       \sum_{k=1}^l2^{-\alpha(l-k)}
          \sum_{q\in V_k}\lambda_{qs}\\
 &\le\frac{41^2}{1-2^{-\alpha}}
       \sum_{c\in\mathcal C}\sum_{l=1}^m\sum_{s\in V_l}d_s^c.
\end{aligned}$$ Applying the exact telescoping identity <a href="#eq:prices-scalar-defect-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-scalar-defect-budget">[eq:prices-scalar-defect-budget]</a> proves <a href="#eq:price-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:price-budget">[eq:price-budget]</a>. In particular, one may take $C=41^2|\mathcal C|/[\sqrt3(1-2^{-\alpha})]$ in its final inequality.

<a id="eq:prices-vector-decomposition"></a>
We next construct the decomposition needed by the local estimates. Fix $Q=Q_p$ at level $k$, a label $n$, and write $\Omega_Q=\{y:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(y,Q)<10r_k\}$. If a level-$k$ tent $\phi_q$ is positive at $y\in\Omega_Q$, then $|q-y|_\infty<r_k$ and $|y-p|_\infty<11r_k$, so $q\in\mathcal R(Q)$. Hence <a id="eq:prices-local-partition"></a>$$h=\sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q)}\phi_qh
 \quad\text{on }\Omega_Q.
 \label{eq:prices-local-partition}$$ Apply <a href="#eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction">[eq:prices-vertex-reconstruction]</a> with the two coordinates $c_{n,\varepsilon}$ and multiply by $e_\varepsilon(n)$. This gives the measure identity $$\label{eq:prices-vector-decomposition}
 h=\sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q)}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}}
            e_\varepsilon(n)\rho_q^{c_{n,\varepsilon}}
   +\sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q)}\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}}
        \sum_{l=k}^m\sum_{s\in V_l}
            e_\varepsilon(n)\lambda_{qs}\beta_s^{c_{n,\varepsilon}}
 \quad\text{on }\Omega_Q.$$ The components here are measures on the whole plane. We retain them on their full supports; only their sum is being compared with $h$ on $\Omega_Q$.

For a good root $(q,n)$, both $c_{n,\varepsilon}(h_q)$ are nonnegative. The constant-sign and mass assertions in <a href="#eq:prices-scalar-masses" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-scalar-masses">[eq:prices-scalar-masses]</a> therefore imply $\rho_q^{c_{n,\varepsilon}}\ge0$, including the zero case. These residuals are the positive measures $\sigma_i$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local">3</a>, in the unit directions $e_+(n),e_-(n)$. Each of these directions has dot product at least $1/2$ with either test direction $e_+(n)$ or $e_-(n)$. For a bad root, put the two signed residual terms among the unrestricted errors, with assigned relative size one. Their total variation is at most $$\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}}
       |\rho_q^{c_{n,\varepsilon}}|(\mathbb R^2)
 =\sum_{\varepsilon\in\{+,-\}}|c_{n,\varepsilon}(h_q)|
 \le2a_q.$$ Thus no good-root mass enters the error price for the directional estimate.

<a id="eq:prices-directional-decomposition-cost"></a>
For each term in the second sum of <a href="#eq:prices-vector-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-vector-decomposition">[eq:prices-vector-decomposition]</a>, its total vector mass is zero, its variation is at most $2\lambda_{qs}d_s^{c_{n,\varepsilon}}$, and its support lies in $\overline B(s,\sqrt2r_l)$. Assign it the relative size $s_d=r_l/r_k=2^{k-l}$. When $l>k$ this gives exactly the cancellation and support conditions required for a small error. When $l=k$ it gives an allowed size-one error. The physical sum of discounted variations is consequently bounded by $$\sum_d s_d^\alpha|\beta_d|(\mathbb R^2)
 \le2D_Q+
       2\sum_{\substack{q\in\mathcal R(Q)\\(q,n)\text{ bad}}}a_q.
 \label{eq:prices-directional-decomposition-cost}$$ Here $\beta_d$ denotes the vector error pieces just specified. The sum is finite and every component is absolutely continuous. The terminal pairs are included, even at roots with zero vector mass; there is no terminal remainder.

To apply Lemma <a href="#lem:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local">3</a>, let $r=r_k$, $T(X)=p+rX$ and $\widetilde g=g\circ T$. Its gradient measure and disk averages satisfy <a id="eq:prices-scaling"></a>$$\label{eq:prices-scaling}
 \widetilde h=r^{-1}(T^{-1})_\#h,
 \qquad A_t\widetilde g(X)=u_{rt}(T(X)),
 \qquad \nabla_XA_t\widetilde g(X)=r\nabla u_{rt}(T(X)).$$ Indeed $\nabla_X\widetilde g=r\nabla g\circ T$ and $\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X=r^{-2}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x$. Transform every component measure by the same $r^{-1}(T^{-1})_\#$ rule. Its mass and variation are multiplied by $r^{-1}$; zero total vector mass and positivity are preserved. A pair at level $l$ is now supported in a ball of radius $\sqrt2r_l/r_k$. Taking $C_s=2$ places this closed support inside an open ball of radius $C_s r_l/r_k$. The transformed identity holds on the $10$-neighborhood of $[0,1)^2$.

The radius sets for $\widetilde g$ are the images of $\mathcal I_k,\mathcal J_k$ under division by $r$, including the comparison with all positive radii in $\mathcal I_k$ and both forced endpoints in $\mathcal J_k$. Furthermore, the integral over the unit cell of any of the gradient suprema in Lemma <a href="#lem:local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:local">3</a> is $r^{-1}$ times the corresponding physical integral over $Q$. Apply <a href="#eq:local-direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-direction">[eq:local-direction]</a> with cone margin $\kappa=1/2$ to each of the two test directions, and multiply by $r$. The supremum of the sum of the two nonnegative terms is at most the sum of their suprema. Together with <a href="#eq:prices-directional-decomposition-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-directional-decomposition-cost">[eq:prices-directional-decomposition-cost]</a>, this proves the first estimate in <a href="#eq:prices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices">[eq:prices]</a>.

For the second estimate in <a href="#eq:prices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices">[eq:prices]</a>, fix any one label and use the same decomposition, now placing *all* root residuals among the size-one errors. The positive part $S$ is zero, and the physical discounted error mass is at most $2D_Q+2\sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q)}a_q$. The size estimate <a href="#eq:local-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-size">[eq:local-size]</a>, after the same transformation, bounds the physical gradient integral by an absolute multiple of this quantity. For the value estimate the change of variables instead gives <a id="eq:prices-value-scaling"></a>$$\label{eq:prices-value-scaling}
 \int_{[0,1)^2}\sup_{0<t,t'\le4}
       |A_t\widetilde g(X)-A_{t'}\widetilde g(X)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}X
   =r^{-2}\int_Qv_k(x)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$ Its right side in <a href="#eq:local-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-value">[eq:local-value]</a> is bounded by $Cr^{-1}(D_Q+\sum_q a_q)$. Multiplying by $r$ therefore bounds $r_k^{-1}\int_Qv_k$, exactly as asserted. Adding this to the size bound completes the proof. ◻

</details>

The prices now pay for every scalar cancellation and leave only nearby vector masses $a_q$ in the local bounds. Controlling those remaining masses requires a choice of directions across scales and positions. The next section constructs a positive measure on complete refinement paths, recording a birth level, so that the mass of paths through a vertex $q$ whose birth has already occurred is exactly $a_q$. The scalar measures above supply neither this path measure nor the spatial estimates for the direction law; their role is the reconstruction and discounted budget proved in this section.

<a id="sec:labels"></a>
### Directions that change slowly in scale and position

The common prices in Proposition <a href="#prop:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prices">9</a> pay for scalar cancellation. Its remaining terms are nearby vector masses $a_q$. We now choose directions so that these masses pay only a summable amount for changes of direction. A maximal consecutive interval of levels carrying the same direction will be called a *run*. Ending a run produces endpoint terms in the local estimates, so the first cost is the probability of a change between successive levels.

There is also a spatial cost. We will average signed directional estimates with weights depending on $x$ and then integrate by parts. For any finite Lipschitz probability row $(\omega_j(x))_j$, normalization gives $$\sum_j\nabla\omega_j=0,
 \qquad
 \sum_j U_j\nabla\omega_j
   =\sum_j(U_j-U_*)\nabla\omega_j$$ almost everywhere, for arbitrary scalar values $U_j$ and a common value $U_*$. Thus the cost of differentiating a row is controlled by the differences $U_j-U_*$, rather than the sizes of the values themselves. In Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">5</a>, the differences will be bounded by sums of the small-radius oscillations $v_l$ defined in Section <a href="#sec:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prices">3</a>. Their integral over a level-$l$ cell costs $r_l$ times its price and root masses, by <a href="#eq:prices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices">[eq:prices]</a>. A row built from level-$k$ tents has derivatives of size $r_k^{-1}$. The required joint bound therefore carries the ratio $r_l/r_k=2^{k-l}$, for $k\le l$. This explains both the row-derivative cost and its weight in the packing estimate below.

Retain $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$, $h=\nabla g\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x$, $V=|h|(\mathbb R^2)$, and the levels $1,\ldots,m$, with $m\ge1$. We use the hats, vector masses $h_q=a_q\theta_q$, and refinement coefficients from Section <a href="#sec:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prices">3</a>, defined at every integer level. The coarser levels will be needed to compare nearby directions. The palette $\mathcal N$ is the fixed $\tau/20$-net of the unit circle, where $\tau=1/100$. Recall that $(q,n)$ is good when $\theta_q$ makes an angle at most $\pi/6$ with $n$. All vertex and palette choices below use fixed tie rules, independently of the observation point $x$. Directions at zero vector masses retain the prescribed unit-vector convention.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Directions in neighboring cells

At each vertex we first look for a nearby direction carrying maximal mass. This choice prevents a small neighboring mass from imposing an unpaid change of direction. For $z\in V_k$, choose a vertex $w(z)\in V_k$ maximizing $a_w$ among $|w-z|_{\infty}\le300r_k$, and set $$\Theta_z=\theta_{w(z)}.$$ Choose $n_z\in\mathcal N$ with $|n_z-\Theta_z|\le\tau/20$. The level is part of the vertex label in this notation. Define $$E_k(q)=
 \max_{\substack{z\in V_k\\|z-q|_{\infty}\le200r_k}}
       |\Theta_z-\theta_q|^2,
 \qquad q\in V_k.$$ In particular, $0\le E_k(q)\le4$. The quantity $E_k(q)$ measures how far the nearby chosen directions can depart from its own direction. Put $A_j=\sum_{p\in V_j}a_p$ for every integer $j$. By <a href="#eq:prices-vector-mass-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices-vector-mass-budget">[eq:prices-vector-mass-budget]</a>, these total vector masses satisfy $0\le A_j\le A_{j+1}\le V$. Their increase pays the weighted sum of the disagreements.

<a id="stmt-lem-labels-neighbors"></a>
<div id="lem:labels-neighbors" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:neighbor-budget"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Neighboring direction budget). *There is an absolute constant $C$ such that $$\sum_{k=1}^m\sum_{q\in V_k}a_q E_k(q)\le C V.
 \label{eq:neighbor-budget}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-labels-neighbors"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-labels-neighbors</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $k$ and $q$ with $a_q>0$, choose a maximizing $z$ in the definition of $E_k(q)$, and put $w=w(z)$. Since $q$ is among the candidates for $w(z)$, we have $$a_w\ge a_q,
 \qquad |w-q|_{\infty}\le500r_k.$$ Take $L=12$, and choose a nearest vertex $p\in V_{k-L}$ to $q$ in each coordinate. Then $$|q-p|_{\infty}\le\tfrac12r_{k-L},
 \qquad
 |w-p|_{\infty}
 \le\bigl(\tfrac12+500\,2^{-12}\bigr)r_{k-L}
 <\tfrac58r_{k-L}.$$ Each coordinate factor of $\phi_p(q)$ is at least $1/2$, and each coordinate factor of $\phi_p(w)$ is greater than $3/8$. Hence $\lambda_{pq}\ge1/4$ and $\lambda_{pw}\ge9/64$. For this coarse vertex write $$H_{p,k}=\sum_{s\in V_k}\lambda_{ps}a_s-a_p.$$ The vector refinement identity implies, also when $a_p=0$, <a id="eq:labels-neighbor-defect"></a>$$H_{p,k}
 =\sum_{s\in V_k}\lambda_{ps}a_s
                  (1-\theta_p\cdot\theta_s)
 =\frac12\sum_{s\in V_k}\lambda_{ps}a_s
                  |\theta_s-\theta_p|^2.
 \label{eq:labels-neighbor-defect}$$ Here $\theta_p\cdot h_p=a_p$ is true even at zero, for the prescribed unit direction at zero. The triangle inequality for squared distances, the two lower bounds on the interpolation coefficients, and $a_w\ge a_q$ therefore give $$a_q E_k(q)
 \le2a_q\bigl(|\theta_q-\theta_p|^2
                    +|\theta_w-\theta_p|^2\bigr)
 \le C H_{p,k}.$$ For fixed $k$, each coarse vertex is chosen by at most a fixed number of fine vertices, depending only on $L$. Vertices with $a_q=0$ contribute nothing. The column sums of the refinement coefficients now give $$\sum_{q\in V_k}a_q E_k(q)
 \le C\sum_{p\in V_{k-L}}H_{p,k}
 =C(A_k-A_{k-L}).$$ Finally, $$\sum_{k=1}^m(A_k-A_{k-L})
 =\sum_{k=1}^m\sum_{j=k-L+1}^k(A_j-A_{j-1})
 \le L V,$$ because every nonnegative increment appears at most $L$ times. This proves <a href="#eq:neighbor-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:neighbor-budget">[eq:neighbor-budget]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The finite-state label law

The neighboring budget does not prescribe a direction at every point. We interpolate the fixed vertex choices by the tents, retaining the previous direction unless it is appreciably far from the sampled one. The interval on which the switching probability vanishes will keep small direction fluctuations from creating repeated run endpoints.

For each $x\in\mathbb R^2$ we define a probability law $\mathbb P_x$ on $\mathcal N^m$. At the first step, sample $z\in V_1$ with probabilities $\phi_z(x)$ and set $n_1=n_z$. At a step $k\ge2$, condition on the old label $n=n_{k-1}$, sample $z\in V_k$ with probabilities $\phi_z(x)$, and switch to $n_z$ with probability <a id="eq:labels-switch-function"></a>$$p_k(n,z)=P(|n-\Theta_z|),
 \qquad
 P(d)=\min\{1,\tau^{-2}(d-\tau)_+^2\}.
 \label{eq:labels-switch-function}$$ Otherwise retain $n$. These samples and switch trials are made independently of the past conditional on the old label. If $p_k(n,z)>0$, then $n_z\ne n$: the opposite equality would imply $|n-\Theta_z|\le\tau/20<\tau$.

<a id="eq:labels-initial-row"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-transition-row"></a>
For a label $n$, let $\delta_n$ denote the probability vector concentrated at $n$. The initial row and subsequent transition rows are, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 \pi_1(x)
 &=\sum_{z\in V_1}\phi_z(x)\delta_{n_z},
 \label{eq:labels-initial-row}\\
 \pi_k(x;n,\cdot)
 &=\delta_n+\sum_{z\in V_k}\phi_z(x)p_k(n,z)
                              (\delta_{n_z}-\delta_n),\quad k\ge2.
 \label{eq:labels-transition-row}
\end{aligned}$$ Every entry is nonnegative and every row sums to one, as is also clear from the sampling rule. The rows are continuous, piecewise bilinear interpolations of bounded vectors, with Lipschitz constant at most $C/r_k$. These formulas, together with the initial row, specify the probability of each label sequence as a product of entries; this is the law $\mathbb P_x$. We denote its expectation by $\mathbb E_x$ and the marginal law of $n_k$ by $\zeta_k(x)$. When convenient, the initial row is viewed as a transition from a single auxiliary state $*$, with $\zeta_0(x)=\delta_*$ for every $x$.

The row switch probability for a prescribed old label is $$\rho_k(x\mid n)=\sum_{z\in V_k}\phi_z(x)p_k(n,z),\qquad k\ge2.$$ Since a trial counted by this expression changes the label, define <a id="eq:labels-switch-rates"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 R_k(x)&=\sum_{n\in\mathcal N}\zeta_{k-1}(x;n)\rho_k(x\mid n)
       =\mathbb P_x(n_k\ne n_{k-1}),\quad 2\le k\le m,\\
 R_1(x)&=R_{m+1}(x)=1.
 \end{aligned}
 \label{eq:labels-switch-rates}$$ The two sentinel values count the beginning and end of a history; they apply also when $m=1$. The functions $R_k$ are continuous, since the rows are Lipschitz and each marginal is obtained by finitely many matrix products.

<a id="eq:labels-derivative-costs"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-uniform-costs"></a>
Let $G$ be the union of the grid lines at levels $1,\ldots,m$. It is a fixed Lebesgue-null set. Off $G$ all the tent rows are differentiable. Define the scaled expected row-derivative costs there by $$\begin{split}
 T_k(x)&=r_k\sum_{n\in\mathcal N}\zeta_{k-1}(x;n)
          \sum_{n'\in\mathcal N}
             |\nabla_x\pi_k(x;n,n')|,\quad 2\le k\le m,\\
 T_1(x)&=r_1\sum_{n'\in\mathcal N}
                         |\nabla_x\pi_1(x;n')|.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:labels-derivative-costs}$$ Set $T_k=0$ on $G$. These are measurable versions of the costs, and all later bounds on their suprema are essential-supremum bounds. The derivative in this definition acts on one transition row; it is not the derivative of the marginal $\zeta_k$. Off $G$, at most four level-$k$ tents have a nonzero gradient, and $|\nabla\phi_z|\le\sqrt2/r_k$. It follows from the row formulas that $$0\le R_k\le1,
 \qquad 0\le T_k\le C.
 \label{eq:labels-uniform-costs}$$ The norm inside the inner sum in <a href="#eq:labels-derivative-costs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-derivative-costs">[eq:labels-derivative-costs]</a> is the Euclidean norm of the spatial gradient, whereas norms of probability vectors below are their $\ell^1$ norms.

<a id="stmt-lem-good-label"></a>
<div id="lem:good-label" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Good labels near a controlled vertex). *Set $c_*=(\tau/10)^2$. Suppose $q\in V_k$, $E_k(q)\le c_*$, and $|x-q|_{\infty}\le50r_k$. Then $(q,n_k)$ is good with $\mathbb P_x$-probability one.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-good-label"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-good-label</summary>

*Proof.* Every vertex $z$ with $\phi_z(x)>0$ satisfies $|z-q|_{\infty}<51r_k<200r_k$, and hence $|\Theta_z-\theta_q|\le\tau/10$. An initial or switched label therefore has distance at most $\tau/10+\tau/20$ from $\theta_q$. If a label $n$ is retained after sampling $z$ with positive probability, then $P(|n-\Theta_z|)<1$, so $|n-\Theta_z|<2\tau$. Its distance from $\theta_q$ is consequently less than $21\tau/10$. Both bounds are less than $2\sin(\pi/12)$, since $\tau=1/100$. The angle between the resulting label and $\theta_q$ is thus less than $\pi/6$, as required by the definition of a good pair. ◻

</details>

This lemma pays the bad-root terms in the first line of <a href="#eq:prices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prices">[eq:prices]</a>. If $q\in\mathcal R(Q_p)$ at level $k$, then $|x-q|_\infty\le21r_k$ throughout $Q_p$. Whenever $(q,n)$ is bad and $E_k(q)\le c_*$, the marginal probability of $n_k=n$ therefore vanishes on that whole cell. In the remaining case, $a_q\le c_*^{-1}a_qE_k(q)$, whose sum is bounded by <a href="#eq:neighbor-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:neighbor-budget">[eq:neighbor-budget]</a>, with the fixed cell and palette multiplicities. It remains to pay root masses at the endpoints of runs and in the terms where spatial weights are differentiated.

For $q\in V_l$ define the closed square $$D(q)=\{x\in\mathbb R^2:|x-q|_\infty\le25r_l\}.$$ It contains every level-$l$ pricing cell whose root neighborhood contains $q$: indeed $q\in\mathcal R(Q_p)$ and $x\in Q_p$ give $|x-q|_\infty\le21r_l$. We can now state the estimate that pays both types of cost on those whole cells.

<a id="stmt-prop-label-packing"></a>
<div id="prop:label-packing" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:label-packing"></a>
**Proposition 12** (Packing of switch and derivative costs). *For every real $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ and every $m\ge1$, the quantities constructed above satisfy $$\sum_{l=1}^m\sum_{q\in V_l}a_q
 \left[
   \sup_{x\in D(q)}\bigl(R_l(x)+R_{l+1}(x)\bigr)
   +\sum_{k=1}^l2^{k-l}
       \operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{x\in D(q)}T_k(x)
 \right]
 \le C V.
 \label{eq:label-packing}$$ The constant is absolute and independent of $m$.*

</div>

We prove this proposition in three steps. First a positive measure on refinement paths will realize each $a_q$ exactly. Along each path, vector additivity will bound switches at its terminal position. Finally comparison of the laws at nearby points will turn that fixed-position estimate into the suprema over $D(q)$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Particles and intrinsic direction changes

<a id="eq:labels-child-capacity"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-node-column-sums"></a>
The scalar decomposition in Section <a href="#sec:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prices">3</a> is an identity of signed measures with cancelling pairs. Here we need a different object: a positive measure on complete refinement paths, with exact occupancy $a_q$ after birth. It must also retain the path before birth, because the label law has been evolving since level one. This path measure will not depend on $x$. We will combine it with $\mathbb P_x$ by evaluating that law at a path’s terminal vertex. A path node at level $k$ is $\xi=(q_1,\ldots,q_k)$, where $q_j\in V_j$ and each consecutive refinement coefficient is positive. Its path weight and mass are $$b_\xi=\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\lambda_{q_jq_{j+1}},
 \qquad M_\xi=b_\xi a_{q_k}.$$ The empty product at a root is one. The associated vector is $b_\xi h_{q_k}$. Vectors add from the children to their parent, and therefore $$M_\xi\le\sum_{\eta\text{ child of }\xi}M_\eta.
 \label{eq:labels-child-capacity}$$ Only a finite forest is needed. Choose all level-one roots whose closed tent supports meet $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h$, and retain all their positive-weight paths through level $m$. There are finitely many roots and each node has finitely many children. If $a_q>0$, the support of $\phi_q$ meets $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h$. Every ancestor tent then meets $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h$ by support nesting, so every positive-weight path to $q$ starts among the chosen roots. In particular no contribution to a nonzero mass is lost by this finite restriction. If $h=0$, the forest may be empty. Consequently, at each level $k$, $$\sum_{\xi:\,q_k=q}b_\xi=1\quad\text{if }a_q>0,
 \qquad
 \sum_{|\xi|=k}M_\xi=A_k.
 \label{eq:labels-node-column-sums}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-labels-particles"></a>
<div id="lem:labels-particles" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:labels-node-occupancy"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-vertex-occupancy"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-path-locality"></a>
**Lemma 13** (A finite particle measure). *There is a finite positive measure $\mu$ on pairs consisting of a terminal path $(q_1,\ldots,q_m)$ and a birth level $B\in\{1,\ldots,m\}$ such that, for every node $\xi$ at level $k$, $$\mu\{B\le k,\ (q_1,\ldots,q_k)=\xi\}=M_\xi.
 \label{eq:labels-node-occupancy}$$ In particular, $$\mu\{B\le k,\ q_k=q\}=a_q,
 \qquad \mu(\text{all particles})=A_m\le V.
 \label{eq:labels-vertex-occupancy}$$ Writing $y=q_m$ for the terminal position of a particle, its complete path obeys $$|y-q_s|_{\infty}<r_s\qquad(1\le s\le m).
 \label{eq:labels-path-locality}$$ The path is defined also at levels preceding its birth.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-labels-particles"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-labels-particles</summary>

*Proof.* At each root create mass $M_\xi$ with birth level one. Suppose the population already present at a level-$k$ node has mass $M_\xi$, and put $S_\xi=\sum_{\eta\text{ child}}M_\eta$. If $S_\xi>0$, send the fraction $M_\eta/S_\xi$ of each existing population type to the child $\eta$. The mass sent there is $M_\xi M_\eta/S_\xi\le M_\eta$ by <a href="#eq:labels-child-capacity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-child-capacity">[eq:labels-child-capacity]</a>. Create at that child additional mass $M_\eta-M_\xi M_\eta/S_\xi$ with birth level $k+1$. If $S_\xi=0$, all these masses are zero. This operation preserves existing particles and fills each child to exactly its prescribed mass. A new particle at a child ending in $q_{k+1}$ receives the entire prefix $(q_1,\ldots,q_k,q_{k+1})$ of that child, as well as its new birth level. Thus a particle born below a zero-mass ancestor still has a specified direction history at that ancestor. No particle mass is counted as occupying that ancestor before birth.

After the finite number of levels, the masses of the resulting terminal-path and birth-level types define a finite atomic measure $\mu$. All particles present at $\xi$ continue to terminal descendants of $\xi$, conserving their mass $M_\xi$. Conversely, any terminal particle with that prefix and $B\le k$ was already present at $\xi$. Particles born later may carry the same prefix, but the condition $B\le k$ excludes them. This proves <a href="#eq:labels-node-occupancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-node-occupancy">[eq:labels-node-occupancy]</a>. Summing over prefixes and using <a href="#eq:labels-node-column-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-node-column-sums">[eq:labels-node-column-sums]</a> gives <a href="#eq:labels-vertex-occupancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-vertex-occupancy">[eq:labels-vertex-occupancy]</a>. For a positive consecutive refinement coefficient, $|q_{j+1}-q_j|_{\infty}\le r_j/2$. Hence $$|q_m-q_s|_{\infty}
 \le\sum_{j=s}^{m-1}r_j/2
 =r_s(1-2^{-(m-s)})<r_s,$$ with the same strict upper bound when $s=m$. This proves the locality assertion. ◻

</details>

The particle measure now realizes every vector mass exactly at its level. It lets us prove a pathwise bound and then recover a sum over all cells by integration. We next mark the genuine direction changes along a path. The sum of the norms of descendant vectors may exceed the norm of their sum at the ancestor; this nonnegative difference will pay for the marks.

Mark every root of the path forest. Below a mark, mark the first nodes whose direction $\theta_{q_s}$ has distance greater than $\tau/8$ from the direction at that mark, and repeat the rule below each new mark. These intrinsic marks depend on the path forest and the vector masses; they do not depend on the label process at any point $x$.

<a id="stmt-lem-labels-switch-packing"></a>
<div id="lem:labels-switch-packing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:labels-mark-budget"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-particle-energy"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-particle-switches"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Intrinsic marks and expected switches). *The particle measure satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\xi\text{ intrinsically marked}}M_\xi&\le C V,
 \label{eq:labels-mark-budget}\\
 \int\sum_{s=B}^m E_s(q_s)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu&\le C V,
 \label{eq:labels-particle-energy}\\
 \int\sum_{s=B}^m R_s(y)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu&\le C V.
 \label{eq:labels-particle-switches}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-labels-switch-packing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-labels-switch-packing</summary>

*Proof.* For a marked nonterminal node $\xi$, let $\mathcal L_\xi$ consist of the first subsequent marks on each descending branch, supplemented by terminal nodes on branches with no later mark. This is a finite frontier. Writing $\vartheta_\xi=\theta_{q_k}$ at a level-$k$ node, vector additivity gives <a id="eq:labels-stopped-defect"></a>$$\sum_{\eta\in\mathcal L_\xi}M_\eta-M_\xi
 =\frac12\sum_{\eta\in\mathcal L_\xi}M_\eta
                   |\vartheta_\eta-\vartheta_\xi|^2
 \ge\frac{\tau^2}{128}
          \sum_{\substack{\eta\in\mathcal L_\xi\\\eta\text{ marked}}}
                   M_\eta.
 \label{eq:labels-stopped-defect}$$ For clarity, vector additivity on the frontier says $\sum_{\eta\in\mathcal L_\xi}M_\eta\vartheta_\eta
=M_\xi\vartheta_\xi$. Taking its scalar product with the unit vector $\vartheta_\xi$ proves the identity, even if $M_\xi=0$. For $m>1$, every marked nonterminal node other than a root occurs positively in its preceding marked frontier and negatively in its own summand. Every terminal node occurs in exactly one frontier. The sum of the left sides is therefore $A_m-A_1\le V$. Every nonroot mark occurs in exactly one frontier. Adding the root masses proves <a href="#eq:labels-mark-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-mark-budget">[eq:labels-mark-budget]</a>; if $m=1$, the assertion follows directly from the root mass bound.

By <a href="#eq:labels-node-occupancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-node-occupancy">[eq:labels-node-occupancy]</a>, integration of the number of intrinsic marks at levels $s\ge B$ is the left side of <a href="#eq:labels-mark-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-mark-budget">[eq:labels-mark-budget]</a>. Similarly, $$\int\sum_{s=B}^m E_s(q_s)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu
 =\sum_{s=1}^m\sum_{q\in V_s}a_q E_s(q),$$ so <a href="#eq:labels-particle-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-particle-energy">[eq:labels-particle-energy]</a> follows from <a href="#eq:neighbor-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:neighbor-budget">[eq:neighbor-budget]</a>.

For the switch estimate, fix a particle, including its path, birth level, and terminal position $y$. Run the finite-state law at this fixed point $y$. Let $X_1=1$, and for $s\ge2$ let $X_s=\mathbf 1_{\{n_s\ne n_{s-1}\}}$. Thus $\mathbb E_y X_s=R_s(y)$ for $1\le s\le m$. Among levels $B,\ldots,m$, call a level exceptional if it is intrinsically marked or if $E_s(q_s)>c_*$. Remove those levels and consider a consecutive interval of remaining levels. Throughout this interval, all $\theta_{q_s}$ are within $\tau/8$ of the same most recent intrinsic marked direction, even if that mark occurred before birth. Every sampled vertex at $y$ lies within $2r_s$ of $q_s$ by <a href="#eq:labels-path-locality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-path-locality">[eq:labels-path-locality]</a>, and hence its sampled direction is within $\tau/10$ of $\theta_{q_s}$. Any two sampled directions on the interval are therefore at distance at most $$2(\tau/8+\tau/10)=9\tau/20.$$ After one switch on the interval, the new label is within an additional $\tau/20$ of the direction sampled at that switch. Its distance to every subsequently sampled direction is at most $\tau/2<\tau$, so all later switch probabilities on this interval are zero. The same observation applies after the initial assignment, should it be counted on such an interval. There is consequently at most one counted switch on each remaining interval, regardless of the label entering it.

If there are $J$ exceptional levels, there are at most $J+1$ such intervals and at most $J$ switches at exceptional levels. Thus every realization obeys $$\sum_{s=B}^m X_s
 \le1+2\#\{s\ge B:s\text{ is intrinsically marked}\}
       +2c_*^{-1}\sum_{s=B}^m E_s(q_s).$$ Taking $\mathbb E_y$, then integrating over particles, proves <a href="#eq:labels-particle-switches" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-particle-switches">[eq:labels-particle-switches]</a> from the first two bounds and $\mu(\text{all particles})\le V$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### Comparison of label laws at nearby points

We have bounded the expected switches at the single point $y$ attached to each particle. This does not yet give Proposition <a href="#prop:label-packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:label-packing">12</a>: the local prices are integrated on cells, so their coefficients must be controlled uniformly there. We also still need the row-derivative cost. The same comparison of nearby rows supplies both missing bounds.

We compare the law at any point of $D(q_l)$ with the law at the terminal position of a particle. The comparison uses the complete particle path, including its levels before birth.

<a id="stmt-lem-labels-spatial-comparison"></a>
<div id="lem:labels-spatial-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:labels-spatial-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Spatial comparison). *Fix a particle, a level $1\le l\le m$, and $x\in D(q_l)$. Set $$c_s=R_s(y)+E_s(q_s),\qquad 1\le s\le m.$$ For $1\le k\le\min(l+1,m)$, $$R_k(x)+T_k(x)
 \le C\sum_{s=1}^k2^{s-k}c_s.
 \label{eq:labels-spatial-comparison}$$ The assertion for $R_k$ holds at every $x$ in the specified set, and the assertion for $T_k$ holds almost everywhere there. The constant is uniform over particles, $l$, and $x$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-labels-spatial-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-labels-spatial-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* By <a href="#eq:labels-path-locality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-path-locality">[eq:labels-path-locality]</a>, $|x-y|_{\infty}<26r_l$. At a step $s\le\min(l+1,m)$, any vertex $z$ whose tent is nonzero at $x$ or at $y$ satisfies $$|z-q_s|_{\infty}
 \le r_s+26r_l+r_s
 \le54r_s<200r_s.$$ Off $G$, a tent with nonzero gradient at $x$ also has positive value there, so the same bound covers every differentiated tent. At grid points the probability comparison still uses the exact tent values; no assertion about a classical row derivative is needed. Thus all the directions involved differ from $\theta_{q_s}$ by at most $\sqrt{E_s(q_s)}$.

<a id="eq:labels-row-rate"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-row-derivative"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-row-difference"></a>
Fix $s\ge2$ and an old label $n$. The function $d\mapsto\sqrt{P(d)}=\min\{1,\tau^{-1}(d-\tau)_+\}$ is $\tau^{-1}$-Lipschitz. For any two involved vertices $z,z'$, it follows that $$p_s(n,z)
 \le2p_s(n,z')+2\tau^{-2}|\Theta_z-\Theta_{z'}|^2
 \le2p_s(n,z')+8\tau^{-2}E_s(q_s).$$ Averaging $z'$ with the probabilities $\phi_{z'}(y)$ shows that the largest $p_s(n,z)$ among all involved vertices is bounded by <a id="eq:labels-row-rate-comparison"></a>$$C\bigl(\rho_s(y\mid n)+E_s(q_s)\bigr).
 \label{eq:labels-row-rate-comparison}$$ The row formula <a href="#eq:labels-transition-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-transition-row">[eq:labels-transition-row]</a> and the tent derivative bounds then give $$\begin{aligned}
 \rho_s(x\mid n)
 &\le C\bigl(\rho_s(y\mid n)+E_s(q_s)\bigr),
 \label{eq:labels-row-rate}\\
 r_s\sum_{n'\in\mathcal N}|\nabla_x\pi_s(x;n,n')|
 &\le C\bigl(\rho_s(y\mid n)+E_s(q_s)\bigr)
 \label{eq:labels-row-derivative}
\end{aligned}$$ almost everywhere in the second assertion. Moreover, the union of the sets of tents nonzero at $x$ and $y$ has uniformly bounded cardinality, and each tent is $C/r_s$-Lipschitz. Subtracting the two rows therefore yields the pointwise bound $$\|\pi_s(x;n,\cdot)-\pi_s(y;n,\cdot)\|_{\ell^1}
 \le C2^{s-l}\bigl(\rho_s(y\mid n)+E_s(q_s)\bigr).
 \label{eq:labels-row-difference}$$ For $s=1$, the analogous derivative and difference bounds are $C$ and $C2^{1-l}$, respectively, by <a href="#eq:labels-initial-row" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-initial-row">[eq:labels-initial-row]</a>. These bounds are covered by using $c_1=1+E_1(q_1)\ge1$.

The rows have now been compared for each fixed old label. The old-label distributions themselves also differ between $x$ and $y$, and must be accounted for before estimating $R_k$ and $T_k$. Let $d_s(x,y)=\|\zeta_s(x)-\zeta_s(y)\|_{\ell^1}$ and $d_0=0$. Multiplication by a stochastic matrix $\Pi$ contracts the $\ell^1$ norm of signed row vectors: for any such vector $v$, $$\|v\Pi\|_1
 \le\sum_n |v(n)|\sum_{n'}\Pi(n,n')=\|v\|_1.$$ The marginal recursion therefore gives $$\begin{aligned}
 d_s(x,y)
 &\le d_{s-1}(x,y)
   +\|\zeta_{s-1}(y)(\pi_s(x)-\pi_s(y))\|_{\ell^1}\\
 &\le d_{s-1}(x,y)+C2^{s-l}c_s.
\end{aligned}$$ The first step uses the single-row interpretation. At later steps the last inequality follows by averaging <a href="#eq:labels-row-difference" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-row-difference">[eq:labels-row-difference]</a> against the old-label law at $y$, which averages $\rho_s(y\mid n)$ to $R_s(y)$.
