Consequently <a id="eq:labels-marginal-distance"></a>$$d_j(x,y)\le C\sum_{s=1}^j2^{s-l}c_s,
 \qquad j\le\min(l+1,m).
 \label{eq:labels-marginal-distance}$$ This recursion does not assume independence between marginal labels at the two observation points.

For $k\ge2$, first average the row switch rate and the row derivative cost at $x$ against $\zeta_{k-1}(y)$. Equations <a href="#eq:labels-row-rate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-row-rate">[eq:labels-row-rate]</a>–<a href="#eq:labels-row-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-row-derivative">[eq:labels-row-derivative]</a> bound the result by $C c_k$. Replacing this old-label distribution by $\zeta_{k-1}(x)$ costs at most $C d_{k-1}(x,y)$, because row switch rates are at most one and scaled row derivative costs are uniformly bounded. Thus $$R_k(x)+T_k(x)
 \le C c_k+C\sum_{s=1}^{k-1}2^{s-l}c_s.$$ Since $k\le l+1$, we have $2^{s-l}\le2\,2^{s-k}$. This proves <a href="#eq:labels-spatial-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-spatial-comparison">[eq:labels-spatial-comparison]</a> for $k\ge2$. For $k=1$ it follows directly from $R_1=1$, <a href="#eq:labels-uniform-costs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-uniform-costs">[eq:labels-uniform-costs]</a>, and $c_1\ge1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The packing estimate

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-labels-spatial-comparison-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-labels-spatial-comparison</summary>

<a id="eq:labels-cost-occupancy"></a>
<a id="eq:labels-double-convolution"></a>
*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:label-packing" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:label-packing">12</a>.* Denote the bracket for a level-$l$ vertex by $\mathcal C_l(q)$. All its quantities are nonnegative and measurable; the switch rates are continuous and the derivative costs have specified measurable versions. The particle occupancy identity gives the exact rewriting $$\sum_{l=1}^m\sum_{q\in V_l}a_q\mathcal C_l(q)
 =\int\sum_{l=B}^m\mathcal C_l(q_l)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\mu.
 \label{eq:labels-cost-occupancy}$$ Fix a particle, and continue to write $c_s=R_s(y)+E_s(q_s)$. The comparison in Lemma <a href="#lem:labels-spatial-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:labels-spatial-comparison">15</a> is uniform on each $D(q_l)$, so it applies to the supremum and essential supremum in $\mathcal C_l(q_l)$. For $l<m$, it bounds both $R_l$ and $R_{l+1}$. For $l=m$, the convention $R_{m+1}=1$ contributes just one additional unit. The derivative terms have the exact geometric convolution $$\sum_{k=1}^l2^{k-l}\sum_{s=1}^k2^{s-k}c_s
 =\sum_{s=1}^l(l-s+1)2^{s-l}c_s.$$ It follows that $$\sum_{l=B}^m\mathcal C_l(q_l)
 \le C\left[
  1+\sum_{l=B}^m
     \sum_{s=1}^{\min(l+1,m)}(l-s+2)2^{s-l}c_s
 \right].
 \label{eq:labels-double-convolution}$$

For a fixed $s\ge B$, the coefficient of $c_s$ on the right is bounded by $$\sum_{j=-1}^{\infty}(j+2)2^{-j}<\infty,
 \qquad j=l-s.$$ Thus the terms at or after birth cost at most $C\sum_{s=B}^m c_s$. Their integral is at most $C V$ by <a href="#eq:labels-particle-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-particle-energy">[eq:labels-particle-energy]</a> and <a href="#eq:labels-particle-switches" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-particle-switches">[eq:labels-particle-switches]</a>.

For the terms before birth, use $0\le c_s\le5$. Put $q=B-s\ge1$ and $j=l-B\ge0$. Their total for this particle is at most $$5\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}
           (j+q+2)2^{-(j+q)}
 =5\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n(n+2)2^{-n}<\infty.$$ This bound is independent of $B$ and of the particle, and therefore its integral is at most $C V$ by <a href="#eq:labels-vertex-occupancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-vertex-occupancy">[eq:labels-vertex-occupancy]</a>. The isolated unit in <a href="#eq:labels-double-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-double-convolution">[eq:labels-double-convolution]</a> has the same bound. Inserting these estimates into <a href="#eq:labels-cost-occupancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-cost-occupancy">[eq:labels-cost-occupancy]</a> proves <a href="#eq:label-packing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-packing">[eq:label-packing]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:assembly"></a>
### Signed finite-radius envelopes

The common prices control unfavorable directional components. The direction law controls the endpoints created when a direction changes. We now combine those estimates, keeping the remaining directional projections signed until integration by parts. The essential cancellation is that the derivatives of a probability row sum to zero. Subtracting the potential of the preceding history then leaves only oscillations at smaller radii.

Throughout this section, $g\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ is real, $u_t=A_tg$, $V=\int|\nabla g|$, and $r_k=2^{-k}$ for $1\le k\le m$. Use the prices and label law of Sections <a href="#sec:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:prices">3</a> and <a href="#sec:labels" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:labels">4</a>. For $1\le i\le j\le m$, put <a id="eq:run-envelope"></a>$$\label{eq:run-envelope}
 F_{ij}(x)=\max_{r_j\le t\le2r_i}u_t(x).$$ The maximum is attained, since $t\mapsto u_t(x)$ is continuous. These envelopes are compactly supported: all their radii are at most $2r_1=1$, so all their averages vanish outside the closed $1$-neighborhood of $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}g$. They are Lipschitz, with common Lipschitz bound $\|\nabla g\|_\infty$.

Outside one null set all the finitely many $F_{ij}$ are differentiable. At any such point $x$, every radius $t$ attaining $F_{ij}(x)$ satisfies <a id="eq:envelope-touching"></a>$$\label{eq:envelope-touching}
 \nabla F_{ij}(x)=\nabla u_t(x).$$ Indeed, with this radius held fixed, the differentiable function $F_{ij}-u_t$ is nonnegative everywhere and is zero at $x$. Its gradient there is zero. This proves the assertion also when there are several attaining radii. The same touching principle underlies the optimizing-radius identity of Hajłasz and Malý (Hajłasz and Malý 2010, Theorem 2 and Remark 3) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[HajlaszMaly2010](#ref-HajlaszMaly2010)]; here it applies directly to the smooth signed averages. No selected radius is differentiated.

<a id="eq:finite-band"></a>
To prove Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-band">2</a>, it suffices first to bound the normalized band by an absolute constant independent of $m$: $$\label{eq:finite-band}
 \int_{\mathbb R^2}|\nabla F_{1m}|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\le C\int_{\mathbb R^2}|\nabla g|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-labels-spatial-comparison-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-labels-spatial-comparison</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-band">2</a>.* We first bound the normalized band in <a href="#eq:finite-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-band">[eq:finite-band]</a>. For a label history $\mathbf n=(n_1,\ldots,n_m)\in\mathcal N^m$, a *run* is a maximal interval $[i,j]$ of indices on which the label is constant. Its radius interval is $[r_j,2r_i]$. Adjacent intervals meet at an endpoint, and their union is $[r_m,1]$. Thus $$F_{1m}=\max_{[i,j]\text{ a run}}F_{ij}.$$ At a point where all the envelopes are differentiable, some run attains this maximum, and its gradient agrees with $\nabla F_{1m}$ by the same touching argument.

<a id="eq:run-master"></a>
The cone inequality <a href="#eq:cone-algebra" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cone-algebra">[eq:cone-algebra]</a> states that, for every $n\in\mathcal N$ and $z\in\mathbb R^2$, $$|z|\le C\left[n\cdot z+
                 \sum_{\pm}(-e_\pm(n)\cdot z)_+\right],$$ and the bracket is nonnegative. Apply it to a run attaining $F_{1m}$, add the nonnegative brackets from the other runs, and average over the history law at $x$. This gives $$\label{eq:run-master}
 |\nabla F_{1m}|
 \le C\,\mathbb E_x\sum_{[i,j]\text{ a run}}
 \left[n_i\cdot\nabla F_{ij}
       +\sum_{\pm}(-e_\pm(n_i)\cdot\nabla F_{ij})_+\right]
 \quad\text{a.e.}$$ There are finitely many histories and runs. In particular, their expectations and all differentiations of their weights below are finite sums.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### The unfavorable components and the ends of runs.

For a run $[i,j]$, choose any attaining radius $t$ at the point under consideration, and a bin $[r_k,2r_k]$ containing it, with $i\le k\le j$. If $i<k<j$, the interval $[t-r_k/4,t+r_k/4]$ lies inside $[r_j,2r_i]$. The maximizing property therefore gives $t\in\mathcal I_k(x)$. If $k=i$ or $k=j$, the full bin is still contained in the run interval, so $t$ maximizes on that bin and belongs to $\mathcal J_k(x)$. Touching, including at tied radii, identifies the gradients.

<a id="eq:negative-sum"></a>
Write $$B_k(x)=\sup_{t\in\mathcal J_k(x)}|\nabla u_t(x)|,
 \qquad
 A_{k,n}(x)=\sup_{t\in\mathcal I_k(x)}
                  \sum_{\pm}(-e_\pm(n)\cdot\nabla u_t(x))_+.$$ A bin is an end bin of its run only if a run starts at $k$ or ends at $k$. These events have probabilities $R_k$ and $R_{k+1}$, respectively, with the sentinel conventions $R_1=R_{m+1}=1$. Also each bin belongs to exactly one run. Since each of the two negative projections is bounded by the gradient norm, we obtain $$\label{eq:negative-sum}
 \mathbb E_x\sum_{[i,j]\text{ a run}}\sum_\pm
       (-e_\pm(n_i)\cdot\nabla F_{ij})_+
 \le\sum_{k=1}^m\sum_{n\in\mathcal N}
          \mathbb P_x(n_k=n)A_{k,n}(x)
       +2\sum_{k=1}^m(R_k+R_{k+1})B_k(x).$$ No measurable choice of the attaining radius is needed: this is a pointwise bound by the measurable suprema on the right.

Here are the spatial sums in this estimate. For a level-$k$ cell $Q=Q_p$ and $q\in\mathcal R(Q)$, every $x\in Q$ satisfies $|x-q|_\infty\le21r_k$. Thus $Q\subset D(q)$, and $Q$ also lies in the $50r_k$ neighborhood used in Lemma <a href="#lem:good-label" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-label">11</a>. If $(q,n)$ is bad and $E_k(q)\le c_*$, that lemma says $\mathbb P_x(n_k=n)=0$ throughout $Q$. Every remaining bad-root charge is bounded by $a_q\le c_*^{-1}a_qE_k(q)$. Take the supremum of each label probability on $Q$ and apply the directional part of Proposition <a href="#prop:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prices">9</a>. Its price charges sum by <a href="#eq:price-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:price-budget">[eq:price-budget]</a>; its root charges sum by <a href="#eq:neighbor-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:neighbor-budget">[eq:neighbor-budget]</a>. The finite palette and the $41^2$ cells having a given vertex in their root neighborhood contribute only absolute factors.

<a id="eq:negative-integral"></a>
For the last sum in <a href="#eq:negative-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-sum">[eq:negative-sum]</a>, the endpoint estimate in Proposition <a href="#prop:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prices">9</a> gives, cell by cell, $$\int_Q(R_k+R_{k+1})B_k
 \le C\sup_Q(R_k+R_{k+1})
                \left(D_Q+\sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q)}a_q\right).$$ The price part sums because $R_k+R_{k+1}\le2$. For each root part, enlarge $Q$ to $D(q)$ and use the switch part of <a href="#eq:label-packing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-packing">[eq:label-packing]</a>, again with bounded cell multiplicity. We have proved $$\label{eq:negative-integral}
 \int_{\mathbb R^2}\mathbb E_x\sum_{[i,j]\text{ a run}}\sum_\pm
       (-e_\pm(n_i)\cdot\nabla F_{ij})_+\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\le CV.$$

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Baselines and weighted integration by parts.

<a id="eq:baseline-cost"></a>
<a id="eq:path-potential"></a>
It remains to integrate the signed projections in <a href="#eq:run-master" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:run-master">[eq:run-master]</a>. Subtract $u_{r_i}$ from the envelope of every run starting at $i$. Since $r_i$ is a forced endpoint in $\mathcal J_i(x)$, the same endpoint and packing estimates give $$\label{eq:baseline-cost}
 \int\mathbb E_x\sum_{[i,j]\text{ a run}}|\nabla u_{r_i}|
 =\sum_{i=1}^m\int R_i|\nabla u_{r_i}|
 \le\sum_{i=1}^m\int(R_i+R_{i+1})B_i\le CV.$$ For a fixed history define its vector potential by $$\label{eq:path-potential}
 \Phi_{\mathbf n}(x)=
       \sum_{[i,j]\text{ a run}}n_i\bigl(F_{ij}(x)-u_{r_i}(x)\bigr).$$ Its run partition and labels are fixed; only its scalar functions depend on $x$. It is a compactly supported Lipschitz vector field, and its divergence is the sum of the signed projections after baseline subtraction.

<a id="eq:weighted-ibp"></a>
Use the auxiliary state $n_0=*$ to write the initial row as $\pi_1(x;*,n_1)$. The probability of the history is $$w_{\mathbf n}(x)=\prod_{s=1}^m\pi_s(x;n_{s-1},n_s).$$ Every factor is Lipschitz and takes values in $[0,1]$. The weak product rule and compact support of $w_{\mathbf n}\Phi_{\mathbf n}$ therefore give $$\label{eq:weighted-ibp}
 \sum_{\mathbf n}\int w_{\mathbf n}\,
                       \operatorname{div}\Phi_{\mathbf n}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
 =-\sum_{\mathbf n}\int
                       \Phi_{\mathbf n}\cdot\nabla w_{\mathbf n}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$ Indeed the integral of the divergence of a compactly supported Lipschitz vector field is zero, as follows by testing against a smooth function equal to one on its support. We must exploit the sign of the right side before estimating its size.

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
###### Cancellation after a fixed prefix.

Differentiate the product defining $w_{\mathbf n}$ directly. For its term at step $k$, fix the prefix $\mathbf a=(n_1,\ldots,n_{k-1})$ and set $$W_{\mathbf a}=\prod_{s<k}\pi_s(x;n_{s-1},n_s),
 \qquad
 J_{k,\mathbf n}=\prod_{s>k}\pi_s(x;n_{s-1},n_s).$$ Empty products equal one. The corresponding term of $\nabla w_{\mathbf n}$ is $W_{\mathbf a}\nabla\pi_k(x;n_{k-1},n_k)J_{k,\mathbf n}$. This formula remains valid when some transition entries vanish; it involves no division by them.

<a id="eq:future-normalization"></a>
<a id="eq:row-derivative-zero"></a>
For each fixed prefix and $n_k$, summing the future product gives $$\label{eq:future-normalization}
 \sum_{n_{k+1},\ldots,n_m}J_{k,\mathbf n}=1.$$ To see this, first sum in $n_m$ using the normalization of the last row, and repeat backward to $n_{k+1}$. Also, wherever the rows are differentiable, $$\label{eq:row-derivative-zero}
 \sum_{n_k\in\mathcal N}\nabla\pi_k(x;n_{k-1},n_k)=0,$$ by differentiating their finite sum, which is the constant one.

<a id="eq:prefix-subtraction"></a>
Let $\Psi_{\mathbf a}$ be the potential of this prefix with its last run closed at $k-1$: explicitly, use the sum <a href="#eq:path-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:path-potential">[eq:path-potential]</a> for the runs of $(n_1,\ldots,n_{k-1})$. For $k=1$ set $\Psi_{\varnothing}=0$. This potential is independent of $n_k$ and of every future label. Equations <a href="#eq:future-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:future-normalization">[eq:future-normalization]</a>–<a href="#eq:row-derivative-zero" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-derivative-zero">[eq:row-derivative-zero]</a> therefore imply the exact identity $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{n_k,\ldots,n_m}
        J_{k,\mathbf n}\Phi_{\mathbf n}\cdot
                  \nabla\pi_k(x;n_{k-1},n_k)\notag\\
 &\hspace{8mm}=
   \sum_{n_k,\ldots,n_m}J_{k,\mathbf n}
        (\Phi_{\mathbf n}-\Psi_{\mathbf a})\cdot
                  \nabla\pi_k(x;n_{k-1},n_k).
 \label{eq:prefix-subtraction}
\end{aligned}$$ The subtraction precedes every absolute value in the next estimate.

<a id="eq:prefix-potential"></a>
We claim that each continuation of the prefix satisfies $$\label{eq:prefix-potential}
 |\Phi_{\mathbf n}(x)-\Psi_{\mathbf a}(x)|
       \le\sum_{l=k}^m v_l(x),
 \qquad v_l=\sup_{0<t,t'\le4r_l}|u_t-u_{t'}|.$$ All already completed runs cancel. If the last prefix run $[i,k-1]$ continues to $j\ge k$, its baseline cancels as well, leaving $n_i(F_{ij}-F_{i,k-1})$. The old radius interval contains $r_{k-1}$, and the extension adds only radii below that number. Consequently $$0\le F_{ij}-F_{i,k-1}
 \le\sup_{0<t\le r_{k-1}}(u_t-u_{r_{k-1}})_+
 \le v_k,
 \qquad r_{k-1}=2r_k.$$ Every new run $[l,j]$, beginning at $l\ge k$, contributes a vector of norm $F_{lj}-u_{r_l}\le v_l$, because its envelope includes the baseline and all its radii are at most $2r_l$. If the prefix run continues, the new runs start strictly after $k$; if there is a switch at $k$, the closed prefix itself is unchanged. The empty-prefix case consists entirely of new runs. Since their starting indices are distinct, these observations prove <a href="#eq:prefix-potential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-potential">[eq:prefix-potential]</a>. In particular, the size of an old large-radius envelope never enters the bound.

<a id="eq:flux-cost"></a>
Now take absolute values in <a href="#eq:prefix-subtraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:prefix-subtraction">[eq:prefix-subtraction]</a> and sum over prefixes. The future products are nonnegative and sum to one. The sum of the norms of the step-$k$ product-rule terms is exactly $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{\mathbf a}W_{\mathbf a}
     \sum_{n_k}|\nabla\pi_k(x;n_{k-1},n_k)|
       \sum_{n_{k+1},\ldots,n_m}J_{k,\mathbf n}\\
 &\qquad=
   \sum_{n\in\mathcal N}\zeta_{k-1}(x;n)
                  \sum_{n'}|\nabla\pi_k(x;n,n')|
   =\frac{T_k(x)}{r_k}\quad(k\ge2).
\end{aligned}$$ For $k=1$ the same identity uses the single initial row, exactly as in <a href="#eq:labels-derivative-costs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-derivative-costs">[eq:labels-derivative-costs]</a>. Combining these identities with the product expansion and <a href="#eq:weighted-ibp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:weighted-ibp">[eq:weighted-ibp]</a> yields $$\label{eq:flux-cost}
 \left|\sum_{\mathbf n}\int
                w_{\mathbf n}\operatorname{div}\Phi_{\mathbf n}\right|
 \le\sum_{1\le k\le l\le m}
                    \int\frac{T_k(x)}{r_k}v_l(x)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### Paying for the smaller-radius oscillations.

On a level-$l$ cell, the value estimate in Proposition <a href="#prop:prices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:prices">9</a> gives $$\int_Qv_l\le Cr_l
             \left(D_Q+\sum_{q\in\mathcal R(Q)}a_q\right).$$ Multiply this by $r_k^{-1}\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_QT_k$. For the price terms, $T_k\le C$ and $$\sum_{k=1}^l\frac{r_l}{r_k}
       =\sum_{k=1}^l2^{k-l}\le2.$$ Their full sum is therefore at most $C\sum_{l,Q}D_Q\le CV$. For the root terms use $Q\subset D(q)$ and reorder the finite nonnegative sums. They are at most an absolute multiple of $$\sum_{l=1}^m\sum_{q\in V_l}a_q
       \sum_{k=1}^l2^{k-l}
                   \operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{D(q)}T_k\le CV$$ by <a href="#eq:label-packing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:label-packing">[eq:label-packing]</a>. Thus <a href="#eq:flux-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:flux-cost">[eq:flux-cost]</a> is at most $CV$. Together with <a href="#eq:baseline-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:baseline-cost">[eq:baseline-cost]</a>, this bounds the integral of the signed projections in <a href="#eq:run-master" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:run-master">[eq:run-master]</a> from above by $CV$. Equation <a href="#eq:negative-integral" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:negative-integral">[eq:negative-integral]</a> pays for the remaining terms and proves <a href="#eq:finite-band" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-band">[eq:finite-band]</a>.

For $b/a=2^m$ with $m\ge1$, apply the normalized result to $g_b(x)=g(bx)$. Its normalized envelope is $S_{a,b}g(bx)$, and both gradient integrals acquire the same factor $b^{-1}$ under the change of variables. This proves the stated bound for all bands in the proposition. Finally, when $a=b$, convolution gives $\nabla A_ag=A_a(\nabla g)$, and Tonelli’s theorem yields $\int|\nabla A_ag|\le\int|\nabla g|$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:extension"></a>
### Sobolev input and absolute continuity

The signed estimate now gives the endpoint theorem in two steps. First, approximation passes it to Sobolev inputs and yields a finite distributional derivative measure for the maximal function. Second, local mean subtraction shows that this measure gives no mass to a Lebesgue-null set. The second step is needed to obtain a weak gradient in $L^1$, and it is the reason the finite-band estimate was proved for signed inputs.

For real $g\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ and $0<a\le b<\infty$, continue to write $$S_{a,b}g(x)=\sup_{a\le t\le b}A_tg(x).$$ The disk integrals define these functions independently of the representative of $g$. The positive lower radius makes the supremum finite at every $x$, since $|A_tg(x)|\le\|g\|_1/(\pi a^2)$. The elementary analytic facts used below, including the exact Euclidean variation formula, are proved in Appendix <a href="#app:analytic-tools" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:analytic-tools">7</a>.

<a id="stmt-lem-sobolev-band"></a>
<div id="lem:sobolev-band" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:band-lipschitz"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Sobolev finite bands). *For every real $g\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ and $0<a\le b<\infty$, $S_{a,b}g$ is globally Lipschitz and $$\label{eq:band-lipschitz}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(S_{a,b}g)\le\frac{\|\nabla g\|_1}{\pi a^2}.$$ If $b/a$ is a nonnegative integer power of two, then <a id="eq:sobolev-band"></a>$$\label{eq:sobolev-band}
 \int_{\mathbb R^2}|\nabla S_{a,b}g|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\le C\|\nabla g\|_1,$$ where $C$ is absolute and independent of the band. This includes $a=b$, when the envelope is one average.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sobolev-band"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sobolev-band</summary>

<a id="eq:band-uniform-approximation"></a>
*Proof.* The translation estimate of Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus">19</a> gives, for $t\ge a$ and $z\in\mathbb R^2$, $$\begin{aligned}
 |A_tg(x+z)-A_tg(x)|
 &\le\frac1{\pi t^2}\int_{B(x,t)}|g(y+z)-g(y)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\\
 &\le\frac{|z|}{\pi a^2}\|\nabla g\|_1.
\end{aligned}$$ Taking suprema over the same radius interval on both sides proves <a href="#eq:band-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:band-lipschitz">[eq:band-lipschitz]</a>. The same comparison gives, for any two integrable real inputs, $$\label{eq:band-uniform-approximation}
 \|S_{a,b}g-S_{a,b}\widetilde g\|_\infty
       \le\frac{\|g-\widetilde g\|_1}{\pi a^2}.$$ Here the inequality $|\sup f_t-\sup h_t|\le\sup|f_t-h_t|$ is applied to bounded families of real numbers.

Choose real $g_j\in C_c^\infty$ converging to $g$ in $W^{1,1}$. At the fixed lower radius $a$, <a href="#eq:band-uniform-approximation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:band-uniform-approximation">[eq:band-uniform-approximation]</a> gives uniform convergence of the band envelopes. If $b/a$ is a power of two greater than one, Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-band">2</a> therefore passes to the limit by the local variation inequality in Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-vector-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-vector-measure">22</a>: $$\int|\nabla S_{a,b}g|
 \le\liminf_j\int|\nabla S_{a,b}g_j|
 \le C\lim_j\|\nabla g_j\|_1=C\|\nabla g\|_1.$$ The limit is Lipschitz by <a href="#eq:band-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:band-lipschitz">[eq:band-lipschitz]</a>, so its weak and almost-everywhere classical gradients agree. When $a=b$, testing the convolution against smooth functions gives $\nabla A_ag=A_a(\nabla g)$ distributionally; the integral of its norm is at most $\|\nabla g\|_1$ by Tonelli’s theorem. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-sobolev-band-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-sobolev-band</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* At this point take $g=|f|$. Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus">19</a> shows that $g\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ and $$V_g:=\|\nabla g\|_1\le\|\nabla f\|_1.$$ The planar Sobolev inequality in Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-planar-embedding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-planar-embedding">20</a> gives $g\in L^2(\mathbb R^2)$. The strong $L^2$ maximal inequality (Kinnunen 1997, (1.2)) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Kinnunen1997](#ref-Kinnunen1997)] then yields $Mf=Mg\in L^2(\mathbb R^2)$, hence local integrability. Define $$G_N=S_{2^{-N},2^N}g,\qquad N=1,2,\ldots.$$ Because $g\ge0$, these nonnegative functions increase to $Mg$ and are bounded above by it. Dominated convergence on every bounded set implies $G_N\to Mg$ in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### A finite derivative measure.

<a id="eq:variation-limit"></a>
<a id="eq:local-lsc"></a>
Lemma <a href="#lem:sobolev-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sobolev-band">16</a> bounds $\int|\nabla G_N|$ by $CV_g$ uniformly in $N$. In particular, for every $\varphi\in C_c^1(\mathbb R^2;\mathbb R^2)$, $$\left|-\int Mg\,\operatorname{div}\varphi\right|
 =\lim_N\left|\int\nabla G_N\cdot\varphi\right|
 \le CV_g\|\varphi\|_\infty.$$ The vector-measure statement of Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-vector-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-vector-measure">22</a> supplies a finite vector Radon measure $H=D(Mg)$, with $$\label{eq:variation-limit}
 |H|(\mathbb R^2)\le CV_g.$$ It also gives the local estimate $$\label{eq:local-lsc}
 |H|(O)\le\liminf_{N\to\infty}\int_O|\nabla G_N|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
 \qquad(O\subset\mathbb R^2\text{ open}).$$ These conclusions control total variation. We next show that $H$ is absolutely continuous.

Lower-radius Lipschitz truncations appear in Hajłasz and Malý (Hajłasz and Malý 2010, Lemma 8) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[HajlaszMaly2010](#ref-HajlaszMaly2010)] and Kurka (Kurka 2015, Lemma 7.1) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Kurka2015](#ref-Kurka2015)]; local/nonlocal splitting with subtraction of the input mean appears in Saari (Saari 2019, sec. 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[Saari2019](#ref-Saari2019)]. Here the signed band estimate and Poincaré’s inequality make the localization cost depend only on the nearby input gradient. We give that argument in full.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### Small and large radii near a null set.

<a id="eq:large-radius-null-cost"></a>
Fix a compact Lebesgue-null set $K$. The assertion is immediate if $K$ is empty, so assume it is nonempty. Fix also a dyadic $t=2^{-L}$ with $L\ge1$, and an open set $$K\subset O\subset\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<t\}.$$ For every $N\ge L$, splitting the band at $t$ gives $$G_N=\max\{S_{2^{-N},t}g,\ S_{t,2^N}g\}.$$ Both functions are Lipschitz. The maximum rule, including the equality set, in Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus">19</a> gives $$|\nabla G_N|
 \le |\nabla S_{2^{-N},t}g|+|\nabla S_{t,2^N}g|
 \quad\text{a.e.}$$ For the second term, <a href="#eq:band-lipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:band-lipschitz">[eq:band-lipschitz]</a> bounds its integral over $O$ by $$\label{eq:large-radius-null-cost}
 \int_O|\nabla S_{t,2^N}g|
       \le \frac{|O|}{\pi t^2}V_g.$$

For the first term, partition the plane into half-open grid squares $Q$ of side $t$, and retain those meeting $O$. There are finitely many, since $O$ is contained in a bounded neighborhood of $K$. Choose the cutoff of Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-square" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-square">21</a>, equal to one on $3Q$ and supported in $5Q$, and put $$c_Q=\frac1{|5Q|}\int_{5Q}g,\qquad
 g_Q=\chi_Q(g-c_Q).$$ The product rule and Poincaré’s inequality give <a id="eq:localized-input"></a>$$\label{eq:localized-input}
 \|\nabla g_Q\|_1\le C\int_{5Q}|\nabla g|.$$ Every disk of radius $s\le t$ centered in $Q$ lies in $3Q$. Consequently $$A_sg_Q(x)=A_sg(x)-c_Q\quad(x\in Q, 0<s\le t),
 \qquad
 S_{2^{-N},t}g_Q=S_{2^{-N},t}g-c_Q\quad\text{on }Q.$$ The two envelopes have the same gradient almost everywhere on $Q$. Since $g_Q$ can have either sign, we use precisely the signed form of Lemma <a href="#lem:sobolev-band" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sobolev-band">16</a>. The ratio of its endpoints is $2^{N-L}$, including one when $N=L$. It follows that $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_O|\nabla S_{2^{-N},t}g|
 &\le\sum_{Q\cap O\ne\varnothing}
                \int_Q|\nabla S_{2^{-N},t}g_Q|\\
 &\le C\sum_{Q\cap O\ne\varnothing}\|\nabla g_Q\|_1
 \le C\int_{\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<6t\}}|\nabla g(x)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.
\end{aligned}$$ The last step uses the bounded overlap of the squares $5Q$ and their containment in the $6t$-neighborhood of $K$, both established in Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-square" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-square">21</a>. Grid boundaries are null and do not affect these gradient integrals.

<a id="eq:null-localization"></a>
Combining this estimate with <a href="#eq:large-radius-null-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-radius-null-cost">[eq:large-radius-null-cost]</a> and then <a href="#eq:local-lsc" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-lsc">[eq:local-lsc]</a> yields $$\label{eq:null-localization}
 |H|(O)\le C\int_{\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<6t\}}|\nabla g(x)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x
                  +C t^{-2}|O|V_g.$$ The constants are independent of $N$, $O$, and $t$.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
###### Removing the singular part.

For the fixed $t$, compactness and Lebesgue nullity of $K$ allow the open neighborhood $O$ above to have arbitrarily small area. Since $|H|(K)\le|H|(O)$, <a href="#eq:null-localization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:null-localization">[eq:null-localization]</a> gives $$|H|(K)\le C\int_{\{\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<6t\}}|\nabla g(x)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$ Only now let $t$ decrease to zero through dyadic values. The neighborhoods decrease to the compact null set $K$, and $|\nabla g|$ is integrable; the right side tends to zero. Thus $|H|(K)=0$ for every compact Lebesgue-null set. Inner regularity of the finite Radon measure $|H|$ implies that it vanishes on every Borel null set, and hence on every Lebesgue-null set by containment in a Borel null set.

The Radon–Nikodym conclusion of Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-vector-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-vector-measure">22</a> now gives $H=G\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x$ with $G\in L^1(\mathbb R^2;\mathbb R^2)$ and $$\int|G|=|H|(\mathbb R^2)\le CV_g\le C\int|\nabla f|.$$ For every smooth compactly supported vector field $\varphi$, the defining identity of $H$ reads $-\int Mf\,\operatorname{div}\varphi=\int G\cdot\varphi$. Thus $G$ is the weak gradient of $Mf$. Since $Mf$ is locally integrable, this proves $Mf\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and <a href="#eq:main" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main">[eq:main]</a>, with a globally integrable Euclidean gradient. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 17*. The direct absolute-continuity argument uses the signed band estimate, approximation, and square Poincaré localization. After <a href="#eq:variation-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:variation-limit">[eq:variation-limit]</a>, one could alternatively apply Lahti and Weigt’s centered regularity theorem (Lahti and Weigt 2025, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[LahtiWeigt2025](#ref-LahtiWeigt2025)] to $g=|f|$ on $\mathbb R^2$. The input is globally integrable and has finite variation, and $Mg\in BV_{\mathrm{loc}}$ has already been proved. Its input jump and Cantor derivative measures vanish, so the theorem’s additional condition on the Cantor part is vacuous; its conclusion gives local Sobolev regularity. This optional route is used only after the variation bound and supplies no part of the finite-band estimate.

</div>

<a id="stmt-cor-bv-input"></a>
<div id="cor:bv-input" class="corollary">

**Corollary 18** (Bounded-variation inputs). *Let $BV(\mathbb R^2)$ denote the globally integrable functions whose distributional derivatives have finite total variation. For every real $f\in BV(\mathbb R^2)$, $$Mf\in L^2(\mathbb R^2)\cap BV_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2),
 \qquad |D(Mf)|(\mathbb R^2)\le C|Df|(\mathbb R^2),$$ with the constant from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Writing $P(F)=|D\mathbf 1_F|(\mathbb R^2)$ for perimeter, every measurable set $E$ with $|E|<\infty$ and $P(E)<\infty$ satisfies $$\int_0^1 P(\{M\mathbf 1_E>t\})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\le CP(E).$$ These superlevel sets have finite measure for every $t>0$ and finite perimeter for almost every $t>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-bv-input"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-bv-input</summary>

*Proof.* Let $f_\varepsilon=f*\rho_\varepsilon$ for a smooth nonnegative compactly supported probability approximate identity. Then $f_\varepsilon\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, $f_\varepsilon\to f$ in $L^1$, and $$\nabla f_\varepsilon=(Df)*\rho_\varepsilon,\qquad
 \|\nabla f_\varepsilon\|_1\le |Df|(\mathbb R^2).$$ The variation formula and lower semicontinuity show that these gradient norms tend to $|Df|(\mathbb R^2)$, so the approximation is strict in $BV$. Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-planar-embedding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-planar-embedding">20</a> bounds $\|f_\varepsilon\|_2$ uniformly; an almost-everywhere convergent subsequence and Fatou’s lemma give $f\in L^2$. The convolution approximate identity therefore also converges to $f$ in $L^2$. The pointwise inequality $|Mf_\varepsilon-Mf|\le M(f_\varepsilon-f)$ and the strong $L^2$ maximal inequality yield $Mf\in L^2$ and $Mf_\varepsilon\to Mf$ in $L^2$, hence locally in $L^1$. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> bounds the global weak-gradient norm of $Mf_\varepsilon$ by $C|Df|(\mathbb R^2)$. Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-vector-measure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-vector-measure">22</a> now gives the asserted finite derivative measure and local $BV$ regularity.

For $u=M\mathbf 1_E$ we have $0\le u\le1$. Apply the $BV$ coarea formula (Evans and Gariepy 2015) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[EvansGariepy2015](#ref-EvansGariepy2015)] on $B(0,R)$ and then use monotone exhaustion as $R\to\infty$ to obtain $$\int_0^1 P(\{u>t\})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t
 =|Du|(\mathbb R^2)\le CP(E).$$ Also $|\{u>t\}|\le t^{-2}\|u\|_2^2<\infty$ for every $t>0$. ◻

</details>

This conclusion does not assert global integrability of $Mf$ or absolute continuity of its derivative measure for arbitrary $BV$ input.

<a id="app:analytic-tools"></a>
### Analytic facts used in the limiting argument

We record the approximation, localization, and measure arguments used in Section <a href="#sec:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:extension">6</a>. All gradient norms are Euclidean. The Sobolev space is inhomogeneous: the function and its distributional first derivatives belong to $L^1(\mathbb R^2)$. General Sobolev calculus is treated in (Evans and Gariepy 2015) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[EvansGariepy2015](#ref-EvansGariepy2015)]; the exact chain, product, and Lipschitz facts used here also appear in (Hunter 2014, chap. 3, Propositions 3.21–3.22 and Theorems 3.38–3.39) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Hunter2014](#ref-Hunter2014)]. We give the needed calculations, including the variation formula that preserves the Euclidean norm.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Approximation, translations, and equality sets

<a id="stmt-lem-appendix-sobolev-calculus"></a>
<div id="lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:appendix-translation"></a>
<a id="eq:appendix-maximum"></a>
**Lemma 19**. *For real $u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ there are real $u_j\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^2)$ converging to $u$ in $W^{1,1}$, and $$\label{eq:appendix-translation}
 \|u(\cdot+z)-u\|_1\le |z|\,\|\nabla u\|_1
 \qquad(z\in\mathbb R^2).$$ Moreover $|u|\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$ with $|\nabla|u||\le|\nabla u|$ almost everywhere. If $\chi$ is smooth and compactly supported, then $\chi u\in W^{1,1}$ and $\nabla(\chi u)=\chi\nabla u+u\nabla\chi$. For locally Lipschitz real functions $v,w$, their maximum is locally Lipschitz and satisfies, almost everywhere, $$\label{eq:appendix-maximum}
 \nabla\max(v,w)=
 \begin{cases}
  \nabla v,&v>w,\\
  \nabla w,&w>v,\\
  \nabla v=\nabla w,&v=w.
 \end{cases}$$ Products of locally Lipschitz functions satisfy the weak product rule. Their weak derivatives agree almost everywhere with their classical derivatives.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-appendix-sobolev-calculus"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-appendix-sobolev-calculus</summary>

*Proof.* The smooth-cutoff product rule follows directly by testing the distributional derivative of $u$ against $\chi\varphi$. Choose $\chi_R$ equal to one on $B(0,R)$, zero outside $B(0,2R)$, with $0\le\chi_R\le1$ and $|\nabla\chi_R|\le C/R$. Then $$\|\chi_Ru-u\|_1\longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \|\nabla(\chi_Ru)-\nabla u\|_1
 \le\int_{|x|>R}|\nabla u|+\frac C R\|u\|_1
 \longrightarrow0.$$ Convolving $\chi_Ru$ with a smooth compactly supported approximate identity commutes with its distributional derivatives. The convolutions converge in $L^1$, for both the function and its gradient. This last fact follows from continuity of translations in $L^1$ and the approximate-identity formula; translation continuity follows first for continuous compactly supported functions by uniform continuity, and then for $L^1$ functions by approximation. A diagonal choice of $R$ and convolution radius proves the asserted $C_c^\infty$ density.

For each smooth approximant, the fundamental theorem of calculus along a segment gives $$|u_j(x+z)-u_j(x)|
 \le |z|\int_0^1|\nabla u_j(x+sz)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.$$ Integrating in $x$ and using translation invariance proves the translation bound for $u_j$. Convergence in $W^{1,1}$ proves <a href="#eq:appendix-translation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:appendix-translation">[eq:appendix-translation]</a> for $u$.

Here is also a direct smooth-chain argument for the absolute value. For a continuously differentiable function $F$ with bounded derivative and $F(0)=0$, smooth approximation gives $$\nabla F(u)=F'(u)\nabla u.$$ Indeed $F(u_j)\to F(u)$ in $L^1$. Along a subsequence $u_j\to u$ almost everywhere, and $$F'(u_j)\nabla u_j-F'(u)\nabla u
 =F'(u_j)(\nabla u_j-\nabla u)
       +(F'(u_j)-F'(u))\nabla u$$ tends to zero in $L^1$, by boundedness and dominated convergence. Passing the smooth identity to distributions proves the formula. Apply it to $F_\varepsilon(s)=\sqrt{s^2+\varepsilon^2}-\varepsilon$. These functions satisfy $0\le F_\varepsilon(u)\le|u|$ and converge to $|u|$ in $L^1$. Their derivatives converge pointwise to $\operatorname{sgn}(u)$, with value zero at $u=0$, and are bounded by one. Passing to the limit gives $\nabla|u|=\operatorname{sgn}(u)\nabla u$ and the claimed norm inequality.

A locally Lipschitz function restricts to a Lipschitz, hence absolutely continuous, function on each coordinate line in a rectangle. One-dimensional integration by parts and Fubini’s theorem give its weak partial derivatives. Rademacher’s theorem identifies these with its classical gradient almost everywhere (Hunter 2014, chap. 3, Theorems 3.38–3.39) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Hunter2014](#ref-Hunter2014)]. The usual product rule at joint differentiability points, or equivalently the one-dimensional rule on those lines, proves the weak Lipschitz product rule.

For the equality set in <a href="#eq:appendix-maximum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:appendix-maximum">[eq:appendix-maximum]</a>, put $a=v-w$. At almost every point of $\{a=0\}$ the set has density one and $a$ is differentiable. Its derivative there is zero: a nonzero derivative would give a cone of fixed positive relative area on which $a$ is nonzero in every sufficiently small ball, contrary to density one. Thus $\nabla v=\nabla w$ almost everywhere on their equality set. At such a point, $a(x+h)=o(|h|)$ implies $a_+(x+h)=o(|h|)$, so $a_+$ also has derivative zero. On the open sets $a>0$ and $a<0$, its gradient is respectively $\nabla a$ and zero. Since $\max(v,w)=w+a_+$, this proves <a href="#eq:appendix-maximum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:appendix-maximum">[eq:appendix-maximum]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-appendix-planar-embedding"></a>
<div id="lem:appendix-planar-embedding" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Planar Sobolev embedding). *For every real $u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, $$\|u\|_2^2\le\|\partial_1u\|_1\|\partial_2u\|_1,
 \qquad\text{in particular }\|u\|_2\le\|\nabla u\|_1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-appendix-planar-embedding"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-appendix-planar-embedding</summary>

*Proof.* For $u\in C_c^\infty$, set $$a(y)=\int_\mathbb R|\partial_1u(x,y)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x,
 \qquad b(x)=\int_\mathbb R|\partial_2u(x,y)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y.$$ The fundamental theorem of calculus on the two coordinate lines gives $|u(x,y)|\le a(y)$ and $|u(x,y)|\le b(x)$. Thus $|u(x,y)|^2\le a(y)b(x)$; integrating proves the first inequality. For general $u$, approximate as in Lemma <a href="#lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:appendix-sobolev-calculus">19</a>. Applying the smooth inequality to differences shows that the approximants are Cauchy in $L^2$. Their $L^2$ limit agrees almost everywhere with their $L^1$ limit $u$, by passage to a common almost-everywhere convergent subsequence. Pass to the limit in the inequality. Each coordinate derivative has $L^1$ norm at most $\|\nabla u\|_1$, giving the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
#### Square localization

<a id="stmt-lem-appendix-square"></a>
<div id="lem:appendix-square" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:appendix-poincare"></a>
**Lemma 21**. *For a square $Q$ of side length $t>0$ and $u\in W^{1,1}(\mathbb R^2)$, write $u_Q=|Q|^{-1}\int_Qu$. Then $$\label{eq:appendix-poincare}
 \int_Q|u-u_Q|\le Ct\int_Q|\nabla u|.$$ For every grid square $Q$ of side $t$, there is a smooth cutoff $\chi_Q$ equal to one on the closed concentric dilation $3Q$, supported in the interior of $5Q$, with $0\le\chi_Q\le1$ and $|\nabla\chi_Q|\le C/t$. With $c_Q=u_{5Q}$, it satisfies <a id="eq:appendix-localization"></a>$$\label{eq:appendix-localization}
 \|\nabla[\chi_Q(u-c_Q)]\|_1
       \le C\int_{5Q}|\nabla u|.$$ Every disk of radius at most $t$ centered in $Q$ lies in $3Q$. The dilations $5Q$ of grid squares have bounded overlap. If the squares under consideration meet $O\subset\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<t\}$, all their dilations $5Q$ lie in $\{x:\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(x,K)<6t\}$. Constants are independent of $Q,t,u,O,K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-appendix-square"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-appendix-square</summary>

*Proof.* By rotation and translation it suffices to take $Q=(0,t)^2$. First suppose $u$ is smooth. Jensen’s inequality gives $$\int_Q|u-u_Q|
 \le t^{-2}\int_Q\int_Q|u(x)-u(y)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}y\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.$$ Join $x=(x_1,x_2)$ to $(y_1,x_2)$ and then to $y=(y_1,y_2)$. For the first segment, with $x_2$ fixed, integrating its derivative bound in $x_1,y_1$ gives at most $$t^2\int_0^t|\partial_1u(s,x_2)|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.$$ Indeed each $s$ lies between $x_1$ and $y_1$ for a set of pairs of area at most $t^2$. Integration in $x_2$ and $y_2$ therefore gives $t^3\int_Q|\partial_1u|$. The second segment similarly costs at most $t^3\int_Q|\partial_2u|$. After division by $t^2$, the bound $|\partial_1u|+|\partial_2u|\le\sqrt2|\nabla u|$ proves <a href="#eq:appendix-poincare" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:appendix-poincare">[eq:appendix-poincare]</a>. Global smooth approximation proves it for the stated Sobolev input, since both the functions and their means converge on $Q$.

Rescale a fixed smooth cutoff to obtain $\chi_Q$. The product rule and <a href="#eq:appendix-poincare" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:appendix-poincare">[eq:appendix-poincare]</a>, now on $5Q$, yield $$\|\nabla[\chi_Q(u-c_Q)]\|_1
 \le\int_{5Q}|\nabla u|+\frac C t\int_{5Q}|u-c_Q|
 \le C\int_{5Q}|\nabla u|.$$ In each coordinate the disk containment follows from $t/2+t=3t/2$. A point lies in only a fixed number of the dilated squares, since their centers form a grid of spacing $t$. Finally, if $x\in Q\cap O$ and $y\in5Q$, then $|y-x|\le3\sqrt2t$. Hence $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(y,K)<(1+3\sqrt2)t<6t$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
#### Vector variation and local lower semicontinuity

<a id="eq:appendix-variation"></a>
For $u\in L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$ and open $O\subset\mathbb R^2$, define its distributional variation on $O$ by $$\label{eq:appendix-variation}
 \mathcal V(u;O)=
 \sup\left\{-\int u\,\operatorname{div}\varphi:
       \varphi\in C_c^1(O;\mathbb R^2),\ |\varphi(x)|\le1\right\}.$$ The test-field norm is Euclidean. The class is closed under negation, so the same supremum results if the tested integral is replaced by its absolute value.

<a id="stmt-lem-appendix-vector-measure"></a>
<div id="lem:appendix-vector-measure" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:appendix-local-lsc"></a>
**Lemma 22**. *Suppose $u_j\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^2)$ converge locally in $L^1$ to $u$, and $\sup_j\int_{\mathbb R^2}|\nabla u_j|\le M<\infty$. Then the distributional gradient of $u$ is a finite vector Radon measure $H$, with $$\label{eq:appendix-local-lsc}
 |H|(\mathbb R^2)\le M,\qquad
 |H|(O)=\mathcal V(u;O)
       \le\liminf_j\int_O|\nabla u_j|
 \quad\text{for every open }O.$$ If $|H|(K)=0$ for every compact Lebesgue-null set $K$, then $H=G\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x$ for some $G\in L^1(\mathbb R^2;\mathbb R^2)$ and $\|G\|_1=|H|(\mathbb R^2)$. In that case $u\in W^{1,1}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, with weak gradient $G$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-appendix-vector-measure"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-appendix-vector-measure</summary>

*Proof.* For every smooth compactly supported vector field $\varphi$, local $L^1$ convergence implies $$\left|-\int u\,\operatorname{div}\varphi\right|
 =\lim_j\left|\int\nabla u_j\cdot\varphi\right|
 \le M\|\varphi\|_\infty.$$ Smooth compactly supported fields are uniformly dense in $C_0(\mathbb R^2;\mathbb R^2)$, the continuous fields vanishing at infinity: first cut off outside a large compact set and then convolve. Thus the functional extends uniquely to this space with norm at most $M$. Apply the scalar Riesz representation theorem to its two coordinates to obtain finite signed Radon measures $H_1,H_2$. Their vector $H=(H_1,H_2)$ represents the functional and therefore the distributional gradient of $u$. The Riesz and Radon–Nikodym theorems used here are recorded in (Simon 2018, chap. 1, Theorem 5.14 and Equation (4.17)) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)].

We spell out why the representation retains the Euclidean total variation rather than the sum of coordinate variations. Put $\lambda=|H_1|+|H_2|$ and write $H=q\lambda$ using the scalar Radon–Nikodym theorem. The coordinate densities of $q$ have absolute value at most one. For a Borel set $E$, the definition of vector total variation by finite Borel partitions gives $$|H|(E)=\int_E|q|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda.$$ The upper bound follows from the triangle inequality on each part. For the reverse bound, partition the bounded range of $q$ into finitely many sets of diameter at most $\varepsilon$, and choose a vector $q_s$ in each. On the corresponding partition $E_s$ of $E$, $$\sum_s|H(E_s)|
 \ge\sum_s|q_s|\lambda(E_s)-\varepsilon\lambda(E)
 \ge\int_E|q|\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\lambda-2\varepsilon\lambda(E).$$ Let $\varepsilon\downarrow0$. Set $\mu=|H|$ and $\sigma=q/|q|$ where $q\ne0$, with value zero elsewhere; then $H=\sigma\mu$ and $|\sigma|=1$ almost everywhere for $\mu$.

<a id="eq:appendix-continuous-variation"></a>
For every open $O$, this representation gives $$\label{eq:appendix-continuous-variation}
 \mu(O)=\sup\left\{\int\varphi\cdot\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}H:
          \varphi\in C_c(O;\mathbb R^2),\ |\varphi|\le1\right\}.$$ One inequality is immediate. For the other, approximate $\sigma$ on $O$ in $L^1(\mu)$ by simple vector functions with values in the closed unit ball. Inner regularity permits replacement of their finitely many disjoint measurable sets by disjoint compact subsets of $O$, losing arbitrarily little $\mu$-mass. Choose continuous cutoff functions equal to one on these compact sets, with mutually disjoint compact supports in $O$, and multiply by the corresponding vector values. Their sum has norm at most one and approximates $\sigma$ in $L^1(\mu)$, with arbitrarily small error. This proves the reverse inequality. These are the usual polar and open-set variation formulas; see also (Simon 2018, chap. 2, Equations (2.1)–(2.4)) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)].

Each continuous compactly supported test field in <a href="#eq:appendix-continuous-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:appendix-continuous-variation">[eq:appendix-continuous-variation]</a> can be extended by zero and convolved with a nonnegative smooth probability kernel of sufficiently small support. The resulting smooth fields remain supported in $O$, have norm at most one, and converge uniformly. Consequently the same supremum is obtained with $C_c^1$ test fields. The distributional identity identifies it with $\mathcal V(u;O)$ in <a href="#eq:appendix-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:appendix-variation">[eq:appendix-variation]</a>. Taking $O=\mathbb R^2$ and using the functional norm bound gives $|H|(\mathbb R^2)\le M$.

For each fixed admissible field supported in $O$, $$-\int u\,\operatorname{div}\varphi
 =\lim_j\int\nabla u_j\cdot\varphi
 \le\liminf_j\int_O|\nabla u_j|.$$ Taking the supremum proves the local lower semicontinuity in <a href="#eq:appendix-local-lsc" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:appendix-local-lsc">[eq:appendix-local-lsc]</a>. This argument requires only local integrability of $u$.

Finally, if $\mu(K)=0$ for all compact Lebesgue-null $K$, inner regularity implies $\mu(E)=0$ for every Borel null set $E$. Every Lebesgue-null set is contained in a Borel null set, so $\mu$ is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. Write $\mu=h\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x$ by the positive Radon–Nikodym theorem, where $h\ge0$ and $\int h=\mu(\mathbb R^2)$. Set $G=h\sigma$, with value zero where $h=0$. Then $H=G\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x$ and $|G|=h$ almost everywhere. Thus $\|G\|_1=|H|(\mathbb R^2)$, and the defining distributional identity for $H$ is precisely the weak-gradient identity for $G$. The needed inner regularity is, for example, (Simon 2018, chap. 1, Lemma 5.7) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)]. ◻

</details>

In Section <a href="#sec:extension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:extension">6</a>, this lemma first yields a finite derivative measure from the expanding bands. The signed cutoff argument then proves that it vanishes on compact null sets; the lemma’s final assertion identifies the resulting weak gradient.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The tangent-disk sweep. All boundary circles pass through $z$. A shell point $y$ with $|y−z| ≥ ρ$ enters between the parameters $l − v$ and $l + v$, with $v$ proportional to $δ/ρ^{2}$. Mass outside $B(z,ρ)$ already present at $l − v$ lies in the inner disk $B(x,l−2δ)$. When $z ∈ D(x,r,δ)$, this disk is contained in the controlled disk $B(x,r−δ)$. The drawing is schematic.",
      "figure_id": "fig:tangent-disks",
      "pdf_page": 10,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:tangent-disks",
          "exact_quote": "The tangent-disk sweep. All boundary circles pass through \\(z\\).\nA shell point \\(y\\) with \\(|y-z|\\ge\\rho\\) enters between the parameters\n\\(l-v\\) and \\(l+v\\), with \\(v\\) proportional to\n\\(\\delta/\\rho^2\\). Mass outside \\(B(z,\\rho)\\) already p",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[scale=1.05,>=Stealth]\n \\draw[gray!60,thin] (1.55,0) circle[radius=1.55];\n \\draw[black,thick] (2,0) circle[radius=2];\n \\draw[gray!75,dashed] (2.45,0) circle[radius=2.45];\n \\draw[->,gray] (-.35,0)--(5.35,0) node[right] {\\(\\theta\\)};\n \\fill (0,0) circle (1.8pt) node[below left] {\\(z\\)};\n \\fill (1.55,0) circle (1.3pt) node[below=5pt] {\\(z+(l-v)\\theta\\)};\n \\fill (2,0) circle (1.6pt) node[above=4pt] {\\(x=z+l\\theta\\)};\n \\fill (2.45,0) circle (1.3pt);\n \\draw[gray] (2.45,-.07)--(5.3,-.85)\n   node[right,black] {\\(z+(l+v)\\theta\\)};\n \\fill (2,2) circle (2pt) node[above right] {\\(y\\)};\n \\draw[dotted] (0,0)--(2,2);\n \\node[align=left,anchor=west] at (5.3,1.65)\n   {\\(\\displaystyle B(z+L\\theta,L)\\)\\\\[2pt]increases with \\(L\\)};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "A schematic geometric diagram illustrating a tangent-disk sweep. Two circular disks passing through a common boundary point $z$ on the left are shown, with a horizontal ray extending to the right with an arrow labeled $\\theta$. Along this ray, the points $z$, $z + (l - v)\\theta$, $x = z + l\\theta$ (marked with a solid point), and $z + (l + v)\\theta$ are indicated. In the shell region between the boundaries of the inner disk and the outer disk, a point $y$ is plotted. An arrow pointing outward on the right is accompanied by the text \"$B(z + L\\theta, L)$ increases with $L$\"."
    }
  ],
